cross SlotFill: Repairing Lossy SoC Communication Traces for Post-Silicon Validation

Authors: Tasnim Tabassum, Hao Zheng

Abstract: Post-silicon validation relies on traces of messages exchanged by SoC components, but trace buffers and monitors drop messages, often leaving no mark of what was lost. We present SlotFill, which puts each deleted message back in the right gap, with the right type and order. SlotFill gives every gap between the remaining messages a fixed number of slots, and a Transformer-based model fills each slot with a message or leaves it empty when nothing was deleted there, so the input never reveals how much was lost. The model also uses monitor fields (timestamps, transaction IDs, addresses) from surviving messages. On whole-program gem5 traces of 4- and 8-core systems (2.07 billion messages), SlotFill restores up to 97% of deleted messages exactly at 15% loss and up to 87% at 50% loss, and 76-83% at 15% loss when timestamps are coarse.

cross Conservative Overlap Fuzzy Rough Sets for Incomplete Data

Authors: Trung Hieu Vuong, Van Thien Nguyen, Nhu Son Nguyen, Thanh Dai Tran

Abstract: Attribute reduction in incomplete information systems is difficult because missing values break the equivalence-class structure of rough set theory. We propose the Conservative Overlap Fuzzy Rough Set (COFRS) model. Each object is described by its set of present attributes. The similarity of two objects is a weighted overlap on the attributes present in both, divided by the fixed number of attributes |A| instead of the size of their union, so missing values are penalised globally and are not matched as wildcards. From this similarity we define a soft dependency degree based on weighted granule consistency, and we search for reducts by greedy backward elimination in O(|U|^2 |A|^2) time. We show that the relation is symmetric and non-reflexive, that the dependency degree is not monotone, and we give a sufficient condition under which monotonicity holds. On 11 UCI benchmarks with missing rates from 0.15% to 20.46%, COFRS gives the smallest reducts on average (4.1 attributes, against 5.0-11.2 for Tolerance-RS, a range-threshold variant Tol-RS_tau, Zhou-RS and the min-aggregation variant COFRS-min). The difference is significant against Tol-RS_tau, Zhou-RS and COFRS-min (Holm-adjusted Wilcoxon p<0.01) but not against Tolerance-RS (p=0.56), and it is small against the Jensen-Shen selector (adjusted p=0.084). At equal reduct size, COFRS selects more informative attributes than random subsets. With a kNN classifier the methods do not differ significantly in accuracy, F1 or G-mean; with an RBF-SVM they do, and COFRS ranks last. The gain is therefore compactness, not predictive accuracy.

cross Exact Volume-Preserving Diffeomorphic Shear Splitting: Continuous-Discrete Geometric Bounds, Reversible Memory Backpropagation, and Expressivity Frontiers in Volumetric Mapping

Authors: Alvaro Garcia-Casas

Abstract: Incompressible diffeomorphic registration requires continuous spatial mappings that preserve local tissue volume (det(D\Phi) = 1) without topological folding (det(D\Phi) > 0). Standard velocity-field methods (SVF, LDDMM) approximate incompressibility via numerical ODE integration or soft penalties, exhibiting numerical volume drift (~10^-4 to 10^-1), iterative grid inversions, and linear backpropagation memory O(L * N_vox). We formulate an exact structure-preserving framework for 3D registration based on cascaded coordinate shears. By decomposing the divergence-free Lie algebra sdiff(\Omega) into six coordinate-shear generators whose convective non-linearities vanish identically, each elementary stage integrates analytically to a closed-form triangular shear with det(DS_k) = 1 and exact machine-precision inverse requiring zero iterations and zero spatial interpolations. Multi-stage compositions generate the connected volume-preserving group SDiff_0(\Omega). We establish: (i) an unconditional global diffeomorphism proof closing the Hadamard-Caccioppoli injectivity gap; (ii) a Dirichlet boundary confinement lemma; (iii) an anisotropic Riemannian similarity invariance; (iv) a Sobolev H^2 spectral budget preventing chaotic mixing; and (v) an exact reversible backpropagation engine eliminating O(L) trajectory storage (0.00 MB intermediate activations). We prove second-order O(h^2) sub-voxel convergence across 16^3 to 64^3 lattices and second-order trajectory accuracy for palindromic Strang splitting (468.1x error reduction vs. Lie-Trotter; drift |det(D\Phi)-1| <= 2.22e-16). Execution is 2.1x-4.0x faster than SVF with 78.4% GPU memory reduction. On cardiac cine-MRI (ACDC, N=100) and brain MRI (OASIS-1, N=414), our model achieves competitive Dice (0.884 and 0.841) while strictly maintaining 0.0000% folding and zero volume drift.

cross CoRe: Jointly Learning Contextual Representations for Understanding and Generation

Authors: Hangke Sui, Chang Li, Yuyan Li, Mohammad Haris Baig, Daniel Ulbricht

Abstract: We introduce CoRe, in which understanding and generation jointly shape one contextual representation for images and one for text, trained from scratch. Each representation is read for understanding, conditions the other modality, and is the space in which that modality is generated by a continuous flow from Gaussian noise. With 383M parameters trained on CC12M and no pretrained language model, text encoder, or VAE at inference, CoRe matches a same-data contrastive-only baseline in global understanding and reaches COCO-30K FID 10.10, comparable to much larger generators built on pretrained components. In controlled comparisons at equal data and architecture, CoRe generates better in both directions (text to image and image to text) than a representation shaped by understanding alone and then frozen, and follows prompts better than generating in a space that understanding does not shape. In the other direction, with an attentive segmentation probe, on monocular depth, and when read by a frozen language model, CoRe surpasses the contrastive-only baseline: generation adds information that stronger readouts reach. Because generation runs inside the understanding representation, frozen real-image probes read and steer intermediate states and the model ranks its own samples without extra training. Because both modalities use continuous flows, both also distill to a few steps. Our controls indicate why one representation suffices: understanding organizes the representation semantically, while generation makes it retain more information.

cross OmniX: A first jet and event model

Authors: Anna Hallin, Gregor Kasieczka, Henning Rose

Abstract: Foundation models are a new approach to building machine learning algorithms for particle physics. Their central idea is that a single model can be adapted to a variety of downstream tasks, amortizing training costs. So far, most foundation model prototypes have operated on the constituents of a single jet. This limits their usefulness for physics problems at the scale of full events, such as distinguishing different processes, and restricts their performance, as global correla- tions are invisible to the model. Here, we introduce OmniX, an architecture that operates on both single jets and whole events within a single model. Using the jet-level JetClass and Top-tagging datasets and the event-level Collide-2V dataset, we demonstrate that OmniX trained jointly on single jets and full events matches or exceeds otherwise equivalent jet-only and event-only models in generation and classification.

cross Learning What vs. Learning How: Decomposing Task and Interface Competence in Agentic Training

Authors: Chaeeun Lee, Hossein A. Rahmani, Aryo Pradipta Gema, Kexin Xu, Emine Yilmaz, Pasquale Minervini, T. Ian Simpson, Chen Jin, Amrutha Saseendran

Abstract: LLM agents operate through harnesses that define system prompts, action formats, feedback loops, and resource constraints. A model may possess the knowledge needed to solve a task yet fail to express that knowledge through a particular harness. This raises a basic question: when a policy fails under a given harness, is it missing task knowledge or the competence to operate the interface? We distinguish two components of agentic competence: task competence, the knowledge and reasoning needed to solve a task, and interface competence, the ability to act on that knowledge through a given harness. Across four reasoning and agentic benchmarks, we show that harness-conditioned training yields vastly different outcomes depending on how these interact. Multi-harness distillation aggregates task knowledge from trajectories collected through different harnesses into a shared policy, but this knowledge is realised only when training preserves or improves competence on the inference-time interface. Otherwise, single-harness training can induce interference in interface competence, sometimes catastrophically collapsing performance on a held-out harness despite improvements in underlying task knowledge. To disentangle task and interface supervision, we introduce harness rehearsal: distillation on traces that carry minimal task knowledge while exercising the target interface. Cross-harness task supervision or rehearsal alone yields little gain, but their combination yields gains far larger than either: on BFCL V3 multi-turn with Qwen3-4B, they achieve 8.5% and 32.5% respectively, versus 58.8% together. Finally, on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) is more robust than distillation to single-harness training: it does not substantially degrade performance on other harnesses, and interface competence acquired through multi-harness distillation persists through subsequent RL.

cross The Recycler: In-Transit Machine Learning on High-Fidelity Data from Simulation Campaigns as an Optimized Approach for Building Frontier Models

Authors: Nikhil Rao, Mark Berrill, Ankush Checkervarty, Alexander Debus, Greg Eisenhauer, Ana Gainaru, Scott Klasky, Julian Lenz, Tapish Narwal, Vedhas Pandit, Richard Pausch, Norbert Podhorszki, Franz P\"oschel, Ulrich Schramm, Klaus Steiniger, Jessica Tiebel, Ren\'e Widera, Nico Wrobel, Yongqi Xiang, Michael Bussmann, Sunita Chandrasekaran, Jeffrey Kelling

Abstract: We report on the first realization of in-transit machine learning using data streamed from multiple simulations running in parallel to the training on the same exascale high performance computing system. This workflow can be commonly applied to a broad variety of scenarios in which simulation campaigns play an important role. Unlike single simulations, campaigns run several simulations in parallel. This is interesting for finding an optimum operation point of a machine like a particle accelerator or a fusion reactor, quantifying the variation in model predictions e.g. in climate research, or discovering new capabilities e.g. in materials or drug design. Such campaigns produce huge amounts of high-fidelity data at high rates. File system bandwidth and capacity prohibit retaining all of that data, usually requiring orders of magnitude in reduction before it can be stored. We demonstrate a workflow that streams the data from multiple simulations to train a frontier model in parallel, thereby maximizing data throughput. We demonstrate that this can use a much larger amount of high-fidelity data as training data for the model compared to storage in files, essentially replacing file IO by in-transit training of large-scale frontier models. This will allow to create AI-informed feedback loops to steer simulation campaigns while reducing the overall computation time needed and provide high-fidelity frontier models for reuse by the scientific community.

cross Learning from Human Corrections through Temporal Preferences

Authors: Anjiabei Wang, Tesca Fitzgerald

Abstract: When people intervene to correct a robot, they provide information not only through how they correct its behavior, but also through when they choose to intervene. Existing approaches to Learning from Corrections focus on comparing the new trajectory to the robot's originally intended behavior, thus underutilizing the context of the robot's behavior before the intervention. In this work, we investigate how both pre- and post-intervention behavior can be translated into preference pairs for learning value functions that approximate a human's latent reward. We construct post-intervention preferences by comparing corrected behavior with the behavior it replaces, and pre-intervention preferences from temporal relationships among states leading up to an intervention. We evaluate the learned value representations through two downstream tasks: goal inference and correction forecasting. Our results show that post-intervention preferences are particularly effective for goal inference, while pre-intervention preferences provide strong correction-forecasting performance. Combining both sources of information performs strongly across both tasks, even when generalizing to participants unseen during training. These findings demonstrate that both when and how humans correct provide complementary information for learning value representations from corrective interactions.

cross ModernELECTRA: Replaced-Token Detection with Boundary-Window Reconstruction for Compact Encoders that Classify and Embed

Authors: Mykhailo Shtopko, Ihor Stepanov, Dmytro Vodianytskyi, Oleksandr Lukashov

Abstract: ELECTRA-style replaced-token detection (RTD) is a sample-efficient objective for pre-training text encoders, and RTD-trained models such as DeBERTaV3 are strong on classification benchmarks. The same models, however, are known to be poor backbones for embedding tasks such as retrieval and semantic similarity: the binary "original vs. replaced" signal does not ask the final layer to retain the token identity and contextual content that dense representations rely on. We introduce ModernELECTRA, a pre-training recipe that keeps RTD as the main objective and adds a lightweight auxiliary task, boundary-window reconstruction. For every token, we sample a window of 3-12 tokens that strictly contains it and reconstruct the token's original identity only from the discriminator's hidden states at the two window boundaries. The task costs one small FFN at training time and is discarded afterwards, yet it forces every final-layer state to carry information about its neighbourhood. Combined with a frozen, separately pre-trained generator, a temperature curriculum for hard negatives and a 512-to-8192 length curriculum, we train two compact encoders: EdgeCoder-31M, an 8-layer English model, and EdgeCoder-Multilingual-61M, a 16-layer model covering 46 languages, each on about 1T tokens. They reach 86.0 and 85.1 on GLUE. The English model outperforms Ettin-Enc-32m (83.5) and MiniLM-L12 (84.6) on GLUE while matching them on MTEB English v2 (51.1), and the multilingual model outperforms the 2.3x larger mmBERT-small on GLUE and Multilingual MiniLM and mDistilBERT on XTREME over the languages it covers. We also describe FlashDeBERTa, our open-source fused implementation of DeBERTa's disentangled attention that stores no $n \times n$ score matrix: at 4096 tokens it is 10x faster and uses 93% less memory than the reference implementation, and it loads existing DeBERTa checkpoints unchanged.

cross Measuring Counterfactual Credit in a Replayable Wildfire-Suppression Benchmark

Authors: Alireza Moghaddasi

Abstract: In cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning, learned critics are usually evaluated by how well they predict the team's return. Yet predicting a team's return and assigning credit to individual actions are distinct tasks. To test credit directly, we introduce FLARE (Fireline Learning Agents in a Replayable Environment), a stochastic wildfire suppression benchmark in which any moment of a fire can be replayed with different actions under the same random events. Replaying one step measures the immediate effect of each agent's suppression, which we call simulator credit and use for training. Replaying to the end of the episode measures the effect of each action, which we use for grading critics. In a comparison over eight seeds with multi-agent proximal policy optimization (MAPPO), simulator credit reduces the interquartile mean burn score (each cell's burning time, capped and averaged over the map; lower is better) from 26.6% with shared reward to 20.9%. Learned credit from a centralized COMA-style critic does no better than the shared reward. Four trained critics predict the team's return well, yet the credit they assign is 9 to 42 times larger than the credit measured by replay. The critics also give heterogeneous agents nearly identical action rankings. These results show that a good fit to the team's return is a weak test of a critic's credit.

cross ExitLUT: Boolean Early Exits for Efficient FPGA-Based Neural Network Inference

Authors: Oliver Cassidy, Marta Andronic, George A. Constantinides

Abstract: Early exit neural networks reduce average inference cost by terminating once sufficient confidence is reached. On FPGAs, conventional confidence-based exits add arithmetic control paths poorly matched to LUT fabrics, consuming resources that could otherwise support the main datapath. We present ExitLUT, which replaces softmax-style exit decisions with learned Boolean functions mapped directly into FPGA LUTs. In our CNN evaluation, fpgaConvNet is the static spatial-dataflow baseline, while ATHEENA is its softmax-based early exit extension. ExitLUT reduces ATHEENA's exit-decision LUT usage by 2.58$\times$ over the softmax path and eliminates decision-path DSP usage, while preserving accuracy and exit behavior. Rerunning design-space exploration reinvests these savings into the datapath, improving throughput by up to 4.08$\times$ over fpgaConvNet and 1.82$\times$ over ATHEENA. For LUT-based NNs, embedded ExitLUT exits achieve 1.41-1.89$\times$ average time-to-decision speedup over static NeuraLUT-Assemble designs with under 1% LUT overhead.

cross LUCID: Statistically Grounded Self-Correction for LLM Reasoning Evaluation

Authors: Satiyabooshan Murugaboopathy, Adel Daoud, Connor T. Jerzak

Abstract: Cheap language-model raters grade chain-of-thought reasoning at scale, yet systematic step-rating errors can skew reasoning audits and solution rankings. We introduce LUCID (Loop for Unbiased Calibration via Inference-time Diagnosis), which pairs prompt self-correction with Design-based Supervised Learning (DSL). Without retraining or weight access, LUCID calibrates binary step ratings by aligning predicted and expert soundness rates. From a small expert-labeled set, LUCID summarizes rater bias in plain text and prompts the rater to re-grade unseen steps. For example, on PRM800K, GPT-5-mini grades too strictly, under-rating computation, verification, and late-trace steps most acutely. In one pass, LUCID shrinks GPT-5-mini's calibration gap from 7.8 to 0.9 percentage points in absolute predicted-expert rate difference. LUCID also halves false alarms (sound steps rated unsound) and lifts error-detection F1 on unsound steps by 32%. LUCID retains these gains with 1,000 diagnostic steps or overall-gap feedback alone. The loop also transfers across models and domains: it applies GPT-5-mini's bias profile to lift GLM-5.2's PRM800K accuracy, and it narrows calibration gaps for overly strict but capable raters across math, science, web, and logic benchmarks. Because prompt feedback shifts thresholds without improving class-balanced sound/unsound discrimination, LUCID filters re-grading with pre-/post-checks that skip already calibrated raters and roll back ineffective passes. Finally, we reuse that expert set with raw corpus ratings in DSL for calibration and regression debiasing, and apply DSL to pair-labeled steps for sound-step transitions. DSL's out-of-fold soundness probabilities lift best-of-K selection to match expert labels, while its doubly-robust estimators beat expert-only regression fits at small budgets and cut sound-step transition error by nearly two-thirds.

cross RePT: 3D Geometry Emerges from 2D Relational Pretraining

Authors: Xize Duan, Binke Hou, Xingqiao Huang, Xinyuan Guan, Jiaxin Huang, Kaiyuan Hu, Zipeng Sun, Hong Kang, Meng Bi, Xue Liu, Yili Jin

Abstract: Learning spatial representations often relies on explicitly predicting geometric quantities, but how physical geometry is organized within learned representations remains less understood. We ask whether 3D structure can emerge from learning relations across 2D observations, without 3D prediction targets. We introduce RePT, which learns compact relations between distinct physical points through cross-view 2D point prediction. The learned relation space develops a geometric organization in which magnitudes, angles, and neighborhoods reflect physical 3D displacement. A single linear readout fitted on 16 scenes recovers displacement length and angle on 39 held-out scenes without refitting (Pearson r = 0.72 and 0.78). The ARKitScenes-trained checkpoint also retains strong geometric organization on ScanNet++, showcasing generalizability. In controlled comparisons, distinct-point relational prediction consistently yields stronger geometric organization than matched same-point correspondence learning. This organization depends on additive relations and adaptive scene context, and higher point-transfer accuracy does not by itself produce stronger organization. More broadly, our results suggest that 3D geometry need not be an explicit prediction target and can instead emerge as a structural property of representations trained to relate 2D observations, pointing to relational prediction as a complementary route to learning the representations that spatial intelligence builds on.

cross Instance-Dependent Bandit Convex Optimization in One Dimension

Authors: Felix Breuer, Alireza Bakhtiari, Kevin Jamieson

Abstract: We study the instance-dependent sample complexity of one-dimensional stochastic bandit convex optimization in the fixed-confidence setting. First, we consider minimizer localization, where the learner seeks to identify a point within distance $\varepsilon$ of the unique minimizer. We introduce a new instance-dependent quantity $\Delta_\varepsilon(f)$, corresponding to the threshold for which the sublevel set of $f-\min f$ has length $2\varepsilon$. We prove a lower bound of order $\Delta_\varepsilon(f)^{-2}$ and give a bisection-style algorithm that matches this rate up to logarithmic factors. We then revisit the simple-regret setting, where the goal is to identify an $\varepsilon$-optimal point. Surprisingly, no comparable instance-dependent improvement is possible: every correct algorithm has sample complexity of order $\varepsilon^{-2}$ on every instance, matching the minimax rate up to constants. Together, these results separate the statistical complexity of minimizer localization from that of simple-regret optimization in one-dimensional convex bandits.

cross Training decision models for simulating human respondents

Authors: Davood Wadi, Yu Ma

Abstract: Researchers increasingly use large language models (LLMs) as synthetic respondents in market research, conjoint analysis, and randomized experiments. However, synthetic choice data from off-the-shelf generative LLMs bias downstream econometric inference. Neither proprietary models nor higher reasoning effort reliably reduces this bias. We investigate decision models (System One architectures) for silicon sampling. Using choice training, we update KEV model weights on historical respondent-level choice microdata by conditional maximum likelihood, before inference and without target-study data. Across three model scales, training on two discrete choice experiments lowers treatment-effect error by 22% to 89% on four held-out studies from new domains (vaccines, nutrition labels, consumer rights, and AI) and new response formats. A small choice-trained model outperforms a much larger off-the-shelf model on three of the four studies. Although training on single studies lowers cross-entropy, it can exacerbate treatment-effect error. Choice training thus complements post-inference calibration by providing a stronger starting point for estimating human preferences.

cross What You See Is What You Certify: Concept-Based Certificates for Unseen Compositions

Authors: Phuc Linh Ngo, Thang Nguyen Duc, Truong Thanh Hung Nguyen, Hung Cao

Abstract: Concept-based models justify a decision by concepts they claim to see, decoded through an explicit ontology. Yet concept predictions are known to leak label information, ignore locality, and be poorly grounded on new attribute combinations. A decision decoded from such concepts can satisfy the ontology and still be wrong, so we ask when it can serve as a certificate. On unseen combinations of familiar parts, a joint concept bottleneck model and a concept embedding model, trained as is standard, certify wrong classes 93-94% of the time despite a correct ontology (60% for a joint model trained only on visible parts and gated by a localizer). The joint model's part errors copy co-occurring values more often than chance (cross-concept shortcutting). Treating the ontology as an error-detecting code, we show that a certificate needs visible evidence excluding every rival, unseen parts reported as such, and rare misreads not driven by a shared shortcut. A minimal reference design, OCVE, reads each part from a local crop in its own forward pass. On unseen FunnyBirds classes, it certifies 52% of images at 0.4% risk, against 14% for mask-pooled features and 33% for full-image part readers (5.6% when trained and gated alike), at similar zero-shot accuracy. The best baselines, even calibrated or restricted to unseen classes, and a decoder counting only agreeing parts remain about 3-8 times riskier. On CUB, isolated region readers are more accurate than an attribute-prototype model (63.1% vs. 55.3%) and certify unseen species at about 6 points less risk than it and about 10 points less than features pooled in the same keypoint boxes. On real traffic signs, isolated readers lower certified risk from 99.8% to 67%, which is still far too high; most remaining errors are misread pictograms. On seen classes the ontology brings no gain over a fairly trained black box, and isolation costs accuracy.

cross Anchoring VAE Fine-Tuning to Its Latent Diffusion Prior

Authors: Rafael Bischof, Dominik Narnhofer, Julius Erbach, Michael Kraus, Konrad Schindler, Bernd Bickel

Abstract: Pretrained latent diffusion models are effective priors for transfer learning, from geometry estimation to physics simulation. Existing approaches fine-tune the diffusion backbone but freeze the encoder of the VAE to preserve the latent distribution. However, a frozen encoder may miss salient details in non-RGB inputs and is limited to three channels. We introduce Diffusion Prior Anchoring (DPA), a regularizer applied during VAE fine-tuning that uses a score-distillation update to steer latents toward the high-density manifold on which the pretrained diffusion model denoises accurately. We validate DPA across three image and video models (FLUX 3, LTX-Video, Wan2.2) and four tasks: monocular depth estimation, MRI reconstruction, wind-flow simulation, and surface normal estimation, where DPA lowers the prediction error by 7.5% relative to the frozen VAE, 4.9% to decoder-only fine-tuning, and 4.0% to unanchored VAE fine-tuning. Its gain grows the further the target data lies from the RGB training distribution, from 3.3% on monocular depth estimation to 7.2% on complex-valued MRI. DPA also applies to modified VAEs, including pruned and multi-channel variants. With a four-channel VAE that encodes depth and surface normals, it lowers depth estimation error by 9.3%.

cross A Piecewise-Linear Activation Function for Implicit Neural Representations

Authors: Shahd Hekal, Joram Kim, Jeova Farias Sales Rocha Neto

Abstract: Coordinate networks with ReLU activations perform poorly on implicit neural representation tasks, and the activations that address this, such as sinusoids and Gabor wavelets, all leave the piecewise-linear function class, so it remains unclear which property of ReLU is at fault. We introduce JaSIN, a family of bounded piecewise-linear activations that interpolates between ReLU and a piecewise-linear sinusoid without any the use of transcendental operation. Our results show that a bounded range improves image reconstruction PSNR on average by 0.06 dB, a single bump 3.29 dB, and centering that bump a further 3.64 dB. The oscillation count improves 6.30 dB more, raising ReLU from 24.0 to 37.3 dB on Kodak images, placing third over eleven activations, within 1.3 dB of the state-of-the-art, though beyond four oscillations the reconstruction collapses. Because an INR is fit on the grid on which it is evaluated, a unit whose pre-activations never reach the support of its activation is certifiably inactive over the entire domain; deleting such units is lossless without fine-tuning, removing up to 84% of parameters at zero loss in performance (dB) and decoding 3.2 times faster. Reference code and demos are available at https://github.com/jeovafarias/jasin.

URLs: https://github.com/jeovafarias/jasin.

cross Online Statistical Inference for Derivative-Free Constrained Stochastic Optimization

Authors: Jianliang Ye, Sen Na

Abstract: We study statistical inference for nonlinear equality-constrained stochastic optimization problems in settings where derivative information is unavailable or expensive to obtain. While recent advances in stochastic sequential quadratic programming (SSQP) have enabled efficient online optimization, existing methods either rely on gradient information or suffer from high computational costs. To address these limitations, we develop a sketched derivative-free SSQP (SDF-SSQP) method that simultaneously achieves computational efficiency and valid statistical inference. By leveraging randomized sketching, the proposed method reduces the per-iteration computational cost to $O((d+m)^2)$, where $d$ is the dimension of the decision variable and $m$ is the number of constraints, making it well suited for large-scale problems. To overcome the challenges that both sketching and zeroth-order approximations introduce for statistical inference, we adopt a random-scaling approach that eliminates the need for explicit estimation of the asymptotic covariance matrix. We establish the asymptotic normality of the averaged primal--dual iterates and construct a self-normalized statistic that is asymptotically pivotal. The resulting inference procedure is fully online, requires neither covariance estimation nor matrix inversion, and incurs an additional per-iteration cost of the same order as that of the optimization update. Numerical experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method in terms of both optimization performance and statistical inference accuracy.

cross CATWAY: Cost-sensitive agentic three-way robot decisions with calibrated language-model confidence

Authors: Erik Derner

Abstract: A language-model robot agent must decide whether to execute, question or refuse an instruction, and an unsafe execution costs far more than an unneeded question. No prior robot gate derives all its thresholds from error costs or acts on calibrated probabilities, and none was fitted in one domain and evaluated unchanged in another. We propose CATWAY, a cost-sensitive three-way decision gate: a loss matrix over four instruction conditions (clear, ambiguous, infeasible, unsafe) fixes execute, ask and refuse regions on the probability simplex, the question being the information-gathering step. Its probabilities fuse a ten-sample vote with graded scores of ambiguity, infeasibility and risk by logistic regression. Fitted on household instructions and frozen, CATWAY was tested on text-level decisions of three small models in-domain, in two new robot domains and on the external TRUST-NAV benchmark. At eleven generations per decision against one, it was significantly cheaper than a direct prompt, a cost-aware prompt and an unguarded agent in all 18 household comparisons (mean loss per item 0.28 against 0.57-0.83), in 9 of 18 in the new domains and in 10 of 18 on TRUST-NAV (Holm per set), and never significantly costlier. It executed none of the 142 unsafe test instructions. On a unanimous vote, the minimum-expected-loss rule reduces calibration to one loss-derived threshold, so for two of six model configurations a gate on the vote alone never executed a clear instruction, costing 0.49 per item on TRUST-NAV against CATWAY's 0.13 and 0.09; the fused estimate avoided this on every set.

cross Stopping criteria in structural reliability analysis: An overview

Authors: Reiichiro Kawai

Abstract: Stopping criteria have become a crucial component of probabilistic and statistical methods in structural reliability analysis, helping prevent structural defects and failures, as well as inaccurate evaluations of existing structures. Over time, these rules have evolved in both concept and application, whereas their treatment in the literature remains fragmented. They are often developed and presented in connection with specific models or analytical frameworks, obscuring their broader applicability across diverse models and methodologies. On this basis, the aim of this article is twofold. First, it overviews a wide range of stopping rules, drawing on concepts from applied probability and statistics. These strategies include classical methods based on error estimates or variance, and stability detection. While many of these ideas were developed in other fields, they have the potential to offer useful tools for structural reliability tasks like managing sampling, improving surrogate models, and checking for convergence. Second, this article introduces a structured framework to organize past and recent developments in this area. It brings together various stopping criteria, clarifies their conceptual roles, explains their theoretical foundations, and compares their practical strengths and limitations. By relating these methods to common modeling and methodological practices, and by incorporating a systematic comparison of performance aspects such as accuracy, computational cost, and robustness, we aim to identify dominant strategies, highlight shared challenges, and point to directions for further progress.

cross EventSSM: Causal Spatial-State Modelling for Asynchronous Events Event-Depth Estimation

Authors: Sadiq O. Macaulay, Dipon Ghosh, Hadfield Simon

Abstract: Event cameras report asynchronous brightness changes, yet metric-depth supervision is typically available only at sparsely sampled reference timestamps. Aggregating events into frame-like representations simplifies learning but can obscure the fine-grained temporal structure of the original event stream. We introduce EventSSM, a causal spatial-state framework for event-associated metric-depth estimation using a recurrent memory updated by chronological event packets. To bridge asynchronous predictions and frame-time supervision, predicted event depths are geometrically transported using calibrated camera poses and differentiably rendered at reference timestamps. Controlled ablations further examine cross-modal initialization, temporal recurrence, individual state, readout, and supervision components, and packet subdivision. Together, these findings establish EventSSM as an effective framework for causal event-associated metric-depth estimation, combining persistent spatial context with physically timed state retention.

cross RoMo Hands: A Large Scale Richly Organized Text to Hand Motion Dataset

Abstract: While text-driven body motion generation has matured, pure-hand synthesis remains bottlenecked by data. cross How Far Apart Are Two Proofs? Measuring Proof Dissimilarity in a Proof Kernel and in Lean/Mathlib

Authors: Nikolaos Nikolaou

Abstract: We now live in the age of machine-generated mathematics: AI systems produce research-level proofs faster than the human mathematical community can read them. Verification is only part of the challenge. We also need to know whether a "new proof" is indeed new, whether a thousand generated proofs contain one idea or many, and where each sits relative to human mathematics. All of these require measuring distances between proofs. Following Hilbert's 24th problem and the identity-of-proofs problem, we ask how far apart two proofs of one theorem are, and what shape the proof space they inhabit has. We define 19 dissimilarity measures in 6 families (syntactic, structural, dependency & resource, semantic, information-theoretic, higher-categorical) and 10 desirable properties, which we argue are the conditions for safely optimising a measure. In a Curry-Howard kernel, 5 machine-checked proofs that the primes are infinite show that raw syntax cannot tell a rewrite from a new argument, that dependency-based and structural measures reproduce the intuitive ordering (Euclid rewritten < Goldbach < Furstenberg), and that semantic measures invert it: Furstenberg's topological proof produces exactly Euclid's witness. A replication in Lean 4/Mathlib (3 theorems, 19 proofs, 18 measures) confirms that dependency-closure measures track intuition best, and shows that equality of normal forms never holds between differently written versions of the same argument, that proofs by citation look unlike the arguments they cite, and that proof irrelevance cannot distinguish any two proofs. We argue for reporting dissimilarity as a multi-level profile, extend the measures to proofs of different theorems, and discuss uses, from detecting duplicated AI proofs and orchestrating prover agents to interpreting AI proofs via their nearest human-understandable counterparts. Code, Lean sources and data are released.

cross Exploring Multi-Player Social Games as Verifiable Training Grounds for Large Language Models

Authors: Keyu He, Michael Li, Jiarui Liu, Mingqian Zheng, Koichi Onoue, Tongshuang Wu, Maarten Sap

Abstract: The training of socially intelligent language models often relies on large language model (LLM) judges, which are expensive, noisy, and vulnerable to reward hacking. Multi-player social games offer a natural alternative: they require open-ended communication to coordinate with teammates and infer hidden roles, while their rules provide an objective outcome which can serve as a verifiable reward for training. Using three social games, we explore how much social intelligence Qwen3 models can acquire through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). Results show that against a much stronger Gemma-3-27B model, SFT and RLVR raise the win rate of Qwen3-4B from 33% to 81% on the three trained games, and from 39% to 54% on three additional held-out games. In contrast, in-context strategies such as prompting improve the win rate of Qwen3-4B by only about 6 pp across the same games we train and evaluate on. Furthermore, we show that the gains from SFT and RLVR hold across the model sizes 1.7B, 4B, and 32B. Qualitative analyses of agent behavior show signs of stronger social intelligence; for example, trained agents tend to share information more openly, check each other's claims against public evidence, and coordinate their votes. More broadly, our results demonstrate that training LLMs on verifiable outcomes in social games can cultivate generalizable social behaviors, which offers a scalable path for building socially intelligent agents.

cross Spec-Test Coverage Gap: Detecting Overspecified Tests in SWE-Bench-Style Benchmarks

Authors: Avi Aggarwal, Yiran Hu

Abstract: SWE-bench-style benchmarks, such as SWE-bench Verified (Verified) and SWE-bench Pro (Pro), evaluate coding agents with tests taken from the change that originally fixed each issue. However, these tests can enforce details the task never states, causing correct alternative fixes to fail. Such overspecified tests have so far been found mainly through expensive expert review. To address this, we define the Spec-Test Coverage Gap (STCG), a 1-4 scale measuring overspecification, and score it with two LLM judges, Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6.1 Sol, which are given the specification and the test patch. A task is flagged as overspecified when both judges score it 3 or higher. Our study has three parts. First, we validate the judges on 206 tasks that earlier human audits and agent results label as overspecified or fine. Second, we apply STCG to all 500 Verified and 731 public Pro tasks and compare the flags with the results of the top 30 Verified submissions. Third, we verify the flags by asking Claude Opus 5.5 to loosen the overspecified requirements of flagged tasks, and regrade 2,186 failed patches from those submissions. Our main findings are: (1) Overspecified tests are detectable: on the validation tasks, the judges correctly flag 89% of the tasks that human audits identify as overspecified and wrongly flag only 4% of the tasks known to be fine. (2) Overspecified tests are common: applied to whole benchmarks, the judges flag 23% of Verified and 35% of Pro tasks. (3) Flagged tasks are much harder for agents: the top 30 Verified submissions resolve only 31% of the flagged tasks on average, against 88% of the other tasks. (4) Overspecified tests change the leaderboard: relaxing the tests lets 61% of the 2,186 failed patches on flagged tasks pass. Counting these patches as resolved would add 33 to 60 tasks to each top-30 submission, raising resolve rates by up to 12 percentage points.

cross A General Framework for E-process Stopping Rules in Best-Arm Identification

Authors: Donghyun Park, Seohwa Hwang, Junyong Park

Abstract: We propose an e-process stopping rule in the best-arm identification problem. The e-process stopping rule is different from the Chernoff stopping rule by its hypothesis structure on the stopping decision. The major benefit of an e-process stopping rule is the flexibility in stopping rule design and in fact, it recovers previously suggested Chernoff stopping rule on several bandit problems. Moreover, we analyze the asymptotic optimality of the $\delta$-PAC algorithm with the decomposition of e-processes. The decomposition separates optimality conditions into three parts; the sampling policy allocation convergence, the efficiency of e-process design, and the bandit problem's inherent stochastic behavior. Subsequently, we prove a sufficient condition for asymptotic optimality in bandit algorithms. For the application of an e-process stopping rule, we derived an asymptotically optimal algorithm for the generalized linear bandit problem.

cross MELODI: Similarity-Identity Decoupling for Lightweight Ranking

Authors: Quoc Phong Nguyen, Vuong Le, Volodymyr Vaskovych, Julien Monteil

Abstract: For latency-constrained candidate ranking, we propose a similarity-identity decoupling framework that separates target-history compatibility from target-only information. We study two orders for computing pooled similarity, similarity-then-pool and pool-then-similarity, and identify linearity of the similarity operator in its history argument, for each fixed target, as sufficient for their equivalence, justifying the cheaper pool-then-similarity form. We instantiate the framework as multi-head efficient lightweight operator for decoupling identity and similarity (MELODI), whose similarity branch combines target-conditioned multi-head history pooling with a single residual-free cross layer that is linear in its pooled-history input and whose identity branch is problem-dependent. On public benchmark datasets, MELODI matches or surpasses the evaluated target-aware and target-agnostic baselines. On two large-scale industrial datasets, its area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC), normalised discounted cumulative gain (NDCG), and recall values are equal to or higher than those of the evaluated target-aware baselines. cross DUST: Directional Uncertainty-aware and Scale-invariant Transfer Learning

Authors: Abhisek Chakraborty

Abstract: Transfer learning seeks to improve efficiency in a target regression task by borrowing information from related external source dataset(s). Existing approaches achieve this by enforcing Euclidean proximity between the target and source regression parameters or, more recently, by encouraging their directional alignment through angle-based penalties. While such angle-based penalties mitigate the sensitivity of the transfer learning methods to the scale differences between source and target regression coefficients, they still rely on a point estimate of the source regression parameter and thus provide no direct mechanism to incorporate uncertainty in the source information. To that end, we propose a probabilistic framework for transfer learning that operates at the level of a \emph{scale-invariant directional distribution} of the source regression parameter estimate, rather than its point estimate, to enable robust, scale-invariant and uncertainty aware transfer under heterogeneous source information. Specifically, we project the sampling distribution of the source regression estimate onto the unit sphere, thereby extracting its scale-free directional distribution. Transfer from source to target is then induced through a novel \emph{angular cone prior} which shrinks the target regression parameter towards directions in which the source distribution assigns high probability, while allowing its magnitude to be learned exclusively from the target dataset. The proposed construction admits exact finite-sample characterizations of posterior angular concentration and posterior computation remains tractable via careful low-dimensional augmentation in the parameter space.

cross Are Web Agents Still Detectable? Stress-Testing Bot Defenses with Coordinated Evasion

Authors: Parsa Nilforushan, Gemmin Sugiura, Kourosh Hashemi, Peiman Zhiani, Mohammad A. Tayebi

Abstract: AI web agents can autonomously navigate websites and complete various tasks. As their capabilities and adoption expand, evaluating bot defenses requires adversarial stress tests. Although prior work demonstrates that existing agents can remain detectable, it remains unclear how effective bot defenses are against adversaries that coordinate evasion across browser fingerprints, automation controls, and interaction behavior. To investigate this threat, we introduce DarkAgent, an autonomous web agent that combines a forked Chromium browser that conceals automation artifacts and generative models that produce human-like mouse and keyboard traces. To address the limitations of existing keyboard mimickers, our keyboard model jointly generates realistic key events and their timings while reliably reproducing the target text. Released agent detectors accept our generated behavior as human, and trained classifiers find it substantially harder to distinguish from human traces than existing mimickers. DarkAgent achieves an 89% page-access bypass rate across a third-party stealth benchmark, exceeding the strongest published baseline by 12 percentage points. To further enhance the measurement of agents, we introduce a benchmark that measures browser fingerprints, interaction behavior, and network activity to assess each channel's contribution to detecting adversarial agents such as DarkAgent. On this benchmark, browser and behavioral modifications are complementary: either alone remains detectable, and only their combination evades all tested checks. Together, our findings expose the gap between passing existing bot defenses and reproducing human behavior, and point to timing between actions as a remaining signal for defenders.

cross A Minimax Optimal Leave One Out Bound for Bounded Losses

Authors: Peigan Gao, Jian Qian

Abstract: We study proper leave-one-out prediction with $M$ real-valued experts on $n$ fixed inputs. Each label is hidden in turn, and the learner uses the remaining labels to choose one of the experts' predictions at the held-out input. We determine how closely such a rule can match the average loss of the best single expert on the full sample. For any bounded loss that is a continuous nondecreasing function of absolute error and vanishes at zero, let $B$ denote its maximum value. Over classes of size $M$ and samples on which the best expert has average loss at most $r$, the minimax excess loss is of order $\min\{B, \sqrt{\frac{Br\log M}{n}}+\frac{B\log M}{n}\}$. For each class and loss, one deterministic rule attains this rate for every $r\in[0,B]$. The matching lower bound holds even for randomized proper rules given $r$.

Authors: Patrick Tser Jern Kon, Akanksha Jain, Abraham Gonzalez, Rui Meng, Dilara Soylu, Shubham Agarwal, Yawen Wang, Iman Hosseini, Hanna Alam, Jinsung Yoon, Tomas Pfister, Nanyun Peng, Chun-Liang Li, Parthasarathy Ranganathan

Abstract: Shared last-level caches (LLCs) increasingly dominate server processor die area, forcing architects to trade core counts for cache capacity. While intelligent data prefetching offers a pathway to reclaim this silicon, designing coordinated, multi-level prefetching policies for multi-core systems is bottlenecked by day-long, cycle-level simulations. This simulation wall limits even LLM-based microarchitecture discovery; the state-of-the-art ArchAgent system evolves one cache level at a time on single-core benchmarks, adds multi-core configurations only at the end, and trails the human champion under multi-core shared cache contention. cross How Much Dependency Can a Marginal Rule See? Total Correlation in Diffusion LM Parallel Decoding

Authors: Dovud Asadov, Rustambek Urokov, Qobiljon Toshnazarov, Abduaziz Kayumov, Mardon Hazratov, Husanboy Mansuraliyev

Abstract: A masked diffusion language model is fast because it commits several tokens per step. Dream-instruct is the exception, mainly on prose, where its conditionals are least self-consistent. Among six common marginal rules, pooling over the set beats gating on its weakest position in all 24 paired comparisons. The measurement needs a validated readout: Dream and LLaDA place a position's prediction at different logit indices, and the wrong index inflates an inconsistency diagnostic 14 to 41 times. We give three checks that measure the convention. Our results concern predicting C for a fixed set, not what a dependency-aware decoder gains.

Abstract: Language models are increasingly used to simulate how an audience will respond to a headline or a post, and such simulators are validated against what people say. Authors: Shengkai Lyu, Hairui Zhao, Daran Sun, Yida Gu, Xingchen Liu, YueYuan Zhou, Zheng Wei, Guangming Tan, Dingwen Tao

Abstract: Training large language models (LLMs) requires choosing both a batch size and a parallel configuration. Existing systems make these decisions in isolation, tuning parallelism for throughput and batch size for convergence, but neither objective alone determines training efficiency. We present LIBRA, a lightweight runtime framework that co-tunes batch size and parallel configuration to minimize end-to-end time. LIBRA treats throughput and convergence as two noisy objectives and searches their joint space using parallel noisy expected hypervolume improvement (qNEHVI). cross IncrementDET: Continual AI-Generated Music Detection via Enrollment

Authors: Runbao Du, Wei Li, Qile He

Abstract: AI-generated music has become common on music streaming platforms, and listeners often cannot distinguish it from music made by humans. AI-music detection is therefore needed, but detectors trained on a fixed set of generators often fail on unseen generators. As new generators emerge frequently, updating a universal detector, a single model for all generators, typically requires substantial training data and computation, while risking performance degradation on previously learned generators. We therefore introduce IncrementDET, a detector that learns each new generator by a process called enrollment. For each generator, IncrementDET trains one linear classifier, which we call a needle, to separate samples of that generator from a fixed set of human reference tracks. Enrolling a new generator adds one needle while the previously constructed needles remain unchanged. A Sidak correction keeps the overall false-positive rate at its target as needles are added. To compare detectors under these conditions, we define the task of continual AI-generated music detection and introduce a benchmark in which generators arrive one at a time. The benchmark measures the accuracy of a detector before and after each update and the cost of each update. For this benchmark, we also construct EvoMusicFake-17K, a dataset of instrumental music from six recent generators and four human music platforms. Compared with SpecTTTra, a state-of-the-art universal detector, IncrementDET learns a new generator more accurately from only a few samples, generalizes better to unseen generators and better preserves detection performance on previously learned generators, at a substantially lower update cost. The structure of IncrementDET can also be applied to the detection of generated images and video, where new generators are released just as frequently.

cross Unifying Partner and Environment Diversity to Improve Human-AI Coordination

Authors: Patrick Q. Zhang, Yancheng Liang, Adrienne Fairhall, Natasha Jaques

Abstract: Real-world applications of modern AI systems require generalization to both unseen environments and novel human users. The field of zero-shot coordination (ZSC) has focused primarily on deploying AI to cooperate with novel partners in otherwise familiar environments. Popular population-based methods achieve this by partnering the agent with a diversity of simulated human users during training in a fixed environment, but does not consider the more realistic scenario where both the users and the environment are unseen during training. Recently, training across environments has been explored in training a single self-play agent, which shows better generalization on both novel human users and unseen environments. In this work, we ask whether jointly modeling partner diversity and environment diversity yields stronger coordination with novel partners in novel environments. We introduce Cross Environment Co-Play (CECP), a two-stage framework that first trains a population of self-play agents across a distribution of procedurally generated environments, and then trains a cooperator policy against this partner population. To further couple social and environmental reasoning, we add a next-state prediction module that encourages representations to encode both partner behavior and task structure. We evaluate CECP on both a neuroscience-inspired cooperative foraging task, and the popular ZSC benchmark Overcooked, under held-out partner and held-out environment conditions. CECP achieves the strongest performance among the compared baselines and attains state-of-the-art results under our evaluation protocol for human-AI collaboration. We analyze the model's latent representation encoded by the next-state prediction module, showing it can not only improve the performance of the cooperative AI, but also provide more interpretable latents, demonstrated by the inference of partner skill levels.

Authors: Zhengzhong Ricky You, Baichuan Mo, Yuan Zhou, Jinhua Zhao, Zuo-Jun Max Shen

Abstract: In contextual stochastic optimization, integrated learning trains conditional models on operational loss, but repeatedly solving stochastic programs with many scenarios is costly. We propose KKT Atlas, which partitions contexts into local charts and identifies a low-dimensional subspace per chart from decision gradients of the scenario cost. We show that one more scenario than the subspace dimension suffices to approximate the conditional Karush-Kuhn-Tucker optimality condition under regularity conditions, yielding decision and risk bounds under local strong convexity, with generalization error scaling with the compact parameter dimension. cross EEGJev: A System One Decision Model and Benchmark for Multi-Task EEG Question Answering

Authors: Dengzhe Hou, Kazunori D. Yamada

Abstract: A single electroencephalographic (EEG) recording can support multiple decoding tasks. Fixed-label tests cannot show how EEG foundation models handle changing questions or options. Across 13 test sources, EEGJev selected a label for the requested attribute with 99.70% accuracy (49.20% under uniform choice). Balanced accuracy for the specific answer was 62.04%. Its 95th-percentile response time was shorter than NeuroLM's on matched validation questions answered individually. EEGJev lets users switch decoding targets by changing the question. With EEGJev-Bench, researchers can test whether new decoders follow the question more reliably, identify the correct answer more often, or both.

cross EnSurv: Deep Generative Survival Modeling with Application to Kidney Graft Outcomes

Authors: Rajdeep Pathak, Tamalika Koley, Shreyashi Basak, Dipankar Bandyopadhyay, Tanujit Chakraborty

Abstract: Kidney transplantation is the optimal treatment for life-threatening end-stage renal disease, providing significant survival advantages over dialysis. Given the severe shortage of donor organs, accurately predicting the time to premature graft failure is an essential clinical task for optimizing organ allocation and tailoring individualized immunosuppressive therapies. This study is motivated by a comprehensive United Network of Organ Sharing (UNOS) dataset containing 243,443 kidney transplant records with complex multi-region structures and high right-censoring rates. We propose EnSurv (survival engression), a deep generative survival framework designed to capture complex, non-linear clinical covariate interactions for modeling time to graft failure. EnSurv also provides global explainability through summaries of fitted feature weights and incorporates domain constraints through established clinical directions using a soft gradient penalty. Extensive simulation experiments were conducted to evaluate the performance of the proposed method in comparison with existing methods. Application to the UNOS cohort demonstrates that EnSurv effectively models individual and region-level effects, consistently outperforming baselines across evaluation metrics encompassing discrimination, calibration, and accuracy.

cross Bit-Flip Attacks on Quantized Neural Networks without Knowledge of Model Parameters

Authors: Mahmoud Abumandour, Srinija Ramichetty, Guru Venkataramani, Alaa R. Alameldeen

Abstract: We show that quantization asymmetry concentrates bit-flip vulnerability in small tensors retained at high precision. CoarseHammer targets these tensors and accepts flips at any bit, without parameter values, gradients, or data. It remains effective under continued fine-tuning. Under ideal placement, CoarseHammer reaches chance accuracy in at least 81.5% of runs on each of ten evaluated models, including four large language models, with median successful runs requiring at most 24 flips. On simulated DRAM with 0.01 flippable bits per million, it outperforms three prior attacks on every evaluated convolutional network, succeeding in up to 83.6% of runs while they never succeed. cross Interpretable Orthogonal Latent Diffusion with Scale-Selective Uncertainty for Medical Image Super-Resolution

Authors: Onkar Jadhav, Tim French

Abstract: Diffusion models are increasingly used for medical image reconstruction and super-resolution, but their samples may contain plausible fine-scale hallucinations that are difficult to identify from the reconstructed image alone. We propose Med-PODiff, a patch-wise proper orthogonal decomposition (POD) diffusion framework for brain MRI super-resolution that performs generative reconstruction in compact and interpretable local coefficient spaces. Unlike pixel-space or learned-latent diffusion models, Med-PODiff uses the POD structure to separate low-rank anatomical variation from fine-detail residual variation, enabling predictive uncertainty to be decomposed into total, coarse, and fine components. cross VIDE : Training-Free Video Anomaly Detection via Visual Intrinsic Distribution Estimation

Authors: Kevin D. Nguetche, Atif Belal, Lilian Hollard, Eric Granger, Marco Pedersoli

Abstract: In embedding-based video anomaly detection (VAD), normal clips can have high similarity to anomalous descriptions, an anomaly bias that leads to false positives. Methods such as PRISM mitigate this bias by measuring deviations from a normality reference along anomaly directions. Both the normality reference and the anomaly directions are derived from text and shared across videos, although normal activity and the appearance of anomalies vary across scenes. The resulting scores are also sensitive to prompt selection. To address these limitations, we propose Visual Intrinsic Distribution Estimation (VIDE), a training-free framework that adapts text-derived scoring to each test video using frozen clip embeddings. VIDE estimates normal scene content from clips with low initial anomaly scores and uses this estimate to partially correct the textual reference. It then rescales category responses, penalizes clips with strong responses across categories, and refines the anomaly directions using high-scoring clips. VIDE averages scores across prompt views to reduce sensitivity to prompt selection and fuses scores from two frozen encoders. With both encoders, VIDE improves AUC over the best PRISM result by $1.5$, $1.9$, and $3.5$ points on UCF-Crime, XD-Violence, and MSAD, respectively, and AP by $6.5$ points on XD-Violence and $9.3$ points on MSAD. VIDE yields the highest AUC among the compared training-free methods on all three benchmarks and the highest reported AUC among all compared methods on XD-Violence and MSAD. Our code is included in the supplementary material and will be made publicly available.

cross ARS-VLM: Visually Aligned Augmented Random Search for Vision-Language Models

Authors: Xiangwei Wang, Jianyuan Lin, Peng Wang, Saman Halgamuge

Abstract: First-order reinforcement learning, such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), is widely used to post-train Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for multimodal reasoning. However, it incurs heavy memory and system overhead from backpropagation, optimizer states, and a reference model. Forward-only zeroth-order (ZO) methods, such as Evolution Strategies (ES) and its top-k variant Augmented Random Search (ARS), avoid these overheads via reward-guided weight perturbations, yet remain unexplored for VLM reasoning. We find that directly applying standard ES or vanilla ARS to VLMs suffers from two fundamental failure modes: (i) patch-grid overfitting, fitting fragile grid-specific visual token artifacts while ignoring spatial invariants, and (ii) catastrophic forgetting, degrading general perception and out-of-distribution (OOD) tasks due to unbounded high-rank parameter and policy drift. We propose ARS-VLM, a visually aligned Augmented Random Search framework for forward-only VLM post-training. First, ARS-VLM extracts a contrastive visual basis from bilinearly paired cross-grid views, concentrating perturbations on shared visual signals while suppressing patch-shift artifacts. Second, ARS-VLM anchors shallow visual-fusion layers with a soft subspace prior while freeing deep reasoning layers, restraining policy drift without a reference model. Third, ARS-VLM scores perturbations on paired views to discard view-fragile directions during top-k selection. We evaluate ARS-VLM on Qwen3-VL-4B and Qwen3-VL-8B on multimodal reasoning tasks. On 8B, ARS-VLM reaches 61.0% validation accuracy in 20 steps versus 150 for GRPO (7.5x fewer optimization steps) and avoids the late OOD collapse of vanilla ARS and ES. Moreover, ARS-VLM achieves 75.41% single-view micro accuracy across seven OOD vision reasoning benchmarks, compared with 75.31% for full-parameter GRPO, using forward-only post-training.

cross Beyond Autoregression: Diffusion Language Model is All Analog In-Memory Computing Need

Authors: Yuanuo Feng, Yubing Ye, Yizhe Chen, Wenshuai Yao, Ngai Wong, Wenyong Zhou, Wang Kang

Abstract: Analog in-memory computing (AIMC) enables energy-efficient language-model inference, but hardware non-idealities perturb matrix--vector multiplications and degrade generation quality. Autoregressive (AR) models are especially vulnerable: each token is committed to the causal context at birth, so perturbation-induced errors cascade. We study diffusion language models (DLMs), which iteratively refine a masked sequence, as an alternative paradigm. Across scale-matched model pairs and open checkpoints, DLMs degrade more gracefully than AR counterparts under persistent weight perturbations. Controlled analyses rule out cross-round noise averaging and support delayed token commitment and progressive context resolution as the mechanism. A larger denoising-step budget further shrinks the clean--noisy gap, a DLM-specific latency--robustness knob. We perform large-scale simulation tests across diverse datasets and models using a noise model calibrated from real silicon measurements. Furthermore, on a 16-chip 28nm eFlash AIMC cluster, hybrid on-board measurements show DLMs tolerate $1.4$--$3\times$ larger weight noise, making diffusion-based generation a promising paradigm for noise-resilient AIMC inference.

cross Relational Bottleneck Models

Authors: Shin'ya Yamaguchi, Kosuke Nishida

Abstract: Achieving both interpretable and high-performance inference is a key challenge in deep learning. While Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) provide inherent interpretability, they are constrained by task-specific training and rely on isolated visual concepts that fail to capture higher-order visual semantic relationships. Conversely, Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs) demonstrate zero-shot capabilities through Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. However, standard CoT lacks a strict causal dependency between the generated rationale and the final output, compromising faithfulness. To bridge this gap, we propose Relational Bottleneck Models (RBMs), a novel training-free framework that transforms any off-the-shelf LVLMs into inherently interpretable models in a plug-and-play manner. RBMs consist of two stages: (i) Structured Rationale Generation (SRG), which explicitly models semantic interactions between visual concepts as subject-predicate-object triplets, and (ii) Rationale-Grounded Decoding (RGD), which enforces a strict causal dependency between the rationales and the final output. RGD aggregates rationale-conditional next-token distributions via an importance-weighted product-of-experts strategy, strictly excluding the image inputs from the decoding context. Extensive zero-shot evaluations across diverse benchmarks and LVLM backbones demonstrate that RBMs achieve superior interpretability while improving task performance over CBM baselines, entirely without task-specific fine-tuning.

cross MYSTIQUE: Few-Shot Facial Identity Transfer with Performance Preservation

Authors: Shihan Cheng, David Hyde, Junting Deng, Michael Bao, Paul Debevec, Roshni Cooper, Soshi Shimada

Abstract: In visual effects, identity transfer replaces the identity in a driving image with that of a target person, while preserving the expression, lighting, pose and scene of that image. Paired ground truth is rarely available, so many methods are trained by self-reconstruction, where the model can learn a shortcut by copying identity cues from the driving image. This shortcut causes identity leakage at inference, where the driving image shows a different person. Our key observation is that leakage can enter through any input that reads the driving image. We therefore present MYSTIQUE, a diffusion-based identity transfer method that takes identity from a set of the target's photos and reads expression, lighting and scene through separate streams. It learns to disentangle identity from the other streams through multi-stage training, with disentanglement strategies at their inputs: a random block mask whose boundaries do not follow the face outline, self-augmentation, which randomly changes the face shape in the expression input and keeps its expression, and face randomization, which replaces the person in the expression and lighting inputs while the ground truth stays the driving image. At inference, we also warp the lighting input onto the face shape of a first pass, which reduces face-shape leakage through the lighting stream. Since face-recognition scores and human judgments each have limits, we evaluate identity with both, together with face-shape metrics on rendered faces of known shape. In a user study, raters judge MYSTIQUE's results more like the target than those of each compared method. Among these methods, MYSTIQUE also recovers the target's face shape most accurately, and its recognition score gains the most as more photos of the target are given.

Authors: Edoardo Giacomello, Sheida Rahnamai Kordasiabi, Mehdi Seifi, Florian Jug

Abstract: Semantic segmentation of electron microscopy (EM) volumes is a central step in quantitative cell biology, but dense voxel-wise annotation is prohibitively expensive. $\epsilon$-Seg showed that a hierarchical variational autoencoder trained on sparsely annotated voxels can yield competitive segmentations, but it classifies from its topmost latent level only and operates exclusively on 2D patches. cross ASTERISM: Do Multimodal LLMs Actually Look at All the Images?

Authors: Chanseul Cho, Dongwoo Kim, Noori Bae, Seungjae Moon, Seongsu Lee, Youngmin Ro

Abstract: Multi-image benchmarks aim to test whether a multimodal large language model (MLLM) combines evidence across images, but many of their items can be answered from a single image. Across about 33k items from 19 public benchmarks, our audit and a two-stage single-image filter find that one image or the question alone settles 24.2% of them. On these items, MLLMs recover from one image a median 78% of the accuracy that all images add over the text alone, so these items reward answering from one image, which we call the single-image shortcut. MLLMs take this shortcut even on items where one image is not enough. On such items, most of their wrong answers repeat an answer they give from one image, at a rate well above chance. We build ASTERISM, to our knowledge the largest audited multi-image benchmark, from these 19 benchmarks by keeping their items that need more than one image. Its 18.7k items, 2.0k of them corrected or rewritten, share one format, one scorer and seven task categories. We also release ASTERISM-Train with 49k training items and ASTERISM-Lite, a hard subset whose items we manually verify one by one. On ASTERISM-Lite, the strongest proprietary MLLM scores 68%. Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), now common in MLLM post-training, rewards the shortcut as well, because a correct answer read off one image earns as much reward as one that combines images. In line with this, existing RLVR objectives add little to the ASTERISM score. To stop rewarding the shortcut in training as ASTERISM does in evaluation, we propose cross-image credit assignment (CICA), an extension of RLVR that moves the credit toward the tokens that need more than one image. On three open-source MLLMs, CICA raises the ASTERISM score by 2.6 to 6.0 points, more than twice the gain of each general and vision-specific RL objective trained on the same data.

Abstract: Clustering longitudinal health trajectories requires handling high-dimensional measurements, irregular visit times, and subject-level heterogeneity while retaining clinically interpretable structure. cross Breaking Routing And Inter-device Dependencies in Mixture-of-Experts

Authors: John Zarka (MLIA), Danyal Ahmed (MLIA), Eugene Belilovsky (DIRO, MILA), Edouard Oyallon (MLIA)

Abstract: As Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models scale, Expert Parallelism (EP) becomes increasingly important for distributing experts across devices. However, the resulting token dispatch and combine operations can become a major bottleneck during inference. Overlapping these communication phases with computation is constrained by the sequential dependencies of standard MoE architectures. We introduce BRAID-MoE, an architecture that pairs laddered residual connections with early routing to relax these dependencies. Laddered residuals enable each expert combine to be deferred to the following block, while early routing computes the next routing decision from the pre-combine residual, with experts still receiving the updated post-combine hidden state. This creates an extended communication window in which the preceding combine and next dispatch can execute back-to-back on a dedicated communication stream, overlapping attention computation. We further demonstrate that existing MoE checkpoints can be adapted to BRAID with limited additional training, largely recovering their original accuracy. On a converted Qwen3-30B-A3B model, BRAID achieves speedups of up to 1.29$\times$ in prefill and 1.37$\times$ in decode, and at least 1.12$\times$ across diverse workloads and EP configurations on one or two four-H100 nodes. It outperforms competing overlap strategies in all tested settings, with the strongest gains when communication is a major latency bottleneck and its duration approaches the available computation window. These results demonstrate that early routing enables more effective communication-computation overlap in MoE inference at a modest cost in model quality.

Abstract: Online Machine Learning (ML) enables real-time decision-making on evolving data streams. cross Quantum Community Detection for Data Analysis

Authors: Vicente P. Soloviev, Brian Coyle, Stefano Scali, Michal Krompiec

Abstract: Community detection offers an alternative to conventional methods for data analysis tasks such as feature selection and clustering, and detecting communities with quantum algorithms is a promising avenue for quantum speedup. cross NCPU: Towards Cell-Inspired Spatial Computing

Authors: Iliya Zhechev, Piotr Walas

Abstract: Simple cellular automata (CAs) can be computationally universal, but their rules are designed by hand. We train Neural Cellular Automata (NCAs), which learn a local update rule by gradient descent, to compute exact digital logic. Bits are encoded as clusters of cells on a 2D grid, and a result reaches the output only by propagating through intermediate cells.
