cross ReQOR: Operator-Discrepancy Subspace Repair for Quantization-Conditioned Backdoors in Large Language Models

Authors: Wonjune Seo, Minkyung Park, Saerom Park

Abstract: Quantization-Conditioned Backdoors (QCBs) cause large language models (LLMs) to exhibit malicious behavior after quantization while remaining benign at high precision. Existing defenses often rely on security-guided configuration selection or iterative optimization, but do not identify which quantization-induced changes should be repaired. We isolate module-local quantization effects from upstream drift by evaluating the high-precision and quantized versions of each module on identical states from the quantized inference trajectory, and formulate QCB defense as repairing the resulting same-state operator discrepancies. We propose ReQOR, a training-free defense that identifies the dominant directions of these discrepancies on benign calibration data and restores the full-precision computation along them in closed form, yielding a factorized residual adapter. ReQOR uses neither attack information nor gradient-based optimization and supports both full-span and low-rank repair. Experiments across four LLMs, three PGD-based QCB attacks, three malicious behaviors, and three quantization formats show substantial QCB mitigation while largely preserving benign utility. In additional experiments on a non-PGD outlier-injection attack, existing baselines largely fail, whereas ReQOR remains effective. Further analysis shows that dominant operator-discrepancy directions provide more effective and rank-efficient repair than rank-matched random, low-energy, or raw weight-error directions, while low-rank ReQOR reduces deployment memory overhead.

cross NABLA: A Noise-Adaptive Blockwise Left-Right Preconditioning Algorithm for Stochastic Optimization

Authors: Yanlin Li, Ganzhao Yuan

Abstract: We propose NABLA, a noise-adaptive blockwise left-right preconditioning algorithm for mini-batch stochastic nonconvex optimization. NABLA combines an exponential moving average of stochastic gradients with block-diagonal row and column preconditioners constructed from matrix-valued second-moment statistics. Unlike conventional matrix-preconditioned methods that use fixed decay parameters, NABLA adjusts the second-moment averaging coefficient through a closed-loop feedback rule determined by the cumulative stochastic-gradient energy and its running average. The first-moment coefficient remains fixed, allowing the trajectory-dependent adaptation to act directly on the structured preconditioners. The resulting method is horizon-free, requires no prior knowledge of the noise level, and automatically adjusts the memory of its second-moment estimators to the observed optimization dynamics. Under standard lower boundedness, smoothness, unbiasedness, bounded-variance, and spectral-boundedness assumptions, we establish uniform stability and variation bounds for the time-varying block preconditioners and control their interaction with momentum estimation and parameter updates. These results yield the noise-adaptive stationarity guarantee $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(T^{-1/2}+\sigma^{1/2}T^{-1/4})$, which automatically reduces to the nearly deterministic $\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(T^{-1/2})$ rate when the stochastic-gradient noise vanishes. NABLA therefore provides a principled approach to combining closed-loop trajectory feedback with memory-efficient structured matrix preconditioning.

cross Simulator-as-Proxy World Model for Robot Manipulation

Authors: Tingting Liao, Mingjia Huo, Hao Li, Zhengzhong Liu, Eric P. Xing, Guangyi Liu

Abstract: Video world models for robots are increasingly conditioned on a simulated or rendered proxy of the scene, on the assumption that the video will show what the proxy shows. Testing this assumption requires a proxy whose state is explicit and can be perturbed. We present SimPWM (Simulator-as-Proxy World Model): given a calibrated camera, it represents, from the first frame alone, the robot by its 3D model and the objects the gripper may touch as 3D points; a rule-based simulator with no trained parameters moves them under the actions, and a video model renders the result. SimPWM returns the realistic video together with the simulated state. We then freeze the simulated objects while the arm moves as recorded and check whether the video still shows the object lifted. On DROID episodes from unseen setups, the answer differs for the arm and for the objects. With each of three base video models, the arm follows the proxy (robot IoU 0.75-0.78, against at most 0.69 for end-to-end models); rendering the robot alone already gives this. What the simulated objects add is the object state: against a robot-only proxy, they cut missed pick-ups from 54% to 38%, and the video follows them only with some base models: on rigid objects, freezing them lowers the lift rate of MiniMax-H3, fine-tuned on episodes whose simulation agrees with the real video, from 42% to 10%, but that of LTX-2.3, trained on the same episodes, only from 57% to 49%, as if it inferred the grasp from the arm motion; more than half of the remaining lifts of MiniMax-H3 are of cloth and rope, which the simulator carries rigidly. With any base model, the simulated state records what the simulation did; under perturbed actions, a video that disagrees with it reveals that the video ignores the simulated state.

cross Data-driven Reduced-order Modeling of a Multistable Viscoelastic Flow

Authors: Shaoxuan Chen, Su Yang, Simon J. Haward, Amy Q. Shen, Panayotis G. Kevrekidis

Abstract: Viscoelastic flow past two side-by-side microcylinders has been shown to exhibit geometry-dependent transitions among symmetric, converging, diverging, and asymmetric-diverging flow states, together with regions of bistability and tristability. Here, we develop a data-driven framework for learning a two-dimensional, parameter-dependent normal form for this bifurcation structure from sparse measurements of stable flow states. We use the flow asymmetry parameters $I'$ and $I''$ as state variables, with the gap ratio $G$ and Weissenberg number $Wi$ as control parameters. In the absence of trajectory data, the measured steady states are enforced as stable equilibria of the learned vector field through equilibrium and local-stability constraints. We examine both polynomial and neural-network representations of the associated normal form and incorporate the physical reflection symmetry directly into the model. The resulting models recover the main geometry-dependent transitions and the overall structure of the experimental phase diagram. To reduce additional stable equilibria that are not constrained by the measurements, we introduce an iterative negative-sample procedure that refines the predicted equilibrium structure. Combining equilibrium data, local stability, physical symmetry, and negative samples yields the closest agreement with the experimental phase diagram among the models considered. The remaining discrepancies reflect the limited information available, solely in the form of sparse equilibrium measurements. Overall, the framework aims to showcase a prototypical example of both the potential and the limitations of inferring nonlinear flow dynamics from sparse equilibrium measurements and highlight the importance of physical constraints and additional dynamical information for reconstructing complex bifurcation structures.

cross Path-probability gradients for optimizing force fields against dynamical observables

Authors: Zhongyao Wang, Lun Song, Yakun Zeng, Xingao Gong, Rui Shi, Mao Su

Abstract: Machine learning force fields inherit the errors of the electronic-structure methods used to generate their training data, and refining them against experimental measurements provides a direct route beyond this limit. However, such refinement remains difficult for dynamical observables because differentiating chaotic molecular trajectories yields unstable gradients. Here, we introduce PathGrad, which differentiates Langevin path probabilities instead of the trajectories themselves. The resulting gradient estimator is accumulated on the fly from thermal noise and force derivatives, and its variance remains stable as the trajectory length increases. We validated the gradient estimator against finite-difference calculations and used it to fine-tune molten-copper and water potentials against experimental diffusion coefficients together with structural constraints. For water, jointly optimizing diffusion and the oxygen-oxygen radial distribution function also improves the predictions of shear viscosity and rotational relaxation, neither of which is included in the optimization objective. Alignment at a single temperature also improves the apparent activation energies of multiple dynamical observables, bringing their temperature dependences closer to experiments. Moreover, the optimized potential exhibits smaller force errors relative to an independent, higher-level quantum-chemical reference, indicating that alignment with experiments improves the fidelity of the potential energy surface beyond the fitted targets. PathGrad thus establishes a general framework for incorporating experimental observables directly into force field optimizations.

cross What Does a Skill Vocabulary Cost? A Closed-Loop Decomposition over Demonstrated Motions

Authors: Maximilian Xiling Li, Nils Blank, Paul Mattes, Pankhuri Vanjani, Rudolf Lioutikov

Abstract: Discrete skill policies execute motions drawn from a finite vocabulary. In most current methods, this vocabulary is a latent codebook learned jointly with a selector and a decoder. When such a policy fails in closed loop, the failure cannot be traced to the vocabulary, to the choice of skill or to its execution. We present Movement Prototype Quantization (MPQ), which places a library of demonstrated motions after a trained policy instead of inside it. MPQ replaces each predicted action chunk with its nearest library element, corrects that element toward the prediction within a bound, and trains no parameters. Across six architectures, including a 7B vision-language-action model, the wrapped policy is statistically equivalent to the original. A reference policy executed through the library is therefore a baseline for any selector over the same library. Substituting a selector's components one at a time attributes its shortfall to them. On LIBERO-90, with selectors on three backbones, quantization costs nothing and the choice of skill costs at most 2 points under the reference's placement and correction. The selectors' own placement and correction account for nearly all of the remaining gap, up to 12 points. cross Theory-Informed Quantum Error Mitigation for Shor Order Finding with Fourier Neural Operators

Authors: Stefano Markidis, Qingxin Yang, Erik {\AA}sgrim, Ivy Peng

Abstract: Quantum noise distorts the distribution of circuit measurement outcomes, often leaving enough information to infer the algorithm's correct result. To recover this latent information from the noisy distribution in Shor order finding, we introduce a theory-informed quantum error mitigation method. We model how circuit errors shift, broaden, or weaken peaks in the output distribution. These models guide both the generation of synthetic training data and the design of a Fourier neural operator with a learned gain that attenuates or amplifies higher retained Fourier modes. We score each candidate order by averaging the correlations between its ideal distribution and the distributions measured in three circuit executions. The network adjusts these scores to guide the order estimate and determine the weights of a normalized mixture of the ideal distributions. On 1,351 simulated test cases for general modular exponentiation, held out by circuit configuration, exact order recovery rises from 19% to 34%, and the median Jensen-Shannon divergence from the ideal distribution falls by 50%, from 0.64 to 0.32. On 101 Mersenne rotation circuits executed across five IBM quantum processors, a separate model trained and calibrated in simulation increases the number of correct order estimates from 35 to 53. Algorithmic structure can remain recoverable even when noise makes it barely visible.

cross Efficient Video Retrieval Augmented Generation via Token Allocation

Authors: Ayush Barik, Fan Lai, Francis Y. Yan

Abstract: Efficient long-video understanding requires identifying relevant evidence while minimizing the tokens processed by vision-language models (VLMs). Existing video retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) methods rely on frame selection and token compression, but overlook how much each additional token is worth. We introduce Lembas, a plug-and-play framework that formulates video RAG as a token allocation problem over frames and subtitles under a shared budget. Our key insight is that the value of a token diminishes within each item and decreases further as similar evidence receives more tokens. Lembas captures both effects in a single objective and solves it with an efficient iterative water-filling algorithm that has a provable convergence guarantee. For multi-turn conversations, Lembas reuses the tokens processed in earlier turns and refines relevance with the VLM's attention at no extra cost. Our evaluations show that Lembas improves accuracy by up to 7.2 percentage points over state-of-the-art baselines at comparable compute. On multi-turn video understanding, Lembas achieves up to 1.5 percentage points higher accuracy with 1.7-17.3x fewer TFLOPs.

Authors: Ali Turfah, Xiaoquan Wen

Abstract: Clustering is a foundational tool for exploratory data analysis across the sciences. We argue that clustering methods should be evaluated along four dimensions: fidelity to the data's underlying structure, interpretability of the recovered groups, stability under data perturbations and algorithmic randomness, and scalability to modern scientific datasets. cross Online V-Learning with Adaptive Longitudinal Data

Authors: Aniruddhan Ganesaraman, Michael R. Kosorok

Abstract: Reinforcement learning methods for estimating optimal dynamic treatment regimes are increasingly applied in mobile health (mHealth), where treatment decisions are made continually. Luckett et al. (2020) proposed V-learning, which estimates an optimal policy from offline trajectories. In practice, mHealth policies are updated online. Batch updating, in which the policy is re-estimated after each block of new data, is a tractable middle ground between offline learning and updating after every observation. However, because each batch's treatment policy is estimated from data pooled across patients in earlier batches, the resulting data are adaptively collected and patient trajectories are dependent, a setting that offline theory does not address. We develop statistical theory for V-learning in this setting with varying, deterministic batch sizes. For each fixed batch, as the number of patients grows, we establish uniform consistency of the estimated value function, consistency of the estimated policy, a functional central limit theorem over the policy class, asymptotic normality of the estimated value of the learned policy, and a consistent variance estimator. To first order, the online procedure performs as well as an oracle that deploys the optimal policy after the first batch. With the number of patients fixed and the number of batches growing, the estimated value function and policy remain consistent under a persistent identifiability condition. In simulations, as the number of patients grows with the number of batches fixed, confidence intervals attain near-nominal coverage under adaptive assignment and the online procedure closely matches the oracle. With a small cohort followed over many batches, the learned policies improve steadily.

cross Towards Realistic Evaluation of Uncertainty Quantification for LLM-Based Recommender Systems

Authors: Hao Wu, Sipeng Chen, Weikai Li, Tianyi Chen, Qiancheng Yang, Hang Zhou, Yizhou Sun, Wei Wang, Xiao Luo

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) provide a flexible interface for recommendation systems. However, their issued rankings require reliable instance-level uncertainty monitoring. In this scenario, comparing uncertainty quantification (UQ) methods becomes challenging because LLM rankers lack a standard item-classification head. Their scores may depend on option labels, prompts, generation procedures, and the available user and item evidence. Existing methods derive uncertainty from different sources and apply it at different stages of the recommendation process. Some methods evaluate an issued ranking, whereas others use uncertainty to change the ranking itself, making direct comparison difficult. Here we introduce a fixed-ranking benchmark which evaluates eight heterogeneous UQ signals as record-level monitors under a shared output contract. Second, ranking-aligned Top10Mass provides the strongest scalar Error@10 detection in several evaluation conditions, reflecting effective cutoff-aligned monitoring, while its advantage narrows for popularity-tail targets. Third, scalar UQ signals are weak at identifying which control has the larger clipped NDCG@10 difference, whereas source-specific signals, including Perturbation Rank Instability and simple listwise distances, provide stronger paired-control agreement, reflecting their sensitivity to the two controlled evidence sources. These findings motivate us to evaluate ranking-failure detection, robustness across recommendation conditions, and responses to controlled evidence changes as distinct properties of UQ.

Abstract: While Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as automated evaluators in evaluation and training pipelines, their judgements are often affected by systematic biases that conflict with human preferences. cross The Reliability Function of Error-Correcting Output Codes with Noisy Binary Classifiers

Authors: Gendo Kumoi, Hideki Yagi, Manabu Kobayashi, Shigeichi Hirasawa

Abstract: Error-correcting output coding (ECOC) builds a multi-class classifier from binary classifiers. We study the excess error rate of ECOC over the Bayes (MAP) classifier when each binary classifier outputs a posterior estimate whose log-odds is corrupted by Gaussian noise. Recasting decoding as a test over a Gaussian channel, we derive an upper bound from the union and Chernoff bounds and a lower bound from the nearest competing category and the Mills ratio. The bounds show that a large Hamming distance is both necessary and sufficient for reducing the excess error rate. The excess error rate stays bounded away from zero, by a constant that depends on the two largest posteriors and the noise level but not on the number of categories $M$, whenever the distance between the code words of the two most probable categories is bounded; for equidistant codes this distance is $d_{\min}$. Conversely, it is at most $M-1$ times a factor that is exponentially small in the minimum distance $d_{\min}$, and it vanishes whenever $\kappa\,d_{\min}-\ln(M-1)\to\infty$ for an explicit $\kappa$ (for general codes, under a posterior condition). For $n$-vs-rest codes with $n\geq2$, the upper and lower exponents coincide asymptotically, which determines the reliability function of ECOC in closed form. The results extend to correlated Gaussian noise with the Hamming distance replaced by an effective distance; when noise is shared at the level of categories, the effective distance stays bounded and the excess error rate does not vanish for any code. All theorems are machine-checked in Lean 4. In a semi-synthetic setting, the measured decay agrees with the predicted exponent. On ResNet-50 features of CIFAR-10/100 and ImageNet-1k, the excess error over a log-likelihood decoding reference decreases with $d_{\min}$ and, for larger $M$, levels off at a moderate distance, while codes with $d_{\min}=1$ degrade as $M$ grows.

cross iWAM: Efficient Action Prediction from World Models without Explicit Future Prediction

Authors: Vansh Kapoor, Max Sobol Mark, Sreyas Venkataraman, Max Simchowitz, Aviral Kumar

Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) build policies on pretrained video-generation models, on the premise that predicting how a scene evolves helps determine how to act. However, generating future scenes is expensive for real-time control. This motivates efficient proxies for future imagination, but how to train such proxies without sacrificing action-prediction quality remains unclear. An effective proxy need not generate a high-resolution frame; it only needs to provide enough information to distinguish between possible futures. This leads to the design principle behind iWAM: rather than denoising an entire future frame, can we condition the action policy on intermediate features from a single video-model evaluation? We show that the answer is yes. Exploiting the reversible dynamics of flow-matching models, we invert each training future into the corresponding latent noise vector. However, a single velocity evaluation at this latent does not necessarily yield informative features for action prediction. We therefore learn a bidirectional flow map that amortizes future-generation computation, enabling a single model evaluation to provide effective conditioning features. At inference, we sample a latent noise vector from the prior and condition the policy using just one video-backbone forward pass. Across simulated benchmarks and real robots, iWAM recovers the generalization benefits of WAMs while substantially reducing its inference cost.

Abstract: The development of biomedical vision-language models (VLMs) with contrastive image-text pretraining has been driven by scaling to increasingly large and diverse image-text datasets. replace Solving Attention Kernel Regression Problem via Pre-conditioner

Authors: Zhao Song, Junze Yin, Lichen Zhang

Abstract: The attention mechanism is the key to large language models, and the attention matrix serves as an algorithmic and computational bottleneck for such a scheme. In this paper, we define two problems, motivated by designing fast algorithms for proxy of attention matrix and solving regressions against them. Given an input matrix $A\in \mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$ and a response vector $b$, the first proxy, for $n\gg d$, uses powers of the matrix $A^\top A$, and we consider two types of regression problems: $\min_{x\in \mathbb{R}^d}\|(A^\top A)^jx-b\|_2$ and $\min_{x\in \mathbb{R}^d}\|A(A^\top A)^jx-b\|_2$ for any positive integer $j$. We design fast algorithms for these power regressions, based on sketching and preconditioning. The second proxy is applying exponential entrywise to the Gram matrix, denoted by $\exp(AA^\top)$ and solving the regression $\min_{x\in \mathbb{R}^n}\|\exp(AA^\top)x-b \|_2$. We call this problem the attention kernel regression problem, as the matrix $\exp(AA^\top)$ could be viewed as a kernel function with respect to $A$. For this problem, we show that the sketching and preconditioning approach known for Gaussian kernels gives error $\epsilon\|b\|_2$ for every $b$, with a sketch dimension that is, up to logarithmic factors, $\epsilon^{-2}n$ times the condition number of the kernel matrix, which is at least $n/e$ when the rows of $A$ have norm at most $1$. For rows of norm at most $1$, we also give an algorithm whose sketch dimension, up to logarithmic factors, does not depend on $n$. It sketches the kernel plus a ridge term, and for a given $K\geq 1$ its error is at most $\epsilon\|b\|_2$ plus $(1+\sqrt{\epsilon})$ times the best residual among solutions of norm at most $(K/n)\|b\|_2$. We hope these efforts will provide an alternative perspective of studying efficient approximation of attention matrices.

replace ProFL: Performative Robust Optimal Federated Learning

Authors: Xue Zheng, Tian Xie, Xuwei Tan, Xueru Zhang, Aylin Yener

Abstract: Performative prediction is a framework that captures distribution shifts that occur during the training of machine learning models due to their deployment. As the trained model is used, the induced data distribution evolves, leading to deviations from the original data distribution. The impact of such model-induced distribution shifts in federated learning (FL) is increasingly likely to transpire in real-life use cases. A recently proposed approach extends performative prediction to FL with the resulting model converging to a performative stable point, which may be far from the performative optimal point. Earlier research in centralized settings has shown that the performative optimal point can be achieved under model-induced distribution shifts, but these approaches require the performative risk to be convex and the training data to be noiseless. These assumptions are often violated in realistic FL systems. This paper addresses these limitations and proposes Performative Robust Optimal Federated Learning (ProFL), an algorithm that targets the performative optimal point in FL while mitigating the effects of noisy and contaminated local data. We present a neighborhood-convergence analysis under the Polyak--Lojasiewicz condition, which applies to non-convex objectives. Extensive experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate the advantage of Robust Optimal Federated Learning over the state-of-the-art.

replace Adaptive Linear Networks for Irregular Multivariate Time Series Forecasting in Big Data Systems

Authors: Chengsen Wang, Qi Qi, Bo He, Haifeng Sun, Zirui Zhuang, Jingyu Wang

Abstract: Large-scale sensing, clinical, transportation, energy, and climate systems often produce irregular multivariate time series with sparse observations, heterogeneous sampling intervals, and asynchronous variables. Forecasting at externally supplied future query coordinates requires a model to accommodate variable observation lengths and time locations while retaining efficient variable interaction. We present AiT (Adaptive inverted Transformer), whose ALinear (Adaptive Linear) module generates sample-adaptive temporal weights from observation or query coordinates. More precisely, ALinear is time-conditioned attention pooling without value projection. Available observation or query coordinates generate the corresponding keys or queries, the fixed-shape counterpart may use learned default slots, and the unprojected inputs serve as values. The name "Linear" therefore denotes only the final weighted-sum output form and not equivalence to an unrestricted affine layer or to DLinear. AiT uses ALinear to encode variable-length histories and decode query-conditioned forecasts, and applies Transformer blocks over the resulting variable tokens to model cross-variable relationships. The evaluation on four public irregular forecasting datasets examines prediction accuracy and computational efficiency, while controlled protocols are specified for temporal-design, scalability, missingness, and statistical analyses.

replace Impact of Data Sparsity on Machine Learning for Fault Detection in Power System Protection

Authors: Julian Oelhaf, Georg Kordowich, Changhun Kim, Paula Andrea P\'erez-Toro, Andreas Maier, Johann J\"ager, Siming Bayer

Abstract: Germany's transition to a renewable energy-based power system is reshaping grid operations, requiring advanced monitoring and control to manage decentralized generation. Machine learning (ML) has emerged as a powerful tool for power system protection, particularly for fault detection (FD) and fault line identification (FLI) in transmission grids. However, ML model reliability depends on data quality and availability. Data sparsity resulting from sensor failures, communication disruptions, or reduced sampling rates poses a challenge to ML-based FD and FLI. Yet, its impact has not been systematically validated prior to this work. In response, we propose a framework to assess the impact of data sparsity on ML-based FD and FLI performance. We simulate realistic data sparsity scenarios, evaluate their impact, derive quantitative insights, and demonstrate the effectiveness of this evaluation strategy by applying it to an existing ML-based framework. After 50-fold decimation, the model achieves an F1-score of 0.999 $\pm$ 0.000 for FD. For FLI, removing current measurements reduces the F1-score by 55.61%, and the Bus 2 loss scenario reduces it by 9.73%. These results quantify the sensitivity of the two tasks to the evaluated input restrictions.

replace Text-to-Image Prompt Optimization for Unseen Rewards

Authors: Semin Kim, Yeonwoo Cha, Jaehoon Yoo, Seunghoon Hong

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) prompt optimization rewrites user prompts to improve the quality of generated images under a target reward. Existing methods typically assume a known reward, relying on reward-specific training or handcrafted instructions. We instead study prompt optimization for unseen rewards, which are unknown at design time and accessible only through scores at test time. We observe that removing reward-specific guidance substantially reduces the search efficiency of LLM-based optimizers, even though their optimization histories contain evidence of what the reward favors. To address this challenge, we introduce RATTPO (Reward-Adaptive Test-Time Prompt Optimization), a reward-generic method that turns this implicit evidence into explicit search guidance. At each round, RATTPO contrasts high- and low-scoring prompts to infer competing hypotheses about promising rewriting directions, then generates and evaluates candidates under each hypothesis. Across four reward families, RATTPO consistently outperforms reward-generic baselines at equal budgets and achieves performance comparable to reward-tailored optimizers without any reward-specific adjustment.

replace On the notion of missingness for path attribution explainability methods in medical settings: Guiding the selection of medically meaningful baselines

Authors: Alexander Geiger, Lars Wagner, Daniel Rueckert, Dirk Wilhelm, Alissa Jell

Abstract: The explainability of deep learning models remains a significant challenge, particularly in the medical domain where interpretable outputs are essential for clinical trust and transparency. Path attribution methods such as Integrated Gradients (IG) rely on a baseline that represents the absence of informative features, a notion commonly referred to as missingness. However, commonly used baselines (such as all-zero inputs) are often semantically meaningless in medical contexts, where intensity values carry clinical significance. In this work, we revisit the notion of missingness for medical imaging, expose the limitations of standard baselines in this setting, and formalize a stricter missingness we term semantic missingness: a baseline must not merely lack signal, but must represent a clinically plausible state in which the disease-related features are absent. This formulation motivates a counterfactual-guided approach to baseline selection, in which a synthetically generated counterfactual (i.e. a clinically normal variant of the pathological input) serves as a principled and semantically meaningful reference. We derive theoretical guarantees showing that counterfactual baselines yield more faithful attributions than standard alternatives, and empirically validate this with two complementary counterfactual generative models, a VAE and a diffusion model, though the concept is model-agnostic and compatible with any suitable counterfactual method. Our experiments show that counterfactual baselines produce consistently more faithful and medically relevant attributions across three diverse medical datasets, outperforming both standard baseline choices and other related methods. Lastly, we also compare against using the counterfactual directly as an explanation and show that employing it as a baseline for IG yields superior results, thereby bridging two separate explainability paradigms.

replace One-Shot Clustering for Federated Learning Under Clustering-Agnostic Assumption

Authors: Maciej Krzysztof Zuziak, Roberto Pellungrini, Salvatore Rinzivillo

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) is a widespread and well-adopted paradigm of decentralised learning that allows training one model from multiple sources without the need to transfer data between participating clients directly. Since its inception in 2016, it has been divided into numerous subfields that deal with application-specific issues, such as data heterogeneity or resource allocation. One such sub-field, Clustered Federated Learning (CFL), is dealing with the problem of clustering the population of clients into separate cohorts to deliver personalised models. Although a few remarkable works have been published in this domain, the problem remains largely unexplored, as its basic assumptions and settings differ slightly from those of standard FL. In this work, we present One-Shot Clustered Federated Learning (OCFL), a clustering-agnostic algorithm that can automatically detect the earliest suitable moment for clustering. We empirically evaluate our methodology by testing various one-shot clustering algorithms for over forty different tasks on five benchmark datasets. Our experiments showcase the good performance of our approach when used to perform CFL in an automated manner without the need to adjust hyperparameters. We also revisit the practical feasibility of CFL algorithms based on the gradients or weights of the clients, providing firm evidence of the high efficiency of density-based clustering methods when used to differentiate between the loss surfaces of neural networks trained on different distributions. Moreover, by inspecting the feasibility of local explanations generated with the help of GradCAM, we can provide more insights into the relationship between personalisation and the explainability of local predictions.

replace Complexity-Regularized Proximal Policy Optimization

Authors: Luca Serfilippi, Giorgio Franceschelli, Antonio Corradi, Mirco Musolesi

Abstract: Policy gradient methods usually rely on entropy regularization to prevent premature convergence. However, maximizing entropy indiscriminately pushes the policy towards a uniform distribution, often overriding the reward signal if not optimally tuned. We propose replacing the standard entropy term with a self-regulating complexity term, defined as the product of Shannon entropy and disequilibrium, which quantifies the distance from the uniform distribution. Unlike pure entropy, which favors maximal disorder, this complexity measure is zero for both fully deterministic and perfectly uniform distributions, i.e., it is strictly positive for systems that exhibit a meaningful interplay between order and randomness. These properties ensure the policy maintains beneficial stochasticity while reducing regularization pressure when the policy is highly uncertain, allowing learning to focus on reward optimization. We introduce Complexity-Regularized Proximal Policy Optimization (CR-PPO), a modification of PPO that leverages this dynamic. We empirically demonstrate that CR-PPO is significantly more robust to hyperparameter selection than entropy-regularized PPO, achieving consistent performance across orders of magnitude of regularization coefficients and remaining harmless when regularization is unnecessary, thereby reducing the need for expensive hyperparameter tuning.

replace Nowcast3D for effective precipitation nowcasting

Authors: Huaguan Chen, Wei Han, Haofei Sun, Ning Lin, Xingtao Song, Yunfan Yang, Jie Tian, Yang Liu, Ji-Rong Wen, Xiaoye Zhang, Xueshun Shen, Hao Sun

Abstract: The evolution of extreme precipitation depends on height-varying wind fields and coupled physical processes, underscoring the importance of three-dimensional information for precipitation nowcasting. Radar volume scans provide such an observational basis. Most learning-based systems, however, either compress these scans into two-dimensional products, losing vertical information needed for height-dependent storm evolution, or rely on purely data-driven mappings that inadequately represent the physical evolution of extreme precipitation. We introduce Nowcast3D, a gray-box learning framework for 3D radar-volume nowcasting, which integrates physically structured neural operators with a conditional diffusion model. The neural operators produce a physics-guided forecast via 3D advection, local diffusion and microphysics processes. Conditioned on this forecast, the diffusion model generates uncertainty-quantified ensembles. Trained on provincial-scale radar volumes covering a $10.24^\circ \times 10.24^\circ$ region and fine-tuned on a $2.56^\circ \times 2.56^\circ$ city-scale region ($0.01^\circ \approx 1$ km), Nowcast3D produces effective near-real-time reflectivity forecasts up to 3 hours ahead. It consistently outperforms competitive baselines in both cross-region and temporal-out-of-sample evaluations, and its inferred motion fields show good consistency with collocated wind-profiler observations. In a blind evaluation with 160 meteorologists, Nowcast3D ranked first and was preferred in 57% of post-hoc assessments, compared with 27% for the leading baseline. These results highlight its operational potential for effective nowcasting of extreme precipitation events.

replace Hierarchical Deep Research for Physically Grounded System-Level Materials Design

Authors: Rui Ding, Rodrigo Pires Ferreira, Yuxin Chen, Junhong Chen

Abstract: Designing multicomponent materials systems requires evidence on composition, interfaces, processing and deviceoperation that is scattered across the literature. Authors: Julian Oelhaf, Mehran Pashaei, Georg Kordowich, Christian Bergler, Andreas Maier, Johann J\"ager, Siming Bayer

Abstract: The growing penetration of renewable and distributed generation is transforming power systems and challenging conventional protection schemes that rely on fixed settings and local measurements. Machine learning (ML) offers a data-driven alternative for centralized fault classification (FC) and fault localization (FL), enabling faster and more adaptive decision-making. However, practical deployment critically depends on robustness. Protection algorithms must remain reliable even when confronted with missing, noisy, or degraded sensor data. This work introduces a unified framework for systematically evaluating the robustness of ML models in power system protection. High-fidelity EMT simulations are used to model realistic degradation scenarios, including sensor outages, reduced sampling rates, and transient communication losses. replace Energy-Guided Generative Modeling for Low-Energy Molecular Structure Discovery

Authors: Guikun Xu, Xiaohan Yi, Ziqiao Meng, Peilin Zhao, Yatao Bian

Abstract: Exploring molecular energy landscapes and identifying ground-state conformations are central challenges in computational chemistry. However, generating diverse low-energy conformers from molecular graphs remains expensive with traditional physics-based pipelines. Existing learning-based approaches remain fragmented: generative models capture conformational diversity but often lack reliable energy calibration, whereas deterministic predictors focus on a single structure and fail to represent ensemble variability. Here we introduce EnFlow, to our knowledge, the first energy-guided generative framework that couples flow-based conformer generation with explicit energy landscape modeling for joint conformational ensemble generation and ground-state identification. By integrating generative dynamics with a learned energy model, EnFlow guides sampling toward low-energy regions of the conformational landscape, improving structural fidelity under extremely few sampling steps while enabling energy-based ranking of generated conformations. Experiments on GEOM-QM9 and GEOM-Drugs show that EnFlow achieves strong performance in conformer generation and ground-state identification while requiring only 1-2 ordinary differential equation (ODE) sampling steps. Single-point GFN2-xTB evaluations further show that the learned energy scores preserve physically meaningful energetic rankings of generated conformations. These results support explicit energy landscape modeling as an effective strategy for low-energy molecular structure discovery through joint modeling of conformational ensembles and their associated energies.

replace Generative Modeling via Drifting

Authors: Mingyang Deng, He Li, Tianhong Li, Yilun Du, Kaiming He

Abstract: Generative modeling can be formulated as learning a mapping f such that its pushforward distribution matches the data distribution. The pushforward behavior can be carried out iteratively at inference time, e.g., in diffusion/flow-based models. In this paper, we propose Drifting Models, a new generative modeling paradigm that evolves the pushforward distribution during training and naturally admits one-step inference. We introduce a drifting field that governs the sample movement and achieves equilibrium when the distributions match. This leads to a training objective that allows the neural network optimizer to evolve the distribution. In experiments, our one-step generator achieves state-of-the-art one-step results on ImageNet 256x256, with FID 1.54 in latent space and 1.61 in pixel space. We hope that our work opens up new opportunities for high-quality one-step generation.

replace A Fast and Accurate Fourier Neural Operator-Based Surrogate for Melt-Pool Prediction in Laser Processing

Authors: Alix Benoit (Empa), Toni Ivas (Empa), Mateusz Papierz (Terra Quantum AG), Asel Sagingalieva (Terra Quantum AG), Alexey Melnikov (Terra Quantum AG), Elia Iseli (Empa)

Abstract: High-fidelity simulations of laser welding capture complex thermo-fluid phenomena, including phase change, free-surface deformation, and keyhole dynamics, but their computational cost limits large-scale process exploration and real-time use. We present the Laser Processing Fourier Neural Operator (LP-FNO), a Fourier Neural Operator (FNO)-based surrogate model that learns the parametric solution operator of laser processing from multiphysics simulations generated with FLOW-3D WELD. Through a reformulation of the transient problem in a reference frame moving with the laser and the application of temporal averaging, the system is recast into a quasi-steady setting suitable for operator learning, even in the stable keyhole welding regime. LP-FNO maps process parameters to three-dimensional temperature fields and melt-pool boundaries across a broad process window spanning conduction and keyhole regimes using the normalized enthalpy formulation. The model achieves an average temperature relative error of approximately 2.5% and intersection-over-union scores for melt-pool segmentation above 0.9. Compared with a fully connected coordinate network, U-Net, and DeepONet, LP-FNO provides the strongest overall accuracy-efficiency trade-off, while retaining the resolution-invariant evaluation capability characteristic of FNOs. We demonstrate that an LP-FNO model trained on coarse-resolution data can be evaluated on finer grids, yielding accurate super-resolved predictions in mesh-converged conduction regimes, whereas discrepancies in keyhole regimes reflect unresolved dynamics in the coarse-mesh training data. LP-FNO thus provides an efficient surrogate modeling framework for laser welding, predicting full three-dimensional fields and phase interfaces over wide parameter ranges in just tens of milliseconds, up to a hundred thousand times faster than finite-volume multiphysics simulations.

Authors: Alexander Fertig, Karthikeyan Chandra Sekaran, Lakshman Balasubramanian, Michael Botsch

Abstract: As autonomous vehicles are rolled out, measures must be taken to ensure their safe operation. replace MUSE: Multi-Tenant Model Serving With Seamless Model Updates

Authors: Cl\'audio Correia, Alberto E. A. Ferreira, Lucas Martins, Miguel P. Bento, Sofia Guerreiro, Ricardo Ribeiro Pereira, Ana Sofia Gomes, Jacopo Bono, Hugo Ferreira, Pedro Bizarro

Abstract: In binary classification systems, decision thresholds translate model scores into actions. Choosing suitable thresholds relies on the specific distribution of the underlying model scores but also on the specific business decisions of each client using that model. However, retraining models inevitably shifts score distributions, invalidating existing thresholds. In multi-tenant Score-as-a-Service environments, where decision boundaries reside in client-managed infrastructure, this creates a severe bottleneck: recalibration requires coordinating threshold updates across hundreds of clients, consuming excessive human hours and leading to model stagnation. We introduce MUSE, a model serving framework that enables seamless model updates by decoupling model scores from client decision boundaries. Designed for multi-tenancy, MUSE optimizes infrastructure re-use by sharing models via dynamic intent-based routing, combined with a two-level score transformation that maps model outputs to a stable, reference distribution. Deployed at scale by Feedzai, MUSE processes over 4 thousand events per second, and over 55 billion events in the last 12 months, across several dozens of tenants, while maintaining high-availability and low-latency guarantees. By reducing model lead time from weeks to minutes, MUSE promotes model resilience against shifting attacks, saving millions of dollars in fraud losses and operational costs.

replace VI-CuRL: Stabilizing Verifier-Independent Reinforcement Learning Reasoning via Confidence-Guided Variance Reduction

Authors: Xin-Qiang Cai, Masashi Sugiyama

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a dominant paradigm for enhancing Large Language Models (LLMs) reasoning, yet its reliance on external verifiers limits its scalability. Recent findings suggest that RLVR primarily functions by eliciting latent capabilities, motivating the development of verifier-free algorithms. However, in such settings, standard methods like Group Relative Policy Optimization face a critical challenge: destructive gradient variance that often leads to training collapse. To address this issue, we introduce Verifier-Independent Curriculum Reinforcement Learning (VI-CuRL), a framework that leverages the model's intrinsic confidence to construct a curriculum independent from external verifiers. By prioritizing high-confidence samples, VI-CuRL effectively manages the bias-variance trade-off, specifically targeting the reduction of action and problem variance. Empirically, VI-CuRL promotes stability and consistently outperforms verifier-dependent/independent baselines across math and general reasoning benchmarks with/without verifiers.

replace Unbiased Approximate Vector-Jacobian Products for Efficient Backpropagation

Abstract: In this work we introduce methods to reduce the computational and memory costs of training deep neural networks. Our approach consists in replacing exact vector-jacobian products by randomized, unbiased approximations thereof during backpropagation. We provide a theoretical analysis of the trade-off between the number of epochs needed to achieve a target precision and the cost reduction for each epoch. replace Coverage Guarantees for Pseudo-Calibrated Conformal Prediction under Distribution Shift

Abstract: Conformal prediction (CP) provides distribution-free marginal coverage under exchangeability, but coverage can fail under distribution shift. replace Fail-Closed Alignment for Large Language Models

Authors: Zachary Coalson, Sanghyun Hong

Abstract: We identify a structural weakness in current large language model (LLM) alignment: refusal mechanisms in these models are fail-open. While existing approaches tend to encode refusal behaviors across multiple latent features, suppressing a single feature (via prompt-based jailbreaks) is sufficient to collapse alignment, leading to unsafe generation. Motivated by this, we propose fail-closed alignment as a design principle for robust LLM safety: refusal mechanisms should remain effective even under partial failures via redundant, independent causal pathways. We present a concrete instantiation of this principle: a progressive alignment framework that iteratively identifies and ablates previously learned refusal directions, forcing the model to reconstruct safety along new, independent subspaces. Across six jailbreak attacks, our method achieves the lowest observed average attack success rate (1.6%) while largely preserving generation quality and preventing excessive over-refusals, with negligible computational overhead. Additional analyses confirm that models trained with our method encode multiple, causally independent refusal directions that prompt-based jailbreaks cannot fully suppress, providing empirical support for fail-closed alignment as a principled foundation for robust LLM safety. Our code for reproducing the results is available at https://github.com/Trustworthy-and-Responsible-AI-Lab/Fail-Closed-Alignment
