replace Sparse Autoencoder Attribution Graphs Are Decided at the Margin

Authors: Karl Li

Abstract: Interpretability research writes a language model's activation as a weighted sum of learned directions, called features. A sparse autoencoder (SAE) learns the features and gives each a coefficient, its weight in that sum. The SAE's encoder first gives every feature a score. A TopK SAE selects the k features with the largest positive scores and zeroes the rest. A feature's margin is how far its score must move before the selection changes. An intervention on one feature's coefficient changes the encoder scores of the SAE at the next layer. An attribution graph predicts that change from the scores' derivatives with respect to the coefficient, taken before the intervention. The graph keeps the original selection, so it misses every feature that enters or leaves. We propose the margin graph, which adds the predicted change to every score and selects again. When one feature is zeroed in a public pair of Pythia-70M SAEs, the median error of the predicted coefficient change, relative to the true change, falls from 0.739 for the attribution graph to 0.121 for the margin graph. Written back into the model, the margin graph's coefficients predict the change in the model's output on subject verb agreement less accurately than the attribution graph's. Selecting again better predicts which features change, but on the task and pair we tested, not the model's output.

replace Lightweight Representation Learning for Low Tucker Rank Tensors

Authors: Hiroki Hasegawa, Yukihiko Okada

Abstract: Iterative estimators for low Tucker rank tensors, such as HOOI, nuclear-norm proximal and ADMM methods, Frank-Wolfe and scaled gradient descent, form large objects in every iteration: unfoldings, Kronecker operators, full SVDs and ambient iterates. The update uses little of these objects, yet they make memory the limiting resource on large tensors and on edge-sized devices. Remedies for each of these costs are known. However, it is unclear when a rewriting keeps the iterates unchanged, and where it pays off against efficient implementations. We therefore present a trajectory-preserving lightweight evaluation. First, we state exactness as an intertwining relation between the stored state and the reference iteration. Second, we show that three known components satisfy this relation for five estimators: computing only the singular triplets that an update needs, contracting without Kronecker products, and storing sparse-plus-factored states. As a result, the iterates and all guarantees of the reference estimator stay the same. On an A100 GPU, with the input data counted in the memory, lightweight HOOI needs the least memory of 15 HOOI-type methods at every size ($95\times$ less beyond the input than the lightest exact implementation on $800^3$ tensors), and it is the fastest method that reaches the HOOI estimate on the largest tensors. Against efficient public code, ours is faster and lighter for three of five estimators. On a surveillance video and a satellite hyperspectral cube, ours is the only implementation that fits a 1 GiB and a 4 GiB device. Finally, we show that the memory and time gains need separate conditions, and we identify where these conditions fail.

replace Thought-deciphering via EEG-to-text alignment

Authors: Xiaopeng Mao, Morten M{\o}rup, Xin Xu, Sang Wan Lee, Sadasivan Puthusserypady

Abstract: Non-invasive brain-to-text interfaces offer alternative communication pathways for people with communication disabilities. However, sentence-level decoding from electroencephalography (EEG) remains challenging. We propose a hierarchical EEG-text alignment framework that retrieves previously unseen sentences by progressively decoding coarse-grained semantic information and fine-grained sentence representations. We hypothesize that broader semantic context can be more reliably captured from EEG than individual sentences. Accordingly, EEG representations are first classified into semantic classes, which are defined as groups of related sentences, followed by sentence retrieval within the predicted class. Among over 2,000 unique text samples, our approach achieves, on unseen sentences, average top-5 and top-10 retrieval accuracies of 62.6\% $\pm$ 1.0\% and 83.5\% $\pm$ 1.0\%, respectively, compared with 0.3\% $\pm$ 0.3\% and 0.5\% $\pm$ 0.2\% for direct EEG-sentence alignment without semantic classification. Qualitative analysis further reveals that decoding performance depends strongly on textual context. These findings demonstrate the potential of hierarchical semantic alignment for advancing sentence-level EEG-to-text decoding.

replace Alleviating Limited Effectiveness and Multi-Class Collapse in Deep Graph-level Clustering

Authors: Xiaoyang Lin, Renchi Yang, Yuqi Chen, Xin Huang

Abstract: Given a collection of $N$ graphs, graph-level clustering (GLC) seeks to partition the $N$ graphs into semantically meaningful groups, which serves as a fundamental task in analyzing graph-structured data. While Graph Isomorphism Networks (GINs) have become the de facto encoders for GLC, they still fail to distinguish graphs due to their neglect of global topology. On top of that, existing deep clustering frameworks suffer from severe "clustering collapse" issues, particularly in imbalanced multi-class scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose S$^3$GLC, a novel framework that integrates global topological characteristics with local neighborhood features for balanced GLC. Theoretically, we leverage the Hoffman-Wielandt inequality to construct Spectral Signature as a rigorous lower bound for graph edit distance (GED), providing a computationally efficient proxy for global topological features. We additionally propose a joint manifold-preserving sparse regression module to fuse Spectral Signature with Spatial Representation, eliminating noise while preserving the complementary information. Furthermore, to mitigate clustering collapse, we introduce a balanced clustering objective based on optimal transport, harnessing the Sinkhorn-Knopp normalization to enforce equipartition constraints on cluster assignments. Extensive experiments on 10 real-world datasets demonstrate that S$^3$GLC significantly outperforms 15 state-of-the-art baselines, achieving remarkable improvements on benchmark datasets such as REDDIT-12K and OGBG-PPA.

replace Speculative Self-Distillation enables Efficient Knowledge Internalization

Authors: Shayan Talaei, Agam Bhatia, Arshia Soltani Moakhar, Jonas H\"ubotter, Amin Saberi, Azalia Mirhoseini

Abstract: Many real-world applications require language models to internalize knowledge that was absent from pretraining, to incorporate continually updated facts, or to learn new domain skills. Self-distillation has emerged as a natural approach for this task. A model conditioned on the source material acts as the teacher, and an unconditioned student is trained to reproduce the teacher's behavior closed-book. We propose Speculative Self-Distillation (SSD), an efficient per-token mixed-policy method for self-distillation. The student generates each rollout by default, while the teacher intervenes only at positions where its next-token distribution substantially diverges from the student's. Unlike off-policy distillation with teacher-generated prefixes, this design keeps training rollouts close to the student's test-time behavior, while avoiding the low-signal states that fully on-policy rollouts drift into. In effect, SSD lets the teacher steer the rollout exactly where the privileged context changes the prediction. On three closed-book QA benchmarks measuring fact recall, knowledge updates, and skill acquisition, SSD nearly matches on-policy distillation while using 38-69% fewer supervised tokens and consistently outperforms off-policy distillation on the held-out test set. On a forgetting stress test, SSD retains the most general capability while also achieving the best in-domain accuracy and using less supervision than the on-policy baseline. Overall, SSD establishes a new accuracy-efficiency-forgetting Pareto frontier for self-distillation.

replace GigaEdge Enhance: An SDK for Practical Streaming Speech Enhancement

Authors: Artem Sokolov, Artem Khaidarov, Yana Lysova, Alexander Gladkikh, Vitaliy Yulis

Abstract: We present GigaEdge Enhance, an open source SDK for streaming speech enhancement on CPU servers, mobile devices, and in browsers. Applications can build enhancement channels from neural denoising, normalization and dynamics blocks, sample rate conversion, and acoustic echo cancellation where supported. These stages can be combined, while normalization can also run with out a neural model. The release includes an 8 kHz INT8 denoiser and discriminative and hybrid 48 kHz FP32 models. A shared C++ core supports ONNX Runtime and TensorFlow Lite through native interfaces, Android and iOS SDKs, and WebAssembly integration. Independent stream contexts share model resources, and consecutive frames from one stream can be processed in a single inference call. We evaluate the released models on EARS-WHAM v2 and compare their quality and processing costs with RNNoise and DPDFNet2. All tested native and Web configurations process files faster than real time, although larger chunks require more time to collect input. We also measure how many streams a shared model can process on a CPU. Our contribution is a configurable deployment system and an experimental study of how model choice, chunk size, and execution environment affect its use. All reported SDK timing uses ONNX Runtime. Keywords: speech enhancement, streaming inference, cross-platform deployment, temporal frame grouping, computational efficiency

replace Antidistillation is Cheap: Lightweight Distillation-Resistance via Stylistic Corruption

Authors: Anh Tu Ngo, Anupam Chattopadhyay

Abstract: Knowledge distillation (KD) is a highly effective paradigm for transferring capabilities from large language models (LLMs) to smaller, more specialized student models. In commercial deployments where frontier models are restricted behind black-box APIs, sequence-level distillation remains a potent vector for competitors to clone proprietary capabilities at a fraction of the development cost. Existing defenses mostly rely on surrogate models to manipulate next-token sampling, which increases computational overhead and real-time inference latency. In this work, we propose a lightweight defense that injects style-steering vectors into the teacher model's activations upon malicious queries. The framework allows hosts to customize the target defensive styles and switch among them per request, thereby making it harder for adversaries to identify a stable, learnable signature. Because this inference-time intervention largely preserves answer correctness and avoids the degenerate artifacts of prior defenses, adversaries are misled into believing they are extracting valid teacher outputs. Meanwhile, standard consumers continue to receive original, unmodified responses. Our experiments demonstrate that the framework consistently degrades student learning across model families, with minimal inference overhead. Our approach serves as a low-cost deterrent that raises the attacker's extraction and post-hoc sanitization costs. Code is available at https://github.com/anhtu96/distill-resist.

URLs: https://github.com/anhtu96/distill-resist.

replace On-policy distillation as a Backdoor Propagation Channel

Authors: Zane Fu, Yanzhao Zheng, Ziting Qin, Qianshan Wei, Yi Yu, Gongli Xi, Jihuai Zhu, Baohua Dong, Hangcheng Zhu, Ruohui Huang, Gang Yu

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is emerging as an important paradigm for the post training of large language models, improving student models by leveraging supervision generated online by teacher models. However, whether this process also transfers latent security risks from teacher models to student models remains insufficiently studied. In this work, we reveal a new security risk in which an attacker first implants a conditional backdoor into the teacher model through supervised fine tuning (SFT), after which OPD can transfer the backdoor to the student model with only an extremely small proportion of trigger prompt exposure. More concerningly, we find that such backdoor transfer is substantially stronger under OPD than under conventional distillation methods, suggesting that OPD may provide an efficient channel for propagating backdoors across models. Our mechanistic analysis shows that the backdoor signal is primarily transmitted through anomalous differences in the conditional probabilities produced by the teacher model for triggered and clean inputs, and is gradually internalized into the internal representations of the student model during training. Furthermore, we investigate how to suppress such anomalous conditional probability signals at the source of distillation supervision and, based on this analysis, propose an OPD training method tailored to this conditional backdoor propagation setting to mitigate the risk of backdoor transfer induced by untrusted teacher supervision.

replace LoopPrune: Recurrent Pruning for Looped Diffusion Transformers

Authors: Weizhong Huang, Huaijia Kou, Yan Zhang, Fei Chao, Xiawu Zheng, Liujuan Cao

Abstract: Looped diffusion Transformers reuse shared blocks to deepen computation and match larger models with fewer parameters, reducing storage and deployment burdens. Yet repeated attention and MLP computations remain costly. Pruning reduces storage and computation by removing redundant parameters or units. Training-free methods struggle to resolve recurrent contributions, while training-based methods lack explicit constraints on position-specific errors affecting later inputs and final outputs. We propose LoopPrune, a pruning and recovery framework addressing these requirements. Recurrent importance estimation combines shared-removal effects and position variation to select a shared structure; shared reconstruction pools recurrent input statistics to compensate weights. Loop-position recovery compares complete denoising predictions at identical student states and matches text guidance, updating all student parameters against a fixed dense teacher. Extensive experiments across four open-source models, six benchmarks, and three pruning paradigms demonstrate effectiveness. At 30% structured parameter pruning, Looped-DiT B/16 retains 93.14% of dense six-benchmark average accuracy, exceeding the best baseline by 9.49%. Across PyTorch, vLLM-Omni, and SGLang Diffusion, pruned SDXSv3 reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 34.36-44.64% and accelerates denoising 1.46-1.74x over dense models.

replace Safe to Omit, Worth Omitting? Keeping the Answer and Cutting Latency When an LLM Agent Skips Another's Message

Authors: Xinyang Li, Chih-Hsuan Yang, Xiaolong Ma, Anjir Ahmed Chowdhury, Vikram Vasudevan, Rajeev Thakur, Feng Yan

Abstract: In multi-agent systems, a receiver agent often waits for a helper agent's message (text or its latent KV cache), which can improve performance but adds latency. We study when a receiver agent can skip the message safely (the final answer rarely changes) and beneficially (the time saved exceeds the cost of deciding). Our mechanism reads the receiver agent's confidence without the message and uses a finite-sample test to certify a threshold at which, with high probability, at most 5% of skipped answers change; if none passes, the system keeps communicating. We test it on OpenBookQA, ARC-Challenge, and MMLU-Pro with three Qwen model pairs, on cross-family agents, and on SQuAD and GSM8K. Four insights emerge. First, oracle headroom is weak evidence that messages help: control inputs without answer-specific content, matched to change answers as often as real messages, reproduce a median 76% of the oracle's gain. Second, whether a message can be skipped depends on how often and how predictably it alters the answer, not on its accuracy; every certified KV-cache path is less accurate than the receiver agent alone. Third, where text messages help, skipping keeps most of their gain: on unseen questions in the four such settings, the policies skip 59 to 80% and keep an estimated 76 to 95% of the gain. In two prospective tests with all rules fixed in advance, our mechanism skips a three-agent system's verifier on 39.9% of sealed questions, keeping 12.55 of its 12.80-point gain, and a 32B helper on 68.4% of held-out questions, keeping its whole gain. Fourth, safety does not determine efficiency: at batch size one, skipping text saves hundreds of milliseconds per query and skipping KV cache far less. Certification extends to SQuAD but not to GSM8K. In eleven tests on unseen questions, every certified policy we could test kept its change rate's 95% upper bound below 5% under our answer parsers.

replace Bradley-Terry Training Can Distort Outcome Probabilities of Initially Accurate Language World Models

Authors: Xinze Shi, Litian Zhang, Zhenjie Su, Zelong Yu, Yuting Zhang, Chaozhuo Li, Qihua Feng, Zifeng Kang, Zhengxuan Hu

Abstract: Comparative feedback, in which a judge says which of two candidate outcomes is closer to what happened, is a practical way to train language world models that assign probabilities to an action's outcomes. We show that, fitted with a Bradley-Terry objective, it need not teach the true outcome probabilities and can distort accurate ones. On a simulated robot push with enumerated outcome distributions, this objective takes a model that is accurate on unseen layouts (hazard error 2.4 percentage points, pp) and moves 33 to 43 pp of its probability onto outcomes that none of a condition's enumerated branches produces. The cause is the objective, not the comparisons: its optimum, computed before training, predicts where the model ends up, and a likelihood that knows the judge's rule learns from the same comparisons without the shift. The shift is robust: it recurs on unseen conditions, under five candidate laws and a three-fold denser enumeration, and about 29 pp remain without the physically impossible events. Its cost is conditional: with those events it inflates hazard probabilities, and a threshold inherited from the pre-feedback model costs about 3 pp of utility per decision on trained and 1 to 2 pp on unseen conditions, through false rejects; without them the utility loss averages below 1 pp over six seeds, though a small regret in action choice remains. Audits of readout, response and decision trace the effect from probabilities to actions.

replace Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation of RL Experts: Toward Continual Post-Training

Authors: Zhen Zhang, Changyi Yang, Xuehai He, Chengzhi Liu, Yutong Yin, Ziyue Liu, Zhuoran Yin, Yangmin Li, Zhihan Liu, Jisen Li, Mingyu Jin, Chuhan Li, Zhaotian Weng, Ruoyu He, Wenyao Gao, Jiaxin Deng, Chenwei Wu, Hui Shen, Wenhao Chai, Chenyang Zhao, Zhuofan Ying, Kieran Liu, Andrew Chen, Pony Ma, Zhaoran Wang, Alkesh Patel, Zhe Gan, Xin Eric Wang

Abstract: RL post-training typically produces domain experts that must then be consolidated into one model, and as new experts keep arriving, this consolidation becomes a continual process. Weight merging and multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) must re-consolidate the whole model whenever an expert is added, and can erode capabilities outside the target domains. We propose Composable Soft-Prompt Distillation (CSPD), which keeps the pre-RL model frozen, distills each RL expert independently into a soft prompt, and composes experts by concatenating their prompts without joint training. A single soft prompt suffices to distill an RL expert, recovering at least 90% of the teacher's gain in 22 of 23 math, code, and agent settings. Independently trained prompts compose well. In a multi-expert setting, the average rises with every expert added, and the full composition matches MOPD (67.83 vs. 67.79). The composition generalizes best across six held-out benchmarks. Input-space adaptation adds little serving overhead, keeping throughput within 1.5% of the unadapted backbone and reaching 2.40x that of rank-4 LoRA. These results point toward continual post-training on a fixed backbone.

replace Interpreting Diffusion Model Activations in Natural Language

Authors: Yukinori Yamamoto, Kotaro Yoshida, Ryotaro Shimizu

Abstract: Interpreting the intermediate activations of diffusion models (DMs) is key to understanding how they generate images. Existing interpretability methods for DMs provide limited support for open-ended interrogation, making it difficult to extract human-interpretable insights. replace The Complexity of Oracle-Efficient Realizable Transductive Learning

Authors: Changlong Wu

Abstract: In realizable transductive online learning, the learner knows the entire sequence of $T$ instances in advance, and the labels are consistent with one of $N$ hypotheses. With explicit access to the hypothesis class, the halving algorithm makes at most $\log_2 N$ mistakes. Can a comparable guarantee be achieved using only polynomially many empirical risk minimization (ERM) queries? We answer this question negatively, even for a weighted-ERM oracle that optimizes arbitrary real-valued linear objectives and returns a complete maximizing hypothesis. For every fixed $\beta\in(0,1)$ and every randomized learner making polynomially many oracle queries, there exists a fixed realizable instance with exactly $N=2^{\lfloor T^\beta\rfloor}$ hypotheses on which the learner makes $\Omega_\beta(\sqrt{T\log N})$ mistakes in expectation. This matches the agnostic-rate upper bound achieved by random playout using $O(T)$ oracle calls. The separation persists for much smaller classes as well: along a sequence with $\log N=\Theta((\log T)^2)$, we establish a mistake lower bound of $\exp(\Omega(\log T/\log\log T))$. Our main technical contribution is a composition lemma that combines two realizable prediction problems such that their mistake lower bounds add, up to a small loss, while keeping the growth of the combined hypothesis class under control. Iterating this lemma yields a family of hard instances spanning the desired parameter regimes, establishing a sharp separation between information-theoretic and oracle-efficient mistake guarantees.

replace De Novo Generation of Odorant Molecules with Targeted Olfactory Receptor Activation Patterns

Authors: Kexin Zhang, Manhao Guan

Abstract: Odor perception arises from a combinatorial code in which each molecule activates a specific subset of olfactory receptors (ORs), and the resulting activation pattern determines the perceived smell. We present a framework for de novo generation of molecules predicted to match target multi-receptor activation profiles. We first construct a panoramic binding matrix over 20 ORs, then cluster it to discover representative activation patterns, and finally formulate a structured selectivity loss, combining pairwise ranking, distribution matching, and complexity constraints, to steer a molecular generator via reinforcement learning. Across nine target profiles of varying complexity, our method achieves high predicted selectivity within 18000 oracle calls and provides the largest gains on profiles that are rare under the prior, especially broad-spectrum and sparse-activator targets.

replace Vasicek Graph Diffusion Model

Authors: Junyi Tang, Chang Liu, Kun Zhan

Abstract: Diffusion-based graph generation models progressively transform Gaussian noise into structured graphs through stochastic dynamics. We observe that the effectiveness of this transformation is fundamentally determined by the evolution of the diffusion trajectory. Existing graph diffusion models often suffer from excessive uncertainty during diffusion and delayed growth of the signal-to-noise ratio, causing useful structural information to emerge only in the later stages of generation. To address this issue, we propose the Vasicek Graph Diffusion (VGD), a graph diffusion process derived from the Vasicek dynamics. The key insight of VGD is data-anchored mean dynamics with a closed-form solution, which analytically establishes a continuous transition from Gaussian noise to the target graph distribution. Unlike conventional graph diffusion processes that rely predominantly on stochastic evolution, VGD introduces a data-anchored drift that continuously steers the latent state toward the target distribution throughout diffusion. As a consequence, VGD maintains a smoothly evolving uncertainty while rapidly increasing the signal-to-noise ratio throughout diffusion, allowing informative graph structures to emerge much earlier during generation. Extensive experiments on multiple graph generation benchmarks demonstrate that VGD outperforms existing diffusion-based graph generative models in terms of generation quality and structural fidelity.

replace Rewirable Circuits in Transformers with Loom Attention

Authors: Da Xiao, Qingye Meng, Shengping Li, Xingyuan Yuan

Abstract: We propose loom attention, which allows a Transformer to dynamically rewire its circuits at every token by interweaving a head's vertical attention over the outputs of earlier heads (to obtain its query, key and value) with its horizontal attention over tokens. We instantiate vertical attention efficiently with normalized and gated linear attention over a per-token matrix memory, which stores the compressed head outputs and serves as a second residual stream. replace Reinforcing Diffusion Models by Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching

Authors: Yihong Luo

Abstract: Forward diffusion reinforcement learning (RL)---enabling deterministic ODE sampling without trajectory backpropagation---has grown increasingly popular for efficiently aligning diffusion models. However, we reveal that existing methods essentially rely on signed reward-weighted regression (RWR), regressing the trainable policy velocity onto rollouts from a behavior distribution. Unbiased baseline subtraction implicitly assumes that the policy velocity equals the behavior marginal velocity---an idealized assumption violated under classifier-free guidance (CFG) rollouts, offline/off-policy updates, and imperfect online self-consistency. This behavior--policy mismatch breaks baseline invariance and injects baseline-induced gradient drift, destabilizing training. To resolve this issue, we propose Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching (iTDM), taking the infinitesimal limit of Trajectory Distribution Matching toward a time-local reward-guided target via the posterior-score identity to yield a sample-level gradient anchored at the behavior velocity without learning an additional reward-tilted target score. Tracking this velocity with an online auxiliary denoiser guarantees exact baseline invariance at the auxiliary optimum, effectively suppressing baseline drift across both online and offline RL. Extensive experiments across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment show that iTDM achieves state-of-the-art in-domain and out-of-domain performance across group-wise online/offline RL and pairwise offline RL. Notably, on the challenging GenEval2 benchmark, iTDM converges nearly 3\times faster than competing methods while scaling smoothly to 0.87 without collapse. Furthermore, iTDM scales effectively to the 5.6B FLUX.3 model, achieving better performance across both in-domain and unseen metrics. Code is available at https://github.com/Luo-Yihong/iTDM

replace-cross Fast Sketching of Polynomial Kernels of Polynomial Degree

Authors: Zhao Song, David P. Woodruff, Zheng Yu, Lichen Zhang

Abstract: Kernel methods are fundamental in machine learning, and faster algorithms for kernel approximation provide direct speedups for many core tasks in machine learning. The polynomial kernel is especially important as other kernels can often be approximated by the polynomial kernel via a Taylor series expansion. Recent techniques in oblivious sketching reduce the dependence in the running time on the degree $q$ of the polynomial kernel from exponential to polynomial, which is useful for the Gaussian kernel, for which $q$ can be chosen to be polylogarithmic (for data of bounded radius and ridge parameter $\lambda\geq 1/\mathrm{poly}(n)$). However, for more slowly growing kernels, such as the neural tangent and arc-cosine kernels, $q$ needs to be polynomial, and previous work incurs a polynomial factor slowdown in the running time. We give a new oblivious sketch which greatly improves upon this running time, by removing the dependence on $q$ from the $nd$ term, which is the leading order term when the dimension $d$ is large. For kernels approximated through a truncated Taylor series, our guarantees hold up to a ridge term $\lambda I$. Combined with a sampling scheme over the degrees of the Taylor series, we further reduce the running time for slowly growing kernels such as the neural tangent kernel, for which we obtain time $\tilde O(nd+\epsilon^{-4}\lambda^{-4}n^6)$ on $n$ unit-norm points with accuracy $\epsilon$. Since the neural tangent kernel has a closed form that can be evaluated exactly in $O(n^2d)$ time, this bound is faster only when $d$ is at least about $(n/(\epsilon\lambda))^4$.

replace-cross Treatment Effects in Extreme Regimes

Authors: Ahmed Aloui, Ali Hasan, Yuting Ng, Miroslav Pajic, Vahid Tarokh

Abstract: Understanding treatment effects in extreme regimes is important for characterizing risks associated with different interventions. replace-cross Randomized Forward Mode of Automatic Differentiation For Optimization Algorithms

Authors: Khemraj Shukla, Yeonjong Shin

Abstract: Reverse mode automatic differentiation (AD) computes a gradient in a single sweep, but at a memory cost that grows with the length of the computational graph. That cost becomes the binding constraint when the graph is long, as in parameter identification governed by differential equations or in training on memory-limited hardware. We study a randomized forward mode gradient (RFG) as an alternative to backpropagation in precisely this regime. RFG is a random gradient estimator built from the directional derivative along a random vector, a quantity that forward mode AD computes without storing a tape. Replacing the gradient with RFG yields a class of optimization algorithms; for RFG-based gradient descent (GD) and Polyak's heavy ball (PHB) methods, we establish convergence results on quadratic functions. The analysis reveals that using random vectors from a probability distribution with the smallest kurtosis yields the fastest rate of convergence. Computational experiments demonstrate the performance of the proposed algorithms and verify the theoretical findings, including beyond quadratic functions. We further characterize when RFG is computationally preferable to backpropagation. Since the variance of RFG grows with the parameter dimension $d$ while the memory required by the reverse mode grows with the length $\ell$ of the computational graph, the relevant figure of merit is the ratio $\ell/d$. We confirm this by measurement against reverse mode with checkpointing. For a physics-informed neural network, where $d$ is large and $\ell$ short, RFG is not competitive. For parameter identification governed by differential equations, where a handful of coefficients is recovered from a long time integration, RFG requires one to three orders of magnitude less memory. The price is a larger iteration count, which leaves its time to solution comparable to or worse than a well-tuned reverse mode.

Our results showed that the MT-based approach using M2M100 outperforms others, with a detection rate of 95.82% and a suggestion accuracy of 78.90% in automatic evaluations (AE) and an average score of 3.0 out of 5.0 in manual evaluation (ME) from native speakers for grammar and logical corrections. Abstract: Existing benchmarks often rely on predefined questions or reference captions, limiting their flexibility for evaluating detailed descriptions on new image collections. replace-cross A Steerable Deep Network for Model-Free Diffusion MRI Registration

Authors: Gianfranco Cort\'es, Xiaoda Qu, Baba C. Vemuri

Abstract: Nonrigid registration is vital to medical image analysis but remains challenging for diffusion MRI (dMRI) due to its high-dimensional, spatio-angular dependence. We present a novel, geometric deep learning framework for model-free, nonrigid registration of raw dMRI data. Central to our framework is the equivalence between equivariant spatio-angular convolutions and 3D steerable convolutions on spherical-harmonic coefficient fields, which we exploit to construct an $\mathrm{SE}(3)$-equivariant U-Net. A by-product of this design choice is freedom from having to augment the data with roto-translated versions of itself. We also formulate a maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) loss between water-displacement distributions, evaluated in Fourier space as a weighted sum of squared signal differences, and extend it with a local contrast correction that reduces to a local correlation. In controlled comparisons, including a known deformation of real tissue, the raw signal yields markedly more accurate alignment than fractional anisotropy under the same optimizer. On held-out HCP subjects and on a held-out clinical-grade cohort (AOMIC) from a different scanner, our method improves anatomical overlap over ANTs SyN on fractional anisotropy, mrregister on fODFs, DDMReg and SynthMorph, without estimating any derived representation, and the steerable network matches a conventional U-Net with fewer parameters while being unaffected by grid rotations of its inputs. This work establishes a foundation for data-driven, geometry-aware dMRI registration directly in the acquisition space.

replace-cross On the Efficiency of Structured Pruning in Small Language Model Pretraining

Authors: Yixiao Li, Xianzhi Du, Ajay Jaiswal, Tao Lei, Tuo Zhao, Chong Wang, Jianyu Wang

Abstract: Companies that deploy generative language models on edge devices often need to train small models from scratch due to legal, data-governance, or product-customization requirements. An alternative approach is to train an enlarged model and compress it to the target size via structured pruning, but this advantage becomes unclear once the cost of pretraining the enlarged model is included. We introduce the enlarge-and-prune framework to study this question under full-cost accounting, and show that conventional enlarge-and-prune pipelines -- which train the enlarged model, prune it, and recover in separate stages -- do not consistently outperform direct small-model training under the same total token budget. In controlled experiments compressing 2.8B models to 1.3B with up to 2T tokens on the same dataset, IDEA Prune consistently outperforms conventional pipelines and modestly improves overall performance relative to the scratch baseline, improving MMLU accuracy to 46.4%, compared with 31.4-33.4% for conventional pipelines. These advantages are preserved after downstream supervised fine-tuning, where IDEA Prune matches or outperforms all baselines of the same size on instruction following, math reasoning, and code generation. Our analysis further reveals that intermediate checkpoints provide more favorable pruning starting points than fully converged ones, and that an enlargement factor of approximately 2.6x yields the best pruned models in the tested regime.

Abstract: Ultrasound videos are an important form of clinical imaging data, and deep learning-based automated analysis can improve diagnostic accuracy and clinical efficiency. However, the scarcity of labeled data and the inherent challenges of video analysis have impeded the advancement of related methods. In this work, we introduce E-ViM$^3$, a data-efficient Vision Mamba network that preserves the 3D structure of video data, enhancing long-range dependencies and inductive biases to better model space-time correlations. With our design of Enclosure Global Tokens (EGT), the model captures and aggregates global features more effectively than competing methods. To further improve data efficiency, we employ masked video modeling for self-supervised pre-training, with the proposed Spatial-Temporal Chained (STC) masking strategy designed to adapt to various video scenarios. Experiments demonstrate that E-ViM$^3$ performs as the state-of-the-art in two high-level semantic analysis tasks across four datasets of varying sizes: EchoNet-Dynamic, CAMUS, MICCAI-BUV, and WHBUS. Furthermore, our model achieves competitive performance with limited labels, highlighting its potential impact on real-world clinical applications.

replace-cross Causal pieces: analysing and improving spiking neural networks piece by piece

Authors: Dominik Dold, Philipp Christian Petersen

Abstract: We introduce "causal pieces", a novel concept for analysing spiking neural networks (SNNs), inspired by "linear pieces" used to study expressivity and trainability in artificial neural networks (ANNs). Causal pieces partition the input and parameter space of a feedforward SNN with single-spike coding into distinct regions where the same subnetwork causes the output spikes. For networks of current-based leaky integrate-and-fire (LIF) neurons with large membrane time constants, we show that within each causal piece, output spike times are locally Lipschitz continuous with respect to inputs and network parameters. We further prove a lower bound on the approximation error that depends on the number of causal pieces. Thus, the number of causal pieces is a measure of the approximation capabilities of SNNs, which is valid despite spike-time discontinuities and applies to networks with both excitatory and inhibitory synapses. Empirically, we find that parameter initialisations yielding more causal pieces on the training set strongly correlate with SNN training success across multiple benchmarks, including Yin-Yang, Fashion-MNIST, and EuroSAT. Moreover, simulations with standard single-spike LIF neurons indicate that our findings extend beyond the theoretically analysed regime. These results establish causal pieces as a powerful and principled tool for analysing and improving the computational capabilities of SNNs.

replace-cross Virology Capabilities Test (VCT): A Multimodal Virology Q&A Benchmark

Authors: Jasper G\"otting, Pedro Medeiros, Jon G Sanders, Nathaniel Li, Long Phan, Karam Elabd, Lennart Justen, Dan Hendrycks, Seth Donoughe

Abstract: We present the Virology Capabilities Test (VCT), a large language model (LLM) benchmark that measures the capability to troubleshoot complex virology laboratory protocols. Constructed from the inputs of dozens of PhD-level expert virologists, VCT consists of $322$ multimodal questions covering fundamental, tacit, and visual knowledge that is essential for practical work in virology laboratories. VCT is difficult: expert virologists with access to the internet score an average of $22.1\%$ on questions specifically in their sub-areas of expertise. However, the most performant LLM, OpenAI's o3, reaches $43.8\%$ accuracy, outperforming $94\%$ of expert virologists even within their sub-areas of specialization. The ability to provide expert-level virology troubleshooting is inherently dual-use: it is useful for beneficial research, but it can also be misused. Therefore, the fact that publicly available models outperform virologists on VCT raises pressing governance considerations. We propose that the capability of LLMs to provide expert-level troubleshooting of dual-use virology work should be integrated into existing frameworks for handling dual-use technologies in the life sciences.

replace-cross TRIM: Achieving Extreme Sparsity with Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven Pruning

Authors: Florentin Beck, William Rudman, Carsten Eickhoff

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) present substantial computational and memory challenges. Existing one-shot pruning methods often use a uniform sparsity target for all rows within a layer, which can be too coarse at high sparsity. This work introduces TRIM (Targeted Row-wise Iterative Metric-driven pruning), a forward-statistics-based wrapper that allocates different sparsity ratios to individual output rows while keeping model weights frozen. TRIM separates the choice of which weights to prune (scoring) from how many to prune in each row (allocation), and composes with existing scorers and layer-wise allocations. Across Qwen2.5, LLaMA-2, and OPT models, its largest gains occur at 70-80% sparsity. We conclude that fine-grained, row-wise allocation is an effective mechanism for substantially improving one-shot pruning quality in the extreme-sparsity regime. Code: https://github.com/flobk/TRIM.

replace-cross Local Policy Manifolds for Efficient Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Qiyue Xia, Tianwei Wang, J. Michael Herrmann

Abstract: Multi-objective reinforcement learning (MORL) aims at optimising several, often conflicting goals to improve the flexibility and reliability of RL in practical tasks. This is typically achieved by finding a set of diverse, non-dominated policies that form a Pareto front in the performance space. However, constructing such a policy set remains computationally demanding, as it requires finding not a single optimal policy, but a set of policies to cover a wide range of trade-offs among the objectives. We introduce LLE-MORL, an approach that traces policy manifolds by utilising the local relationship between the high-dimensional policy parameter space and the performance space. This structured representation enables an efficient search within contiguous solution domains via locally linear extrapolation, allowing for the rapid generation of high-quality solutions without extensive retraining. We also provide a theoretical analysis of the policy manifold structure in order to specify the applicability of the method. Experiments across diverse continuous control domains demonstrate that LLE-MORL consistently achieves higher Pareto front quality and efficiency than state-of-the-art approaches.

replace-cross Perfect Clustering in Extremely Sparse Multilayer Networks with Groupwise Subspace Structure

Authors: Marianna Pensky

Abstract: The paper studies a multilayer network on the same collection of nodes, where each layer follows the Generalized Random Dot Product Graph Model. Furthermore, all layers can be partitioned into groups, each group with its own unique latent subspace structure, but otherwise the matrices of connection probabilities can all be different. This setting is extremely general and includes most multilayer network models as special cases. In addition, we are interested in the situation where the layer networks may be extremely sparse and, therefore, the layer-by-layer analysis is unreliable. This problem can also be seen as a generalization of the classic setting in which one recovers a shared structure across an entire collection of matrices, extending it to the case where such structures are shared only within specific groups of matrices. The key task in this setting is to recover the groups of layers with unique subspace structures, since, after that, the information in those groups of layers can be pooled together by well-studied methods. Until now, clustering of layers in such networks was based on layer-by-layer analysis, which required the multilayer network to be sufficiently dense. Our paper is the first to provide a tensor-based approach to the problem that combines information from all layers in an unconventional manner. Our theoretical results, established under intuitive and non-restrictive assumptions, assert that the new technique achieves perfect clustering under sparsity conditions that, up to logarithmic factors, coincide with the computational lower bound derived for a much simpler model. The paper also delivers a nuanced message about the applicability of the new technique.

replace-cross Playing Markov Games Without Observing Payoffs

Authors: Daniel Ablin, Alon Cohen

Abstract: Optimization under uncertainty is a fundamental problem in learning and decision-making, particularly in multi-agent systems. Previously, Feldman, Kalai, and Tennenholtz [2010] demonstrated the ability to efficiently compete in repeated symmetric two-player matrix games without observing payoffs, as long as the opponents actions are observed. In this paper, we introduce and formalize a new class of zero-sum symmetric Markov games, which extends the notion of symmetry from matrix games to the Markovian setting. We show that even without observing payoffs, a player who knows the transition dynamics and observes only the opponents sequence of actions can still compete against an adversary who may have complete knowledge of the game. We formalize three distinct notions of symmetry in this setting and show that, under these conditions, the learning problem can be reduced to an instance of online learning, enabling the player to asymptotically match the return of the opponent despite lacking payoff observations. Our algorithms apply to both matrix and Markov games, and run in polynomial time with respect to the size of the game and the number of episodes. Our work broadens the class of games in which robust learning is possible under severe informational disadvantage and deepens the connection between online learning and adversarial game theory.

replace-cross Adaptive Microwave Reception with Diamond Spin Sensors

Authors: Amirhossein Taherpour, Abbas Taherpour, Mazen Hasna, Tamer Khattab, Khalid Qaraqe

Abstract: In a diamond-spin microwave receiver, pilot measurements can identify the spin-control phase that best separates binary phase-shift keyed symbols. Applying that phase takes time, during which carrier drift can erase the gain in alignment. We derive an exact feedback-delay threshold for a single payload spin from the phase information in the pilot record. On a finite menu of control phases, the pilot record enters the minimum-error update decision only through the direction of its first circular moment when that moment is nonzero. This result motivates a controller that estimates the direction from orthogonal pilot measurements and retains the existing phase whenever the predicted gain is lost to diffusion. For finite spin ensembles, we bound the distinguishability of the photon-count distributions in terms of optical readout and carrier-phase uncertainty, including fluctuations shared across spins. Packet simulations at matched spin budgets show that gated feedback can improve reliability with a single readout channel. With independently readable quadratures and sufficient spin preparations, decision-directed software tracking achieves lower packet error without a hardware phase update.

replace-cross Efficient Prediction of pass@k Scaling in Large Language Models

Authors: Joshua Kazdan, Colin Sullivan, Rylan Schaeffer, Youssef Allouah, Kyssen Yu, Noam Levi, Sanmi Koyejo

Abstract: Assessing the capabilities and risks of frontier AI systems is a critical area of research; recent work has shown that repeated sampling can dramatically increase both. For instance, repeated sampling allows models to solve difficult math and coding problems, but it may also increase their risk of being jailbroken. Such results raise a crucial question: how can one accurately predict a model's behavior when scaled to a massive number of attempts, given a vastly smaller sampling budget? This question is directly relevant to model providers, who serve hundreds of millions of users daily, and to governmental regulators, who seek to prevent harms. To answer this question, we make three contributions. First, we find that standard methods for fitting these laws suffer from statistical shortcomings that hinder predictions, especially in data-limited scenarios. Second, we remedy these shortcomings by introducing a robust estimation framework, which uses a beta-binomial distribution to generate more accurate predictions from limited data. Third, we propose a dynamic sampling strategy that allocates a greater budget to harder problems. replace-cross Repairing Reward Functions with Feedback to Mitigate Reward Hacking

Authors: Stephane Hatgis-Kessell, Logan Mondal Bhamidipaty, Emma Brunskill

Abstract: Human-designed reward functions for reinforcement learning (RL) agents are frequently misaligned with the humans' true, unobservable objectives, and thus act only as proxies. Optimizing for a misspecified proxy reward function often induces reward hacking, resulting in a policy misaligned with the human's true objectives. An alternative is to perform RL from human feedback, which involves learning a reward function from scratch by collecting human preferences over pairs of trajectories. However, building such datasets is costly. To address the limitations of both approaches, we propose Preference-Based Reward Repair (PBRR): an automated iterative framework that repairs a human-specified proxy reward function by learning an additive, transition-dependent correction term from preferences. A manually specified reward function can yield policies that are highly suboptimal under the ground-truth objective, yet corrections on only a few transitions may suffice to recover optimal performance. To identify and correct for those transitions, PBRR uses a targeted exploration strategy and a new preference-learning objective. We prove in tabular domains PBRR has a cumulative regret that matches, up to constants, that of prior preference-based RL methods. In addition, on a suite of reward-hacking benchmarks, PBRR consistently outperforms baselines that learn a reward function from scratch from preferences or modify the proxy reward function using other approaches, requiring substantially fewer preferences to learn high performing policies.

replace-cross Generalized Guarantees for Variational Inference in the Presence of Even and Elliptical Symmetry

Authors: Charles C. Margossian, Isaac E. Rankin, Lawrence K. Saul

Abstract: Variational inference (VI) approximates a target density $p$ by the best match $q$ in a family $\mathcal Q$ of tractable distributions. The variational approximation is found by minimizing a divergence, most commonly the reverse Kullback-Leibler divergence $\text{KL}(q||p)$, although other divergences can be considered. In practice, $p \notin \mathcal Q$ and so the optimal variational approximation is misspecified. Despite this misspecification, it was recently shown that the minimizer of $\text{KL}(q||p)$ is guaranteed to recover the mean in the presence of even symmetry and likewise the correlation matrix in the presence of elliptical symmetry for a log-concave target $p$. We extend this result in several directions. First, we relax the assumption of log-concavity and instead merely require $p$ to be unimodal, allowing for heavy-tailed targets. Second, we obtain guarantees for cases where $p$ exhibits symmetry along some but not all of its coordinates. These partial symmetries arise naturally in Bayesian hierarchical models, where the prior induces a challenging geometry but still possesses axes of symmetry. Third, we extend these guarantees to $f$-divergences, a broad class which includes the forward and reverse-KL divergences and the $\alpha$-divergences.

replace-cross Fine-Grained Caching for Diffusion Transformers with Few Calibration Conditions

Authors: Zihao Wu, Bohan Zeng, Yuanxing Zhang

Abstract: Diffusion transformers require repeated denoiser evaluations, making image and video generation computationally expensive. We propose a training-free framework that uses a few calibration conditions to construct a fixed, fine-grained module-reuse schedule without schedule search. The design is motivated by an empirical effect along deterministic sampling trajectories: with initial noise fixed, condition-dependent deviations in several module outputs evolve similarly across adjacent steps, so temporal differencing attenuates much of their variation. We term this effect Conditional Common-Mode Rejection (CCMR). Motivated by this observation, we rank timestep--layer--module locations using normalized ratios of consecutive feature displacements and construct a Cache Book through two-stage calibration. The second stage updates a scorer-only reference with each provisional bit after recording its indexed score, while retaining full-compute trajectories. At inference, the fixed schedule requires no per-input policy estimation and can be nested within fixed step-wise cache policies. Module caching alone achieves $1.66$--$1.72\times$ speedup on the evaluated image models and $1.54$--$1.68\times$ on the video models. Combining it with MagCache or SeaCache provides further acceleration at a model-dependent cost in paired fidelity.

replace-cross Statistical Inference for Fuzzy Clustering

Authors: Qiuyi Wu, Zihan Zhu, Anru R. Zhang

Abstract: Clustering is a central tool in biomedical research for discovering heterogeneous patient subpopulations, where group boundaries are often diffuse rather than sharply separated. While soft clustering methods such as fuzzy $c$-means (FCM) allow mixed memberships, principled statistical inference for fuzzy clustering remains limited. We propose a weighted fuzzy $c$-means (WFCM) framework for settings with cluster-size imbalance. Cluster-specific weights $\mathbf{w}$ re-balance the classic FCM criterion to adjust the relative influence of clusters of unequal size, and we construct a normalized working density from the weighted objective with scale parameter $\sigma$ and fuzziness parameter $m$. Estimation is performed via FCM-style alternating initialization followed by numerical likelihood refinement; the intractable normalizing constant is approximated by importance sampling using a data-adaptive Gaussian-mixture as the proposal distribution. replace-cross On Approximate Computation of Critical Points

Authors: Amir Ali Ahmadi, Georgina Hall

Abstract: We show that computing even coarse approximations of critical points is intractable for simple classes of nonconvex functions. We first present a family of $n$-variate cubic polynomials with coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$ that have exact critical points but for which no polynomial-time algorithm can write down a $\sqrt{n}/4$-approximate critical point in its standard representation. We then show that computational hardness persists when an exact critical point has a short representation. More specifically, we prove that for every fixed $\eta>0$, unless P $=$ NP, no polynomial-time (or even pseudo-polynomial-time) algorithm can take an $n$-variate cubic polynomial with rational coefficients ${c_\alpha}$ and return a point with gradient norm at most $(\max_\alpha |c_\alpha|)n^{1/2-\eta}$ whenever the polynomial has a critical point in $\{-1,0,1\}^n$. The algorithm may return an arbitrary point on all other inputs. Both results are essentially tight in their dependence on the dimension $n$. We also prove hardness results for lower-bounded functions and for an alternative notion of approximation, in which approximation is measured by distance to an exact critical point. Overall, our results stand in contrast to the commonly held belief that, in nonconvex optimization, approximate computation of critical points is a tractable task.

replace-cross SparVAR: Exploring Sparsity in Visual AutoRegressive Modeling for Training-Free Acceleration

Authors: Zekun Li, Ning Wang, Tongxin Bai, Changwang Mei, Peisong Wang, Shuang Qiu, Jian Cheng

Abstract: Visual AutoRegressive (VAR) modeling has garnered significant attention for its innovative next-scale prediction paradigm. However, mainstream VAR paradigms attend to all tokens across historical scales at each autoregressive step. As the next scale resolution grows, the computational complexity of attention increases quartically with resolution, causing substantial latency. Prior accelerations often skip high-resolution scales, which speeds up inference but discards high-frequency details and harms image quality. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed SparVAR can reduce the generation time of an 8B model producing $1024\times1024$ high-resolution images to the 1s, without skipping the last scales. Compared with the VAR baseline accelerated by FlashAttention, our method achieves a $\mathbf{1.57\times}$ speed-up while preserving almost all high-frequency details. When combined with existing scale-skipping strategies, SparVAR attains up to a $\mathbf{2.28\times}$ acceleration, while maintaining competitive visual generation quality. replace-cross Instance-optimal high-precision shadow tomography with few-copy measurements: A metrological approach

Authors: Senrui Chen, Weiyuan Gong, Sisi Zhou

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of shadow tomography in the high-precision regime under realistic measurement constraints. Given an unknown $d$-dimensional quantum state $\rho$ and a known set of observables $\{O_i\}_{i=1}^m$, the goal is to estimate expectation values $\{\mathrm{tr}(O_i\rho)\}_{i=1}^m$ to accuracy $\epsilon$ in $L_p$-norm, using possibly adaptive measurements that act on $O(\mathrm{polylog}(d))$ number of copies of $\rho$ at a time. We focus on the regime where $\epsilon$ is below an instance-dependent threshold. Our main contribution is an instance-optimal characterization of the sample complexity as $\tilde{\Theta}(\Gamma_p/\epsilon^2)$, where $\Gamma_p$ is a function of $\{O_i\}_{i=1}^m$ defined via an optimization formula involving the inverse Fisher information matrix. Previously, tight bounds were known only in special cases, e.g. Pauli shadow tomography with $L_\infty$-norm error. Concretely, we first analyze a simpler oblivious variant where the goal is to estimate an observable of the form $\sum_{i=1}^m \alpha_i O_i$ with $\|\alpha\|_q = 1$ (where $q$ is dual to $p$) revealed after the measurement. For single-copy measurements, we obtain a sample complexity of $\Theta(\Gamma^{\mathrm{ob}}_p/\epsilon^2)$. We then show $\tilde{\Theta}(\Gamma_p/\epsilon^2)$ is necessary and sufficient for the original problem, with the lower bound applying to unbiased, kurtosis-bounded estimators when $p \geq 1$. Our upper bounds rely on a two-step algorithm combining coarse tomography with local estimation. Notably, $\Gamma^{\mathrm{ob}}_\infty = \Gamma_\infty$. In both cases, allowing $c$-copy measurements improves the sample complexity by at most $\Omega(1/c)$. Our results establish a quantitative correspondence between quantum learning and metrology, unifying asymptotic metrological limits with finite-sample learning guarantees.

replace-cross UniVer: A Unified Perspective for Multi-step and Multi-draft Speculative Decoding

Authors: Yepeng Weng, Qiao Hu, Takehisa Yairi

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates Large Language Models via draft-then-verify, where verification can be framed as an Optimal Transport (OT) problem. Existing approaches typically handle multi-draft and multi-step aspects in isolation, applying either flat OT to single-step drafts or per-token rejection sampling to tree-structured candidates.
