replace-cross Jacobian-Velocity Bounds for Deployment Risk Under Covariate Drift

Authors: Jonathan R. Landers

Abstract: We study long-horizon deployment of a frozen predictor under dynamic covariate shift. A time-domain Poincar\'e inequality first reduces temporal risk volatility to derivative energy, and a Jacobian-velocity theorem supplies the corresponding pathwise control. A remainder extension separates the model-mediated, controllable term from conditional-risk variation not transmitted through the score Jacobian. Under low-rank drift, the bound decomposes the controllable term into tangent energy parallel and orthogonal to the drift subspace. This motivates drift-aligned tangent regularization (DTR), its anisotropic extension with a weaker orthogonal guardrail, and a matched monitoring proxy. Controlled experiments verify the temporal inequality, the efficiency of directional smoothing, and the necessity of the remainder term. The main field study uses a 128-dimensional gas-sensor array over ten chronological batches. A rank-4 basis estimated entirely before deployment beats ten matched random subspaces, and validation-selected anisotropic DTR improves deployment cross-entropy and volatility over pure DTR in all ten matched seeds while retaining substantially more accuracy than isotropic smoothing. Air Quality and power-consumption studies provide complementary regression evidence. Together, the results support a precise design rule: concentrate smoothness along expected motion, and use limited orthogonal smoothing when the estimated drift subspace may be incomplete.

replace-cross Robust stochastic first order methods in heavy-tailed noise via medoid mini-batch gradient sampling

Authors: Manojlo Vukovic, Dusan Jakovetic

Abstract: We consider a first order stochastic optimization framework where, at each iteration, $K$ independent identically distributed (i.i.d.) data point samples are drawn, based on which stochastic gradients can be queried. We allow gradient noise to be heavy-tailed, with possibly infinite variances. For the considered heavy-tailed setting, many algorithmic variants have recently been proposed based on gradient clipping or other nonlinear operators (e.g., normalization) applied over noisy gradients. In this paper, we take an alternative approach and propose a novel gradient estimator. The core idea of proposed estimator is to split the $K$-sized data batch into $M$ distinct data chunks, form for each chunk the stochastic gradient, and select the stochastic gradient of the chunk that is medoid of gradients of all data-chunks. For a broad class of symmetric heavy-tailed gradient noise distributions, we show that the proposed estimator has a finite second moment even when the underlying gradient noise has an infinite second moment. For nonconvex objectives, this yields convergence to a neighborhood of zero in expected squared gradient norm at a rate of $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1})$. We explicitly characterize the size of this neighborhood in terms of the noise and algorithm parameters. When the time horizon $T$ is known in advance, we establish an $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2})$ convergence rate. A diminishing step size achieves the same rate up to a logarithmic factor. Incorporating clipping or a more general nonlinear transformation yields high-probability convergence guarantees with the same rate up to logarithmic factors. Experimental results indicate that R-SGD-Mini and its clipped or nonlinear variant consistently perform favorably compared to SGD, clipped SGD, Nonlinear SGD and Median-of-Means based methods.

replace-cross Text-to-CAD Evaluation with CADTests

Authors: Dimitrios Mallis, Marco Wang, Ahmet Serdar Karadeniz, Elisa Ricci, Anis Kacem, Djamila Aouada

Abstract: Text-to-CAD has recently emerged as an important task with the potential to substantially accelerate design workflows. Despite its significance, there has been surprisingly little work on Text-to-CAD evaluation, and assessing CAD model generation performance remains a considerable challenge. In this work, we introduce a new evaluation perspective for Text-to-CAD based on automated testing. We propose CADTestBench, the first test-based benchmark for Text-to-CAD, based on CADTests, executable software tests that verify whether a generated CAD model satisfies the geometric and topological requirements of the input prompt. Using CADTestBench, we conduct comprehensive benchmarking of recent Text-to-CAD methods and further demonstrate that CADTests can also guide CAD model generation, yielding simple baselines that surpass performance of current methods. CADTestBench code and data are available.

replace-cross Agent Collectives Should Not Detect Their Own Imposters: A Chess Case Study

Authors: Alexandre Le Mercier, Chris Develder, Thomas Demeester

Abstract: Collectives of AI agents can outperform any single agent on complex tasks, yet remain vulnerable to imposters that deliberately mislead their peers. Because even one such imposter can sabotage the collective, reliable detection is essential. We examine two strategies: embedding detection inside the participating agents, or employing a dedicated external detector that observes the deliberation without joining it. On a deliberately small but challenging chess-based testbed, we find that simply warning agents of possible imposters is counterproductive. It degrades decisions even in the absence of adversaries, triggers unhelpful social dynamics, and leaks information to the imposter. An external detector avoids these pitfalls, yet must continually adapt as imposters evolve new strategies. We show that a lightweight meta-trained detector can rapidly recalibrate to unseen attacks from only a handful of examples, far more efficiently than conventional fine-tuning. To enable continued study of robust multi-agent systems under adaptive adversaries, we release the accompanying benchmark of labeled deliberations spanning a wide range of evolved imposter strategies. Code and data are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/gambit.

URLs: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/gambit.

replace-cross Fairness of Explanations in Artificial Intelligence (AI): A Unifying Framework, Axioms, and Future Direction toward Responsible AI

Authors: Gideon Popoola, John Sheppard

Abstract: Machine learning algorithms are being used in high-stakes decisions, including those in criminal justice, healthcare, credit, and employment. The research community has responded with two largely independent research fields: \emph{algorithmic fairness}, which targets equitable outcomes, and \emph{explainable AI} (XAI), which targets interpretable reasoning. This survey identifies and maps a novel blind spot at their intersection, which is a model that can satisfy every standard fairness criterion in its outputs while being profoundly unfair in its \emph{reasoning process}. We refer to this as the procedural bias, and mitigating it requires treating the fairness of explanations as a distinct object of scientific study. To our knowledge, we provide the first unified theoretical and literature review of this emerging field and elucidate the drawbacks of post-hoc explainers in certifying explanation fairness. Our central contribution is a \emph{conditional invariance framework} formalizing explanation fairness as the requirement that explanations should be indifferent regardless of the protected attributes $ P(E(X) \in \cdot \mid X_\text{rel} = x_\text{rel},\, A = a) = P(E(X) \in \cdot \mid X_\text{rel} = x_\text{rel},\, A = b)$ for all task-relevant $x$, a single principle from which all existing explanation fairness metrics emerge as partial operationalizations. We introduce a seven-dimensional taxonomy, identify three generative mechanisms of explanation inequity (representation-driven, explanation-model mismatch, actionability-driven), and propose a canonical six-step evaluation workflow for operationalizing explanation fairness audits in practice.

replace-cross Learning Scenario Reduction for Two-Stage Robust Optimization with Discrete Uncertainty

Authors: Tianjue Lin, Jianan Zhou, Jieyi Bi, Yaoxin Wu, Wen Song, Zhiguang Cao, Jie Zhang

Abstract: Two-Stage Robust Optimization (2RO) with discrete uncertainty is challenging, often rendering exact solutions prohibitive. Scenario reduction alleviates this issue by selecting a small, representative subset of scenarios to enable tractable computation. However, existing methods are largely problem-agnostic, operating solely on the uncertainty set without consulting the feasible region or recourse structure. In this paper, we introduce PRISE, a problem-driven sequential lookahead heuristic that constructs reduced scenario sets by evaluating the marginal impact of each scenario. While PRISE yields high-quality scenario subsets, each selection step requires solving multiple subproblems, making it computationally expensive at scale. To address this, we propose NeurPRISE, a neural surrogate model built on a GNN-Transformer backbone that encodes the per-scenario structure via graph convolution and captures cross-scenario interactions through attention. NeurPRISE is trained via imitation learning with a gain-aware ranking objective, which distills marginal gain information from PRISE into a learned scoring function for scenario ranking and selection. Extensive results on three 2RO problems show that NeurPRISE consistently achieves competitive regret relative to comprehensive methods, maintains strong calability with varying numbers of scenarios, and delivers 17-200x speedup over PRISE. NeurPRISE also exhibits strong zero-shot generalization, effectively handling instances with larger problem scales (up to 5x), more scenarios (up to 4x), and distribution shifts.

replace-cross Copy, Predict, Code the Rest: Lossless Graph Compression with Decoder-Side Neighbor Prediction

Authors: Jimmy Dubuisson

Abstract: We present a lossless compressor for directed graphs. It copies neighbors from an earlier row, predicts further targets from the part of the graph already decoded, and codes what remains. Its main coder, the candidate-bit coder (CB), proposes a bounded set of candidate targets, mixes their probabilities adaptively, and codes the leftover targets with a popularity-aware model. The encoder tries a fixed set of vertex orders and storage directions and keeps the smallest file that decodes exactly. On the benchmark graphs outside our development set, the selected files are smaller than the best recorded WebGraph and Zuckerli files and the best published HyperFast results. These savings have a cost. CB decodes much more slowly than the baselines and supports only sequential access. A faster companion coder, CG, is still slower than the baselines on large graphs. An indexed variant, CS-RA, gives smaller seekable files than the measured access baselines on nearly all tested graphs, but its cold queries are slow, and it fails on one development graph. We also explain what successful and failed predictions cost, and when copying a repeated row saves information.

replace-cross Structured Prompt Optimization Advances Globally and Locally Interpretable Learning from Complex Text

Authors: Tianyang Zhou, Wenbo Chen, Pierre Jinghong Liang, Leman Akoglu

Abstract: LLMs have advanced text classification, yet existing paradigms face a trade-off: supervised (label only) fine-tuning is scalable but offers limited reasoning on complex text and lacks broader model transparency, while discrete prompt optimization offers human-readable instructions but struggles with performance and scalability. We introduce eXTC (eXplainable Text Classifier), centered on a Standard Operating Procedure (SOP, or rulebook) learned in natural language, in three stages: (1) learning the SOP via a new Structured Prompt Optimization algorithm; (2) SOP-grounded reasoning distillation from a large teacher LLM into a compact LM; and (3) expanding reasoning capabilities beyond the SOP via reinforcement learning. This design enables eXTC to provide (i) fast inference via a compact LM, with (ii) inference-time local reasoning traces, alongside a global, modular explanation of its learned domain rules, while (iii) significantly outperforming existing paradigms across diverse benchmarks in both classification performance and explanation quality, with stage-by-stage gains.

replace-cross Speculative Decoding and the Curse of Multilinguality

Authors: Nirajan Paudel, Michael Ginn, Luc De Nardi, Alexis Palmer

Abstract: Speculative decoding is a popular technique for large language model (LLM) inference, enabling faster generation by drafting multiple tokens with a smaller draft model. However, the effectiveness of speculative decoding has mainly been studied for English. Motivated by the curse of multilinguality, we hypothesize that speculative decoding is far less effective for low-resource languages due to the limited multilingual capacities of smaller models. We test nine low-resource languages and four high-resource languages under a standard speculative decoding setup and find moderate evidence for our hypothesis. Next, we try to improve the multilingual capabilities of the smaller draft model via distillation from the larger model. We find, though, that distillation generalizes poorly across tasks in the same language, and we argue that assembling a task-agnostic, fully representative dataset is infeasible for low-resource languages. Finally, we propose weaker n-gram models as draft models; these provide moderate speed-ups due to their minuscule inference cost.

replace-cross Benchmarking Open-Ended Multi-Agent Coordination in Language Agents

Authors: Kale-ab Abebe Tessera, Andras Szecsenyi, Cameron Barker, Alexander Rutherford, Davide Paglieri, Aidan Scannell, Henry Gouk, Elliot J. Crowley, Tim Rockt\"aschel, Amos Storkey

Abstract: As language models are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents, they must coordinate with others over long horizons in open-ended interactive tasks. Yet existing evaluations rarely test these demands together, focusing instead on single-agent tasks, short interactions, or highly structured multi-agent settings. We introduce $alem$, a JAX-based benchmark for open-ended multi-agent coordination built on Craftax-like dynamics. Alem embeds procedurally generated coordination tasks, soft specialisation, communication, and controllable coordination difficulty into a long-horizon survival world with exploration, crafting, trading, and combat. We evaluate $13$ LLMs zero-shot within homogeneous teams, with trained MARL agents as reference points. Current LLM agents remain far from solving alem, averaging only ~6% normalised return, but their failures are not uniform. On the hardest coordination setting, zero-shot Gemini-3.1-Pro-High approaches MARL agents trained for one billion steps, while GPT-5.4-High achieves strong base-task reward but much lower coordination reward. This contrast shows that individual task competence does not imply coordination competence. Further experiments reveal that communication is the largest contributor to coordination, while memory and reasoning help when used to maintain multi-step plans. In heterogeneous teams, however, combining different models improves base-task progress but lowers coordination reward relative to their homogeneous counterparts. Overall, our results identify coordination as a distinct bottleneck for frontier LLM agents, separate from single-agent capabilities. Alem makes this bottleneck measurable and provides a controlled testbed for developing agents that communicate, allocate roles, and execute shared plans. Code is available at https://github.com/alem-world/alem-env, with LLM traces and a leaderboard at https://alem-world.github.io.

URLs: https://github.com/alem-world/alem-env,, https://alem-world.github.io.

replace-cross Supervising Recurrent Vision Transformers from Bottlenecked Context

Authors: Philippe Weinzaepfel, Christian Wolf, Mert B\"ulent Sariyildiz, Guillaume Bono, Gianluca Monaci

Abstract: Transformers are AI's workhorse but their computational cost becomes prohibitive when processing long sequences. We target long-horizon streaming vision and robotics applications, where it is particularly impractical to store and maintain a history of observations. Recurrent Transformers address this limitation by maintaining fixed-size memory but their performance lags behind that of transformers operating over the full observation history. We argue that this gap does not stem from architectural limitations, but from differences in how these models learn to compress past information. Without access to an observation history, recurrent models must explicitly decide what to retain in memory at each step, a significantly harder learning problem. In this work, we propose a distillation approach that transfers the compression strategy of a classical full-history transformer to a recurrent variant. We enable this by designing a teacher model that explicitly compresses its observation history into a fixed-size bottleneck representation and directly supervise the student's memory with this bottleneck representation, effectively aligning the two compression mechanisms. We show that this approach enables training of a recurrent latent robotic memory with linear-time complexity on the Mem-RPE task while substantially narrowing the performance gap to full-history transformers. We additionally validate the same principle on streaming visual question answering (VQA) and observe improved recurrent predictions thanks to memory distillation.

replace-cross When Variance Is Not an Error Map: Calibrated Uncertainty for Radiative Gaussian Splatting in Sparse-View CT

Authors: Chulin Zhao, Yiran Xu, Gao Yang, Shu Liu

Abstract: Radiative Gaussian Splatting enables fast and high-quality reconstruction in sparse-view CT, but reliable localization of reconstruction errors within the imaged anatomy remains challenging. It is unclear whether current uncertainty maps can reliably indicate where the reconstruction is wrong. We investigate when uncertainty can serve as a spatial error map and present an error-localization system that predicts voxel-wise reconstruction error by combining predictive uncertainty with complementary reconstruction-derived information. Under fixed Gaussian geometry and independent rectified-Gaussian density variables, an exact closed-form predictive moment is derived in this work to enable direct computation of voxel-wise variance without repeated reconstruction sampling. The resulting uncertainty maps were evaluated over both the whole reconstruction volume and the object foreground. Experiments show that strong whole-volume uncertainty-error correspondence can conceal poor error ranking within the object foreground area. This discrepancy recurs across three tested uncertainty constructions on a 15-scene benchmark. Guided by this analysis, our system combines closed-form predictive dispersion with image, geometry, cross-method discrepancy, and projection-residual cues through a learned error predictor. The resulting system substantially improves voxel-wise error localization within the anatomy, providing a spatially resolved approach to reconstruction reliability assessment in sparse-view CT.

replace-cross SkillBoost: A Constrained Exploration-Exploitation Process for Mitigating Skill Overfitting

Authors: Hongqiang Lin, Chao Liu, Xiaofan Bai, Xuan Jin, Yuhong Li, Nenggan Zheng, Xipeng Cao

Abstract: Enabling large language model (LLM) agents to accumulate and reuse experience from past interactions remains a central challenge in real-world applications. A promising solution is to treat skills as trainable states and optimize them in the same way as model parameters in neural network training. However, data-driven skill optimization is prone to overfitting to the limited trajectories collected from real environments. Overexploiting these trajectories overfits the current batch, while unconstrained exploration causes regression on previously solved cases. This tension motivates a constrained search view of skill self-evolution, governed by an exploration--exploitation trade-off. We propose SkillBoost, a three-stage framework that mitigates both risks: structured exploitation localizes observed failures to editable skill components, prior-guided exploration draws on prior knowledge in the LLM to generate diverse repair candidates, and verified acceptance commits a candidate only when it improves performance within a regression bound. Experiments across 23 model--benchmark configurations show that SkillBoost achieves state-of-the-art performance while mitigating overfitting, outperforming both human-crafted and LLM-generated skills. Notably, it matches or even surpasses the performance gains typically achieved through post-training. Furthermore, transfer experiments further show that optimized skills can be reused by other agents on similar tasks. The code is available at https://github.com/HQ-Lin/SkillBoost

URLs: https://github.com/HQ-Lin/SkillBoost

replace-cross Semantic Prism: Generative Segmentation with an Explicit Semantic-Image Interface

Authors: Weize Cai, Yongqi Dong, Zhida Shao, Zixin Fu, Xiaoxi Hu

Abstract: Conditional image generators can render segmentation maps as semantic RGB images, but color drift and boundary mixing can undermine direct decoding. We present Semantic Prism, a framework that combines an explicit semantic-image interface with hierarchical refinement for road-scene segmentation. A generator adapted from a diffusion-distilled backbone produces a semantic RGB image with a single denoising update per crop. Distances to a fixed class-color codebook define interface logits and pixelwise class probabilities. Hierarchical Generator Evidence Alignment (HGEA) combines spatially aligned multilevel features from the VAE encoder of the generator with the input image, generated semantic image, and interface probabilities to predict additive corrections to these logits. Contextual Interface Hierarchy Disagreement (C-IHD) combines pointwise uncertainty, local context, and interface-refinement disagreement in a fixed score for ranking pixel errors, without an auxiliary predictor or an additional network pass. On the 500-image Cityscapes validation set, Semantic Prism achieves 72.07% mean mIoU, improving on direct-interface decoding by 11.39 percentage points. Extensive capacity-matched ablations show higher mean mIoU for multilevel refinement than for flat and single-level alternatives. The Cityscapes-trained model transfers to ACDC Dataset, achieving 46.89% mIoU without target-domain adaptation. Beyond Cityscapes, on BDD100K dataset, a separately trained model achieves 62.22% mIoU on its full 1,000-image validation set. Across the 3 evaluated datasets, C-IHD improves pixel-error area under the AUPR over uncertainty derived from maximum softmax probability, with segmentation predictions held fixed for each comparison. In particular, on ACDC, AUPR increases from 0.6580 to 0.7557, demonstrating that Semantic Prism presents more effective error ranking under adverse-condition domain shift.

replace-cross qshap: Fast Shapley Decomposition of $R^2$ for Gradient-Boosted Trees

Authors: Zhongli Jiang, Min Zhang, Dabao Zhang

Abstract: Numerous methods have been developed to quantify feature attributions in individual predictions for tree ensembles. However, many applications require global measures of feature contributions to overall model performance. Although local attribution scores can be aggregated to characterize feature importance, such summaries do not directly decompose measures of predictive performance, such as $R^2$. This article introduces qshap, available in both R and Python, which provides Shapley decomposition of $R^2$ values for gradient-boosted decision trees (GBDTs) to quantify feature-specific contributions to model performance. By decomposing the quadratic loss of individual observations, qshap provides flexible tools to explore the importance of individual features and observations. qshap currently supports widely used GBDT implementations, including xgboost, lightgbm, and catboost, through a unified tree representation and efficient C++ backends. Its modular design can accommodate other GBDT implementations built from binary decision trees. In addition, we introduce a specialized backend for oblivious trees that exploits their symmetric structure to substantially accelerate computation.

replace-cross The Imperfective Paradox Is Not Necessarily in Large Language Models: A Benchmark Failure Before a Model Failure

Authors: Kaiqiao Han, Mark Steedman, Yizhou Sun

Abstract: Recent work uses an NLI benchmark of the imperfective paradox to argue that large language models exhibit a Teleological Bias and Calibration Crisis. We identify three validity concerns in benchmark construction, including aspectual reduction, semantic bias, and relation bias. Under a strict NLI standard, 76% of the Group A instances do not explicitly rule out culmination; in native-speaker annotation, 38% of Group A and 29% of Group C examples permit an alternative interpretation. To better isolate the semantic contrast, we construct Lexically Matched Minimal Pairs. Moreover, by formulating event-semantic NLI as a multi-step reasoning problem, we evaluate intermediate semantic judgments and use oracle-guided settings. We find that incorrect NLI entailment does not fully support teleological bias. Prompting often changes final NLI decisions without reliably improving the underlying semantic judgments, offering a possible explanation for the Calibration Crisis.

replace-cross Untangling the Mechanisms of Misleading Context in Medical Question Answering

Authors: Robin Linzmayer, No\'emie Elhadad

Abstract: Large language models now answer medical questions with expert-level performance. However, the context these systems act on can be misleading, and misleading context can corrupt a model's medical judgment. To understand how misleading context corrupts this judgment, we examine the model's susceptibility to the context, disclosure of it, mechanism of corrupted reasoning, and monitorability of the decision. On the medical reasoning subset of MedMisBench, a clinician-reviewed question-answering benchmark of 8,627 questions, we inject two types of misleading context cues, fabricated evidence and a bare assertion. We test three reasoning models, two that expose their full reasoning trace and one frontier model that exposes only its response. All three are more susceptible to the assertion than to the fabricated evidence, adopting the asserted answer 10 to 27 points more often. The misleading cues are disclosed in 81 to 98% of traces but only 7 to 90% of responses, and the assertion is disclosed less often than evidence based cues. Resampling from reasoning traces without disclosure shows the two cues corrupt reasoning differently, evidence entering early and accumulating while the assertion redirects the conclusion near its end. An LLM monitor catches 78% of corrupted decisions at 5% false positives when reading an open model's trace with guidance, against at most 32% from any response. The misleading context that models are most susceptible to is disclosed least, and was caught reliably only from an open reasoning trace, which frontier providers withhold.

replace-cross Learning Informative Prior with Infinite-Dimensional Continuous Normalizing Flow for Bayesian Inverse Problem

Authors: Yang Zhao, Junxiong Jia, Tao Zhou

Abstract: Infinite-dimensional Bayesian inference methods, as a core class of methodologies for solving inverse problems of partial differential equations, have been widely applied across numerous scientific fields. A critical obstacle to constructing effective Bayesian inversion methods is how to construct an informative prior measure, particularly in the infinite-dimensional setting. Based on an infinite-dimensionally well-defined neural ordinary differential equation, we establish a general learning framework for infinite-dimensional nonlinear transformations, which can be incorporated into two practical Bayesian prior-learning paradigms corresponding to the direct and indirect data scenarios. A key ingredient of this framework is an equivalence between non-Gaussian and Gaussian measures, which is highly nontrivial: nonlinear transformations with genuinely infinite-dimensional range are involved, and several new limiting mechanisms have to be clarified. The developed framework is applied to three representative inverse problems -- simple source inversion, inverse obstacle scattering, and inverse heat conduction. All three examples substantiate the effectiveness of the proposed methods and, at the same time, demonstrate their broad applicability.

replace-cross Do Latent World Models Really Select the Right Actions?

Authors: Zhengshu Zhang, Zhiyuan Li

Abstract: This paper shows that the planning success of a latent world model does not reveal how well its score selects actions. Latent world models score candidate action sequences by the distance between their predicted embeddings and the embedding of a goal image, and their strong planning results are naturally read as evidence that this score picks good actions. Our study is simple: we take two released models, JEPA-WM and DINO-WM, execute the action sequences their planners consider, and compare what the score prefers with what actually happens. We make two observations. First, the score is informative but unreliable: it picks better actions than chance, yet it often passes over a successful sequence that the search has already found. Second, planning still succeeds, helped by a wider search and by replanning from new observations. With the released search budget, PointMaze planning succeeds in 91% of episodes, yet the top-scored candidate at the end of a search is no more likely to succeed than a random one. These observations suggest that the score should be measured directly, next to the success rate.

replace-cross Support Topology and Gradient Mixing in Sinkhorn Layers

Authors: Dylan Forde

Abstract: Sinkhorn attention layers normalize weights over a fixed sparse mask by alternating row and column scaling. When training these layers, sparse masks are usually judged by their edge count, graph diameter, or forward reachability. We show that the sparsity pattern and marginal budgets also govern how gradients flow backward through the scaling steps. Differentiating one row-column cycle on column log-potentials yields an explicit row-stochastic Markov kernel built from the two half-step transport plans, which reduces to a reversible two-step random walk at a balanced fixed point. Because potentials are defined only up to additive constants on each connected component, reverse-mode gradients propagate as zero-sum vectors whose total-variation norm contracts at a rate given by the kernel's Dobrushin coefficient. This coefficient bounds both unrolled backpropagation and implicit-differentiation truncation error. Crucially, graph connectivity alone does not prevent gradient trapping: we prove a face-lattice dichotomy showing that a mask and marginals guarantee score-uniform one-step contraction if and only if every nonempty face of the transportation polytope retains pairwise two-hop column overlap; otherwise, large learned scores can push the contraction rate arbitrarily close to one. We extend this criterion to multi-layer schedules, prove exact one-sweep mixing for coordinate-factorized and butterfly masks, and derive closed-form bounds for expanders, local bands, and global hub tokens. Deterministic numerical checks verify our finite-difference adjoints, canonical sparse masks, adversarial score limits, and spectral formulas.

replace-cross Beyond Point Forecasts: A Survey on Probabilistic Forecasting for Time Series and Spatiotemporal Data

Authors: Donia Besher, Rajdeep Pathak, Madhurima Panja, Tanujit Chakraborty

Abstract: Probabilistic forecasting is central to decision-making under uncertainty, yet its methodological landscape has become increasingly fragmented across temporal and spatiotemporal forecasting, statistical modeling, machine learning, and deep generative modeling. This survey develops a unified perspective by organizing probabilistic forecasting methods according to where and how uncertainty is introduced into the forecasting pipeline. Our taxonomy connects model-agnostic approaches including ensembles and distribution-free calibration, with model-intrinsic approaches spanning Bayesian modeling, parametric predictive distributions, distributional regression, and modern generative models, and further examines the emerging role of time series foundation models. Beyond methodological synthesis, we identify the assumptions, computational demands, and forms of uncertainty represented by different paradigms, and translate these distinctions into data-driven and domain-specific guidance for method selection. We complement the survey with a cross-paradigm empirical study on univariate, multivariate, and spatiotemporal forecasting tasks. The results reveal that no single uncertainty-quantification paradigm dominates across settings. Calibration, sharpness, predictive accuracy, and computational efficiency can lead to substantially different model preferences, while expressive generative models and zero-shot foundation models exhibit markedly different accuracy-efficiency trade-offs. Lastly, we identify unresolved challenges surrounding uncertainty in evolving dependency structures, physics-informed predictive distributions, forecasting extreme events, handling count-valued, directional, and continuous-time series, and the development of unified software resources. Our survey provides both a conceptual framework and a practical roadmap for probabilistic forecasting research.

replace-cross RheoSampling: Resolving the One-Hot Dilemma in Stochastic Dynamic-Tree Speculative Decoding

Authors: Qiao Hu, Yepeng Weng, Bo Zhang, Takehisa Yairi

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference by drafting multiple tokens in parallel, with tree-based methods further improving efficiency through hierarchical structures. Dynamic-tree methods such as EAGLE-3 perform well under greedy decoding via deterministic top-K expansion and global pruning. However, in stochastic decoding (T>0), this mechanism collapses the draft distribution into one-hot probabilities, causing a severe drop in acceptance rate. This creates a dilemma: dynamic-tree methods sacrifice stochastic sampling to preserve context-aware topology, while static-tree methods preserve stochastic sampling with context-agnostic structures. The issue arises because the same probability distribution is used for two conflicting tasks: constructing the tree and verifying tokens. This coupling makes direct injection of randomness challenging due to the resulting stochastic process. We resolve this by decoupling these roles: RheoSampling assigns a token sampled from the draft distribution a proxy probability for tree expansion and pruning alongside its true sampling probability for verification. Specifically, we inject a sampled token among the deterministic top-K slots and treat it with different probabilities during construction and verification, making RheoSampling the first dynamic-tree method with both context-aware top-K construction and stochastic sampling while maintaining losslessness. We establish the lossless guarantee through an equivalence-class analysis that compresses the stochastic tree space into tractable classes. An OT-based verification strategy and a sparse draft mechanism ensure that theoretical gains translate into practical efficiency. Experiments across LLMs and benchmarks demonstrate improvements in acceptance rate and speedup over state-of-the-art dynamic tree methods. This framework may provide a template for analyzing stochastic tree structures.

replace-cross Using the Whole Slice: AlphaFold2 Inference on an Eight-Chip TPU v5e

Authors: Lorenzo Pazienza, Ihab El Bani

Abstract: AlphaFold2 is written in JAX. JAX treats each chip of a tensor processing unit (TPU) slice as a separate device. A JAX program does not spread its work over the chips by itself, so a user who runs AlphaFold2 on a slice can pay for chips that do nothing. We found no published measurement of how AlphaFold2 inference uses a multi-chip TPU slice. Our goal was to measure how much of an eight-chip TPU v5e slice AlphaFold2 inference uses, and which way of mapping the work in JAX uses all of it. Across 12 experiments, the model used random weights, so we studied execution, not prediction quality. By default, only one chip held data. Batching up to eight input sequences (queries) of 118 residues on one chip with JAX's vmap did not raise throughput. Mapping independent queries to the chips with JAX's pmap ran them in parallel on all eight chips. On a grid of 1 to 8 chips and 100 to 1000 residues, eight chips gave 6.5 to 7.9 times the throughput of one chip. JAX's automatic partitioning did not shorten the call for one query. Our program did not tell the partitioner how to split any array across the chips.

replace-cross MiDShip: Multimodal Dataset of Ship Cargo Hold Structures for Engineering Design

Authors: Noah J. Bagazinski, Md. Ferdous Alam, Jaya Manideep Rebbagondla, Faez Ahmed

Abstract: Ship structures govern a vessel's strength, safety, and manufacturability. Their design is governed by classification-society rules comprising hundreds of requirements, making the design process complex and highly iterative. However, data-driven structural design is limited by the lack of large, structured datasets that connect design geometry with structural geometric measures and rule-based constraints. This paper presents MiDShip, a multimodal dataset of 12,753 synthetically generated cargo-hold structural designs for machine learning and optimization. It contains 6,020 randomly generated designs, 496 designs produced by a stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD)-inspired procedure, and 6,237 designs produced by an equation-informed repair procedure. Each retained design includes a parametric vector, full 3D geometries and simplified geometries formatted for finite-element analysis (FEA) meshing, engineering drawings and corresponding annotated versions, a bill of materials, and structural geometric measures. The dataset also includes twenty-five constraints formulated from classification society rules for stiffener spacing, thickness, and section modulus requirements. Full-vessel loading, buckling, fatigue, and ice-class checks are outside the present scope. The primary contribution is the dataset and codebase, two design-generation studies demonstrate their use rather than introduce new optimization algorithms. MiDShip supports future research in machine learning, generative design, and automated rule-based evaluation for ship structures.

replace-cross InvestigationWorlds: An Agentic Environment for Legal Investigation

Authors: Albert Yu Sun, Andrew Benard, Sil Hamilton, Anna Teresita A. Marcelo, Yong Jae Kim, Carl-Leander Henneking, Rundong Hu, Yuhong Wang, David Mimno, Bishan Yang, Igor Labutov

Abstract: We introduce InvestigationWorlds, an agentic environment for legal investigation. We build on an underused artifact of U.S. civil litigation: the summary judgment motion. This motion relies upon a record composed of real evidence exhibits, and results in a court-adopted hypothesis that is treated as ground truth for the purposes of deciding the motion. Each environment is built from a real U.S. Federal Court case retrieved from Public Access to Court Electronic Records (PACER) and augmented by an attorney-validated generation pipeline that synthesizes role-tagged documents around the original record. The resulting corpus admits multiple coherent factual readings, only one of which matches the court-adopted hypothesis. Evaluating on 100 cases, we find agents often commit to incorrect hypotheses despite retrieving relevant evidence, struggling to distinguish the court-adopted hypothesis from alternative hypotheses.

replace-cross CADFather: Autonomous CAD Reconstruction through Coordinated Tool Use

Authors: Gennadiy Savrasov, Maksim Elistratov, Nikita Gavrilov, Albert Garifullin, Oleg Pavlov, Soslan Kabisov, Vladimir Frolov, Anton Konushin, Andrey Kuznetsov, Dmitrii Zhemchuzhnikov

Abstract: Reconstructing an editable CAD model from a 3D shape remains a challenging engineering task. Existing methods can propose CAD operations, but no single source of proposals works equally well across different part geometries and stages of reconstruction. We introduce CADFather, an autonomous agentic system that coordinates complementary tools to recover parametric CAD programs from 3D meshes. A vision-language assistant inspects renders of the target and intermediate reconstructions, then decides which candidate CAD programs to extend, which tools to invoke, how many proposals to generate, and when to finish. Learned and algorithmic tools propose CAD operations, while numerical optimization refines the parameters of existing programs. Proposed or refined programs are executed and evaluated to provide feedback for subsequent decisions. The agent maintains alternative candidate programs for each target part and preserves the best valid result throughout reconstruction. CADFather uses pretrained generation and assistant models without additional training. We evaluate reconstruction quality and execution validity on the full DeepCAD, Fusion360, and MCB test sets, as well as on CADENA-Bench, CADBench, and BenchCAD. We additionally analyze computational cost and the trade-off between cost and reconstruction quality.

replace-cross Learning joint predictive distributions from observations with coordinate-aware covariance bounds

Authors: Chaeyeon Yi, Yun Am Seo

Abstract: Quantifying uncertainty in derived quantities requires forecasts that capture cross-variable dependence. From station observations alone, CLARA predicts the mean and full covariance of a Gaussian joint distribution in one forward pass per lead, trains on a CPU and recalibrates the covariance without altering correlations. On two nationwide Korean weather networks, ten regions on six continents and a nationwide air-quality network, CLARA outperforms learned comparators in lead-mean energy score, by 5.2-5.5% against comparably sized iTransformers with the same temporal-phase inputs on the Korean networks, with gains that persist in two held-out seasons. We prove that the same lower bound on the Cholesky diagonal admits different covariances in different output coordinates. Changing only these coordinates lowers the pressure-difference continuous ranked probability score by 40-67%, lets the learned correlations outperform a fixed correlation matrix in joint likelihood at every lead and, on an electricity grid, improves the joint likelihood with two other backbones.

replace-cross Find the Fungible: Variable-Resolution Tokenization that Compacts Semantics with a Granularity Dial as a VLM "Catalyst"

Authors: Tianhao Fu, Austin Wang, Charles Chen, Roby Aldave-Garza, Victor Xie

Abstract: Efficient vision-language models should preserve a useful representation of the whole image, even under tight budgets. However, aggressive pruning or indiscriminate merging can erase critical details and disrupt the spatial structure. We thus propose Varest, which maximizes semantic fungibility: tokens describing coherent content can be summarized together with minimal information loss. Object boundaries distilled from a visual foundation model guide grouping, while text localization protects details needed for reading. At lower budgets, Varest merges the image into coarser semantic regions, while maintaining full spatial coverage. After early language decoder layers process this representation, a learned selector prunes visual tokens that later layers no longer need, reducing repeated access to already integrated information. Across three LLaVA backbones and 13 benchmarks, Varest outperforms 10 training-free and 5 trained baselines in mean performance retention at every evaluated budget, preserving 86.10-92.33% of uncompressed performance using only 5.56% of tokens. This SOTA performance further generalizes to modern VLMs like Qwen3.8-27B.

replace-cross The Missing Compliance Class: LLM Graders That Collapse Jailbreak Success, and a Free Check

Authors: Ruqiya Bin Safi

Abstract: Jailbreak evaluations often substitute open-weight 7B to 9B instruction-tuned models as local, deterministic judges for frontier APIs. Holding target models, prompts, responses, and a four-class rubric fixed across eight graders from five organizations, we find that dense 8B and 9B judges collapse compliant responses onto evasion. One assigns compliance zero times in 900 responses while agreeing with our reference judge on 78.0% of items, certifying an unsafe model as safe. This collapse holds across four 900-response corpora, multiplying reported compliance by a factor of 22 to 25 on our primary corpora while non-refusal shifts by 18.0 to 23.0 points. Without the three collapsing graders, compliance varies by 1.7x to 2.8x across all four corpora, whereas post-labelling rubric rulings (+16.3 points) and benchmark choice (15.3 points) still shift non-refusal. Per-class label counts expose class collapse at zero cost without human labels, motivating an eight-item reporting minimum that pairs compliance with non-refusal rates.

replace-cross Confidence-Aware Speed-over-Ground Estimation from Doppler Spectra via Reconstruction-Based Representation Learning

Authors: Malte Stelzer, Marvin Sach, Timo Bartels, Dinu Mihailescu-Stoica, S\"oren Kohnert, Florian Poprawa, Marc Zeller, Tim Fingscheidt

Abstract: Reliable speed-over-ground estimation from ground-facing Doppler spectra is essential for mobile platforms such as railway systems. Classical signal-processing approaches can provide accurate estimates under favorable conditions, but often degrade in challenging conditions. We propose confidence-aware speed-over-ground estimation trained on rail-domain data that estimates speed over ground and frame-level confidence from Doppler spectra with several novel contributions: First, we transfer the idea of exploiting a reconstruction task by an autoencoder for performance estimation from previous work to Doppler RaDAR. Second, we append a speed-over-ground estimation head operating on the autoencoder's latent space features. Third, we omit the reconstruction decoder during inference but add a low-cost dedicated confidence head for frame-wise error-bound estimation. Fourth, we reveal how noise augmentation training can provide high robustness to the method. Experiments show that our approach improves speed estimation accuracy over classical baselines while providing informative confidence estimates that indicate the reliability of individual predictions, even under adverse signal conditions.

replace-cross Write Facts the Way Models Read Them: Lifting the Two-Hop Curse

Authors: Zhiyun Shi

Abstract: A language model that learns a new fact by fine-tuning can repeat it but cannot reason with it: taught that a person is a citizen of a new country, it still cannot say, without chain-of-thought, who the head of state of that person's country is. This two-hop curse is widely treated as a reasoning deficit, and the strongest knowledge editors answer it with synthesized reasoning data. We show that the reasoning is intact and the writing is wrong. Fine-tuning writes a new fact as an answer to be said, whereas the second hop reads the bridge ("that person's country") as an entity, from the middle layers on: patching the model's own representation of the entity into the bridge at those layers largely restores the second hop, yet after fine-tuning the bridge there is no closer to the new entity than before the edit. We therefore propose read-equivalent writing (Legible): one loss term makes the bridge phrase, at the layer where the second hop finishes reading it, match the model's own reading of the new entity, and keeps the same relation's other subjects unchanged. Phrases and targets come from the edited model itself, and no multi-hop training data is used. Across Qwen2.5, Llama-3 and Qwen3, Legible (I) composes learned facts more often than the model composes its own knowledge, on MQuAKE's two-hop-curse questions; (II) reaches 1.8-2.6x the bridge accuracy of CaKE, whose reasoning data composes no better than plain fine-tuning, and has the best average over composition, accuracy and locality against four families of writers; and (III) lifts the curse on RippleEdits, on the benchmark of Balesni et al., who named the curse, and at 14B.

replace-cross How many sorted projections determine any n points in k dimensions?

Authors: Christopher Gorham Lester, Raymond Hau S{\o}rensen

Abstract: Let $d_1,\dots,d_m$ be directions in $\mathbb{R}^k$, where $k\ge 2$. A multiset $X$ of $n$ points of $\mathbb{R}^k$ projects to the $m$ multisets of numbers $\{\{x\cdot d_j : x\in X\}\}$, or equivalently to the $m$ sorted lists of these numbers. How many directions are needed for these projections to determine every multiset of $n$ points? Two simple bounds are known in every dimension: more than $(k-1)\log_2 n$ directions are always needed, and $(k-1)n+1$ directions in general position always suffice. In the plane much more is known: Matousek, Privetivy and Skovron showed that almost every choice of $O(\log n \log\log n)$ directions suffices. Whether anything similar holds in higher dimensions was left open by Dym, Wellershoff, Tsoukanis, Levy and Balan. We observe that two lemmas of Hair and Sahai, proved for list recovery of random linear codes, answer that question, and by tightening their argument we show that in every dimension almost every choice of $(4k-2)\ln(2n)\ln(k\log_2 2n)+k+1$ directions suffices. For $k\le 10$ this beats the linear bound once $n$ is larger than about 100: for example, 144 directions suffice for multisets of 1000 points in the plane, and an exact computation lowers this to 133. Letting some of the random blocks in their argument fail removes a factor $\log k$: almost every choice of $O(k\log n\log\log n)$ directions suffices, uniformly in $k$ and $n$. We also show that $2k-1$ directions never determine three points, so that the logarithmic lower bound is not tight; that every $m$ directions in the plane fail on some pair of multisets of size $O(1.65^m)$, improving the bound $O(1.80^m)$ of Matousek, Privetivy and Skovron; and that for each $k$, $m$ and $n$, either almost every choice of $m$ directions works or almost none does.

replace-cross On Agentic Manipulation Policies

Authors: Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Chen Chen, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, Xueting Li, Jean-Baptiste Regli, Kejia Ren, Petr \v{S}vec, David J. Yoon, Matthias M\"uller, Vladlen Koltun

Abstract: Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2% and 42.9% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation. See https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/ for code and videos.

URLs: https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/

replace-cross Off-Policy RL with Online Inference Tuning: 1st Place Solution to the CoRL 2026 RoboEval Challenge

Authors: Ilia Larchenko

Abstract: The CoRL 2026 RoboEval challenge has eight bimanual tasks, and it ranks a policy not only on whether it succeeds but on how: every stage of the task, done safely, efficiently and with both arms in coordination, scored against human demonstrations. My solution has two parts. The generic part, carried over from my LeHome challenge solution, is off-policy RL on a $\pi_{0.5}$-based policy that is also its own value function. It is trained with AWR and RECAP on advantages of the exact official score, in a loop where every rollout is at the same time a Thompson-sampling trial of about 20 inference parameters. Out of the box, it took the policy to 0.85 RDS on held-out seeds in two days (and it was still very far from convergence). The competition-specific part adds data that local exploration cannot find: generated demonstrations that reach the full reward, and recovery episodes that start from the policy's own failures. The submitted policy reaches 0.990 RDS on my held-out seeds and 0.987 on the official seeds, and it won first place in the challenge, with 0.988 in the organisers' final evaluation on 1,000 unpublished seeds per task.

replace-cross When Should Decision Models Compare Candidates?

Authors: Fangzhi Li, Quan Shi, Wenting Yang, Yueqiao Chen, Jiayu Chang, Shuting Zhao, Zhaowei Xu, Yizhao Chen, Guanchu Wu, Han Xu

Abstract: Language-model agents frequently select among candidate actions, such as tools and web elements. Should decision models score candidates independently or allow their scores to depend on other candidates? We investigate this question by comparing three approaches: Pointwise scoring, Setwise scoring, and Candidate Attention. Across five agent-decision benchmarks, Pointwise scoring remains competitive, while Setwise scoring performs better on some tool-selection and binary-decision tasks. Candidate Attention does not consistently improve upon Pointwise scoring, despite allowing cross-candidate interaction. Using two controlled synthetic tasks, we further examine whether models learn to use candidate-set information. With targeted relational training, Setwise models learn candidate dependencies and generalize to shifted inputs, while Candidate Attention shows weaker transfer. Our results show that access to other candidates does not guarantee effective comparison, and that its benefits depend on both task requirements and training.

replace-cross LoopZip: Visual Token Compression for Efficient Looped Vision-Language Models

Authors: Weizhong Huang, Zhicheng Liu, Yan Zhang, Fei Chao, Xiawu Zheng, Liujuan Cao

Abstract: Looped vision-language models (VLMs) deepen computation by reusing shared layers, approaching larger models' capabilities with fewer parameters and easing the storage and deployment burden of stacking independent layers. However, repeatedly processing long visual sequences still incurs substantial compute and GPU memory costs. Visual token compression reduces these costs by shortening sequences. Existing methods mainly select original tokens directly or combine selection with aggregation. In looped models, the former may overlook changing token importance across calls and discard information needed later; the latter lack joint consideration of output changes across calls, limiting information preservation. We propose LoopZip, a training-free visual token compression algorithm addressing both issues. Visual aggregation builds a compact candidate pool before the looped backbone, and cross-invocation selection averages instruction attention from the first two existing calls of the same shared attention layer to retain tokens needed by both states. Weight-tied value correction then jointly fits shared visual-value adjustments to both pre-selection layer outputs, reducing output deviations across states. To validate LoopZip's effectiveness, we conduct extensive experiments covering two open-source looped VLMs, eleven benchmarks, and three pruning ratios. On LoopVL-1B, LoopZip retains 97.16% of the original model's performance at 50% pruning ratio and exceeds the strongest baseline by 5.36% at 87.5% pruning ratio. Across Hugging Face Transformers, vLLM, and SGLang, LoopVL-1B with LoopZip reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 58.36%-58.98% and achieves 2.01-2.47x end-to-end speedups over the original model.

replace-cross Multimodal Masked Diffusion Autoencoder for Modeling Impaired Driving

Authors: John Gideon, Anshul Gupta, Megan Applegate-Kenton, Kayli Battel, Todd J Rowell, Patricio Reyes Gomez, Laporsha Dees, Emily Sumner, Avinash Balachandran, Guy Rosman

Abstract: Human cognitive state (e.g., distraction, stress), gaze, and behavior interact with the driving environment in subtle ways that AI could use to assist drivers, but ground-truth labels for human cognitive state levels are costly to obtain at scale. We propose a multimodal masked-diffusion autoencoder, ID-MAE, that adds a self-supervised objective to impairment prediction: alongside predicting the impairment label as a masked token, it reconstructs masked gaze and trajectory. To probe the effects of different modalities and types of impairments on model inference, we introduce DIVERT, a driving-simulator dataset containing 103 hours of driving data (n = 109) with ten impairment inductions spanning cognitive, visual, and emotional states, recorded with gaze, vehicle state, and scene video. We demonstrate how ID-MAE affords significant improvements in impairment inference results on both the DIVERT dataset and existing datasets, and enables strong results in additional tasks, such as trajectory and gaze prediction. For location-matched pairs, our model achieves a paired AUC of 0.706 for impairment detection on DIVERT, compared to 0.590 without reconstruction loss, improving over existing baselines. We probe the design space in terms of input modalities, masking scheme, positional encoding, attention mechanism, and decoding. We also investigate the interaction between various impairments in training and inference, demonstrating that visually demanding distractions are separable from cognitive distractions. We plan to publicly release the code and data.

replace-cross MADJEPA: Enhanced Multi-Stage Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture for Medical Anomaly Detection

Authors: Ioana-Sabina Stoian, Florinel-Alin Croitoru, Vlad Hondru, Andreea Iuliana Ionescu, Andrei Paun, Radu Tudor Ionescu

Abstract: Anomaly detection in medical images is a complex task, requiring the development of a robust model using only healthy patient data. Without access to labeled data, modern approaches typically employ self-supervised learning to obtain a model of normality. Since world models promise to build strong internal representations of their environment, we consider them as suitable candidates for the medical anomaly detection environment, namely medical investigations of healthy patients. To this end, we propose MADJEPA, a medical anomaly detector based on an enhanced multi-stage joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA). We design a multi-stage training pipeline to produce a robust detector: (1) image-based JEPA (I-JEPA) training aims to learn a strong representation of normal training data; (2) target encoder distillation aims to modulate over-generalization of I-JEPA representations to abnormal data; (3) pseudo-anomaly classification aims to calibrate anomaly predictions by learning a tight boundary around the normal data manifold. To demonstrate our design, we carry out comprehensive experiments on six datasets covering various medical image domains (e.g. MRI, OCT, X-ray, histopathology), comparing our MADJEPA with numerous state-of-the-art methods. The results show that our carefully designed world model, MADJEPA, attains competitive results, ranking first on three datasets. Our ablation studies further confirm the utility of each training stage, indicating that I-JEPA alone is not sufficient to obtain competitive results in medical anomaly detection. We publicly release our code at https://github.com/Sabina085/madjepa.

replace-cross Loop-OPF: Solver-Informed Learning for Optimal Power Flow with Inference-Time Topology Adaptivity

Authors: Mohamad Chehade, Hao Zhu

Abstract: Learning for optimal power flow (OPF) has recently been advocated to accelerate OPF solutions by using a pre-trained neural network (NN) to predict directly from given inputs such as the load demand. However, existing learning-for-OPF methods largely fall short of ensuring the optimality and feasibility of their predictions. They also struggle to adapt at inference time to unseen topology scenarios, such as those caused by line or generator outages. This work puts forth Loop-OPF, a solver-informed NN that builds its layers from the iterations of the primal-dual hybrid gradient (PDHG) method. Under standard convexity and regularity conditions on DC-OPF, the resultant fixed-point output of Loop-OPF is thus optimal and feasible. To speed up PDHG across diverse OPF scenarios, Loop-OPF learns certain PDHG parameters in a self-supervised manner, without any OPF solution labels. We adopt the deep equilibrium (DEQ) framework for training so that the memory cost does not grow with the number of layers. As the grid topology enters every layer as an input, the same trained Loop-OPF directly adapts to unseen contingencies without retraining. Numerical tests on the IEEE 300-bus and PEGASE 1354-, 2869-, and 9241-bus networks have demonstrated the accuracy and topology adaptivity of the proposed Loop-OPF over competing learning-for-OPF methods.

replace-cross Self-Supervised Continuous-Time Depth Learning and Ego-Motion Estimation from Asynchronous Events

Authors: Sadiq O. Macaulay, Dipon Kumar Ghosh, Hadfield Simon

Abstract: Event cameras register brightness changes pixel by pixel with microsecond precision, whereas the dense depth that could supervise them is costly to acquire and exists only at isolated instants. Condensing the stream into frames eases learning, but at the expense of the very timing that sets event sensing apart. We propose a self-supervised approach in which each event is assigned its own depth in continuous time, and the camera motion is recovered from the same observations. Supervision arises from geometry: lifted into 3D by its predicted depth and displaced by the camera motion at the instant it fired, an event should coincide with others produced by the same edge, so any misalignment exposes an error in depth or motion. Differentiable rendering then turns these per-event estimates into dense depth maps at target times within each window, faithful to the events behind them. On MVSEC, the approach outperforms prior event-only self-supervised methods in most sequences and depth ranges, with the widest margins at night, and recovers camera rotation more accurately than an earlier unsupervised event-based method. Ablations isolate the roles of local spatiotemporal context, the temporal backbone, and the alignment objective. These results position motion-compensated event alignment as a principled source of supervision for continuous-time depth and ego-motion estimation.

replace-cross Learning in the Passive Space: End-to-End Muti-Order Port-Hamiltonian Macromodeling

Authors: Benchao Zhu, Keren Zhu

Abstract: Compact, passive state-space macromodels of interconnects and power-delivery networks are essential for stable transient signal- and power-integrity simulations. However, conventional flows require costly per-layout full-wave electromagnetic (EM) simulations, while both traditional fitting and prior learning surrogates rely on post-hoc fitting or passivity perturbation that degrades accuracy without guaranteeing convergence. In this work, we propose an end-to-end macromodeling framework that learns directly in the passive space, mapping physical layout graphs to multi-order port-Hamiltonian (pH) macromodels. Specifically, our network encodes each layout graph into unconstrained matrix blocks and assembles them via a complete, prefix-nested pH parameterization that guarantees continuous-frequency passivity and stability by construction, yielding valid $k$-state macromodels via direct state slicing in one forward pass. To train all orders without reference realizations, we supervise the network directly on S-parameters through a progressive order-growth curriculum. Evaluated on 27k unseen test structures from a 270k-structure advanced-packaging dataset, our framework reduces mean S-parameter error by $42\%$ on average (up to $65\%$) over established macromodeling tools fitted to ground-truth EM responses, achieves zero passivity violations across $459{,}000$ exported models, and accelerates macromodel generation by $11{,}000$--$39{,}000\times$ ($36$--$85$~ms per layout).

replace-cross Sparse Hodge Preference Optimization: Fine-Tuning Language Models toward Alignment beyond Transitive Preferences

Authors: Yiming Liu, Jianwei Ma, Zhongwei Xing, Tonghui Yu

Abstract: The growing adoption of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), which leverages pairwise preference data, reflects its great success in fine-tuning large language models to align with human expectations. It directly optimizes the policy from human preferences without explicit reward modeling or the complexities of reinforcement learning-based approaches. However, the classic DPO relies on the Bradley--Terry sampling model and cannot effectively capture intransitivity or cyclic structures in aggregated human preference data. In this article, we propose Sparse Hodge Preference Optimization (SHPO), a preference optimization algorithm that augments DPO with a sparse antisymmetric curl residual motivated by the combinatorial Hodge decomposition. We prove that SHPO strictly enlarges DPO's hypothesis class on comparison graphs containing at least one triangle, under some mild regularity conditions on the response representations. We further establish a theoretical guarantee for $\ell_1$-regularized estimation of the curl coefficients under fixed policy and representations. Qualitative studies on three preference datasets under the same QLoRA budget, SHPO-aligned LLM achieves satisfactory performances in preference accuracy, cyclic structure recovery, and GPT-4o-judged generation quality.

replace-cross LimeTree: Linear-Memory Graph Neural Networks over Tree-Graphs for Resource-Efficient Language Modeling and Context Extrapolation

Authors: Elisabeth Fink

Abstract: The search for computationally efficient alternatives to the Transformer has yielded numerous architectures in recent years; however, most exhibit severe weaknesses in associative recall-such as key-value (KV) retrieval-and length extrapolation, where even standard Transformers struggle. This paper introduces LimeTree, a linear-complexity architecture in both memory and compute for sequence modeling. By integrating synthetic KV retrieval tasks directly into standard language modeling training, LimeTree achieves strong perplexity alongside reliable recall. Two variants were evaluated on WikiText-103 and PG-19 under strict compute constraints. On WikiText-103, LimeTree reaches a token-level perplexity of 19 in just 6 hours on four Quadro RTX 8000 GPUs, while maintaining 100\% KV retrieval accuracy even when extrapolating from a training context length of 512 to an evaluation length of 16,384. On PG-19, the model achieves a token-level perplexity of 10.84 in 35.5 hours on a single RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell GPU with minimal hyperparameter tuning. These results position LimeTree as a competitive linear-complexity alternative to Transformers, combining superior perplexity and training throughput with robust in-context retrieval and length extrapolation.

replace-cross Lorentz symmetry in self-supervised representation learning

Authors: Andreas Hermansen, Tobias Golling, Slava Voloshynovskiy

Abstract: Self-supervised learning (SSL) and explicit physical inductive biases have driven major advances in jet tagging, yet they are typically pursued along separate tracks. In this work, we unify both paradigms by integrating the Lorentz-equivariant Geometric Algebra Transformer slim (L-GATr-slim) architecture with the LeJEPA framework for representation learning. Pre-training on 100 million jets from the JetClass dataset, we optimize towards a latent space, which is invariant across physics-motivated jet augmentations. We demonstrate that enforcing Lorentz symmetry in the latent space is critical for representation quality and that this can be achieved either architecturally or learned via kinematic augmentations. With a standard non-invariant Transformer backbone, LeJEPA outperforms established autoregressive, masked particle modelling, and self-distillation baselines in linear- and MLP-probe accuracy, with L-GATr-slim providing further complementary gains. replace-cross Unlocking the Power of Loops in Pretrained LLMs with Loop Residuals

Authors: Xuanlei Zhao, Hailun Xu, Kai Wang, Yang Luo, Yingxuan Ren, Zirui Zhu, Yuxuan Lou, Kun Xu, Yan Wang, Kanchan Sarkar, Yang You

Abstract: Looped Transformers iterate a shared block of layers to increase computation without adding parameters, offering stronger performance and test-time scaling. However, this power has so far required training from scratch and yielded only modest gains for LLMs. Finetuning existing models into loops is far cheaper, yet also brings only marginal gains over SFT. We propose LoopRes, an effective, scalable, and simple approach that turns loops into significant and consistent gains for pretrained LLMs. Building on Attention Residuals, LoopRes lets each looped layer attend directly to the outputs of earlier loops. Since these outputs are iterates of the same layers and become harder to tell apart, we give each a trainable loop-aware key that encodes its structural identity. Attending to all earlier loops also lets layers skip intermediate ones, so we restrict each layer to a residual window of the latest W loops, forcing every loop to build incrementally on recent ones. Across 6 pretrained LLMs from 3 model families and 9 benchmarks, LoopRes delivers significant and consistent gains: at 2x compute, it improves Qwen3-1.7B-Base by 29.1% over SFT, more than doubling the gain of the state-of-the-art baseline. The gains hold on larger models up to 30B and across all three families. More importantly, from 1.7B to 8B, LoopRes scales more steeply than SFT as compute grows, reaching a higher ceiling while needing less compute on long outputs. These results establish LoopRes as an effective looping approach, delivering both better performance and better test-time scaling.
